
================================================================================
BOOK: Psalms
================================================================================

Chapter 1
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 1: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 1 as a foundational instruction on the eternal division between the righteous and wicked. He emphasizes that this distinction "is ancient, ever since the struggle began between sin and grace, the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent, so it is lasting, and will survive all other divisions." The psalm serves as a preface to all devotions, since "it is absolutely necessary to the acceptance of our devotions that we be righteous before God."

The commentary reveals a progression of evil in three stages: ungodly (casting off fear of God), sinners (open rebellion), and scornful (mocking what is sacred). Henry notes this downward trajectory: "Nemo repente fit turpissimus--None reach the height of vice at once."

## Key Practical Applications

**Separation from Evil Influences:** Henry emphasizes practical avoidance of ungodly counsel, warning against even associating with the wicked. The godly person "will not be picked up by them" and must "keep as far from them as from a place or person infected with the plague, for fear of the contagion."

**Meditation on God's Word:** The commentary stresses constant engagement with Scripture: "we must have it in our thoughts, accordingly, upon every occasion that occurs, whether night or day." This involves "discourse with ourselves concerning the great things contained in it, with a close application of mind."

**Fruitfulness in Season:** Henry applies the tree metaphor practically, explaining that believers should bring forth fruit "in due season, when it is most beautiful and most useful, improving every opportunity of doing good."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1:** The three-fold separation (walking, standing, sitting) represents increasing levels of involvement with evil. Henry sees this as essential groundwork: "departing from evil is that in which wisdom begins."

**Verse 2:** The "delight" in God's law reflects inner transformation - "All who are well pleased that there is a God must be well pleased that there is a Bible."

**Verse 3:** The tree imagery emphasizes divine planting and positioning "by the means of grace, here called the rivers of water." Professional integrity is preserved: "His leaf also shall not wither."

**Verses 4-6:** The chaff metaphor emphasizes the ungodly's worthlessness and instability - they are "like the chaff which the wind drives away, the very lightest of the chaff."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout, including:
- Psalm 119:115 ("say to evil-doers, Depart from us")
- Romans 7:16, 22 (delighting in God's law "after the inner man")
- Proverbs 4:14-15 and 6:22 (avoiding evil paths)
- Isaiah 61:3 ("trees of the Lord are full of sap")
- Philippians 4:17 (practices as "fruit, abounding to a good account")

The commentary concludes that God "knows those that are his by name, but we must know them by their character," establishing this psalm as a crucial test for spiritual self-examination.

Chapter 2
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 2: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 2 as primarily **evangelical rather than moral**, focusing on "our Saviour" more than earthly duty. He sees this as a **Messianic prophecy** with David's kingdom serving merely as a type, arguing there is "less in it of the type, and more of the anti-type, than in any of the gospel psalms." The psalm presents a **cosmic conflict** between Christ's kingdom and Satan's dominion, with "hell and heaven contesting" over earthly rule.

The central theological theme is **God's sovereignty over human rebellion**. Henry emphasizes that opposition to Christ is fundamentally "against the Lord and against his anointed," making all enemies of Christ inherently "enemies to God himself." The commentary presents God as completely secure in His heavenly throne, viewing earthly opposition with divine derision.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**On Opposition to Christ**: Christians should expect resistance since "never were the notions of any sect of philosophers... opposed with so much violence as the doctrine and government of Christ." This opposition signals heavenly origin, as "the opposition was plainly from hell originally."

**On Divine Security**: Believers can find comfort in God's perfect repose: "The perfect repose of the Eternal Mind may be our comfort under all the disquietments of our mind. We are tossed on earth... but he sits in the heavens."

**On Christ's Authority**: Henry emphasizes that salvation requires submission: "those that will be saved by him must be ruled by him," though Christ's "bands and cords" are "agreeable to right reason" and "bands of love."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry sees universal opposition from both "princes and people, court and country" despite Christ's kingdom not threatening earthly powers. The rebellion stems from unwillingness to accept divine restraints: "Why do men oppose religion but because they are impatient of its restraints and obligations?"

**Verses 4-5**: God's laughter represents divine superiority over human schemes: "Sinners' follies are the just sport of God's infinite wisdom and power." God "sits still and vanquishes them" without disturbing His eternal rest.

**Verse 6**: The declaration "Yet have I set my king upon my holy hill of Zion" demonstrates God's unshakeable purpose. Christ is "God's King" because He is "appointed by him" and "dear to the Father."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to New Testament fulfillment, citing **Acts 4:24, 13:33, and Hebrews 1:5** as direct interpretations of Christ. He references **Acts 4:27** linking "Herod and Pilate, the Gentiles and the Jews" to the psalm's opposition.

Other significant cross-references include **John 15:24** ("they have hated both me and my Father"), **Luke 19:14** on rejecting Christ's reign, and **2 Thessalonians 2:8** regarding God's word destroying enemies. Henry also connects to **Isaiah 37:22** on despising enemies and **Psalm 83:5** on confederate opposition.

The commentary concludes that this psalm is "very fitly prefixed to this book of devotions" because acceptance with God requires both subjection to law's precepts and gospel grace, coming to God "in the name of a Mediator."

Chapter 3
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 3: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 3 as typological, with David's distress under Absalom's rebellion prefiguring "the peace and holy security of the redeemed" under divine protection. The commentary emphasizes that **"those speak best of the truths of God who speak experimentally"** - David's personal experience of God's faithfulness validates his testimony.

Central themes include divine protection amid overwhelming opposition, the constancy of God's care despite human fickleness, and the believer's unshakeable confidence in God's character. Henry notes how David "wept and prayed, wept and sung, wept and believed" - demonstrating that genuine faith encompasses the full range of human emotion while maintaining trust in God.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing adversity:

**Prayer as Primary Response**: "Whither else should we go but to him when any thing grieves us or frightens us?" He emphasizes that troubles should "drive us to God, not drive us from him."

**Maintaining Worship Despite Circumstances**: Even "undutiful disobedient children" shouldn't hinder "joy in God" or put believers "out of tune for holy songs."

**True Christian Courage**: Henry redefines courage as "gracious security and serenity of mind, in patiently bearing and patiently waiting, than in daring enterprises with sword in hand."

**Handling Rejection**: When people attempt to shake faith by suggesting "there is no help for him in God," believers should respond like David: "Get thee behind me, Satan. The Lord rebuke thee!"

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes David's amazement at his enemies' growth - "like a snow-ball, strangely gathered in its motion." The enemies' declaration of "no help for him in God" represents the ultimate spiritual attack, attempting to "drive him to despair."

**Verse 3**: God provides threefold blessing - **Safety** ("shield for me"), **Honour** ("my glory"), and **Joy/Deliverance** ("lifter up of my head"). Henry particularly emphasizes that true honor comes from divine recognition, not earthly status.

**Verses 4-5**: David's peaceful sleep demonstrates supernatural calm: "I laid me down and slept; I awaked" - showing complete trust in divine protection even amid mortal danger.

## Cross-References and Scripture Connections

Henry connects this psalm extensively to other biblical texts:

- Links to **2 Samuel 12:11** regarding the fulfillment of divine judgment for David's sin with Uriah
- References **Genesis 15:1** on God as "shield"
- Cites **Psalm 35:3** on God's salvation
- Connects to **Isaiah 60:19** on God as glory
- References Christ's passion, noting how "many enemies" surrounded the "Son of David" when crowds cried "Crucify him"

The commentary emphasizes that Christ was "set King upon the holy hill of Zion" (Psalm 2:6), and "it is through him...that our prayers are heard."

Henry concludes by noting that this psalm teaches believers to "encourage ourselves in our God, who owns and protects and will in due time crown his own interest both in the world and in the hearts of his people" - providing both individual comfort and corporate hope for the church under persecution.

Chapter 4
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 4: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 4 as a **"royal sermon"** where David functions as both preacher and king. The psalm demonstrates God's **special relationship with the godly**, emphasizing divine election and protection. A central theme is **the contrast between vanity and true righteousness** - sinners "love vanity, and seek after leasing" while the godly find security in God's favor. 

Henry interprets this messianically, seeing David's experience as prefiguring Christ's kingdom, noting that opposition to God's anointed is ultimately futile since "**The Lord hath set apart him that is godly for himself**."

## Key Practical Applications

**Self-Examination and Reflection**: Henry strongly emphasizes verse 4's call to "commune with your own heart upon your bed." He advocates for **nightly conscience examination** - reviewing the day's actions, repenting of sins, and meditating on God during wakeful hours.

**Proper Worship**: The "sacrifices of righteousness" (v.5) represent **authentic spiritual devotion** - good works flowing from love of God and neighbor, offered with an upright heart rather than mere external ritual.

**Trust Over Self-Reliance**: Henry calls believers to "**trust in his providence, and lean not to your own understanding; trust in his grace, and go not about to establish your own righteousness**."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry identifies two key prayer principles - God as "**the God of my righteousness**" (both righteous Himself and the source of our righteousness) and the importance of **past mercies as grounds for future petitions**.

**Verse 2**: The address to "sons of men" emphasizes human dignity - Henry notes we should "**consider the dignity of your nature**" rather than acting irrationally through sin.

**Verse 3**: God's people are "**God's separated, sealed ones**" - divinely chosen and specially protected, making persecution of them particularly dangerous.

**Verse 4**: The phrase "stand in awe and sin not" calls for **holy reverence as sin's antidote** - maintaining "a holy dread of his wrath and curse."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to other biblical texts:
- **Proverbs 8:4-5** (Wisdom crying to men)
- **2 Peter 1:20** (avoiding overly private interpretation)
- **Romans 1:23** (changing God's glory into shame)
- **Ephesians 4:26** (linking "be angry and sin not" to Paul's teaching)
- **Ephesians 5:19 and Colossians 3:16** (New Testament worship principles)
- **Matthew 18:6** (God's protection of believers)

Henry also references **1 Chronicles 25** extensively regarding temple worship organization, demonstrating how this psalm functioned in Israel's liturgical life, while noting that New Testament worship emphasizes **"melody in the heart"** over elaborate musical arrangements.

The commentary concludes by framing the psalm as both **personal devotion and evangelistic appeal** - believers finding security in God's favor while sinners are called to abandon vanity for genuine righteousness.

Chapter 5
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 5: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 5 as a prayer psalm addressing the believer's relationship with a holy God amid enemy opposition. The central theological theme is **God's dual nature as both prayer-hearing and sin-hating**. Henry emphasizes that David serves as "a type of Christ," with his enemies pointing toward "Christ's dependence on his Father and triumphs over the powers of darkness."

The commentary highlights **God's absolute holiness** in contrast to human wickedness. Henry notes that "God has no pleasure in wickedness, though covered with a cloak of religion," establishing that divine holiness is incompatible with evil. This creates both comfort for the righteous and terror for the wicked.

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer Discipline**: Henry emphasizes the importance of morning prayer, stating believers should pray when "most fresh, and lively, and composed" before daily business begins. He advocates for intentional prayer, comparing it to a marksman directing "his arrow to the white."

**Worship Commitment**: David's resolution to worship publicly in God's house demonstrates that "duties of the closet are designed to prepare us for, not to excuse us from, public ordinances." Secret prayer should complement, not replace, corporate worship.

**Patient Waiting**: Henry stresses believers must "look up" after prayer, waiting expectantly for God's response like "he that has shot an arrow looks to see how near it has come to the mark."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry notes David's progression from "words" to "meditation" to "cry," showing increasing spiritual intensity. The phrase "my King and my God" represents personal covenant relationship - "the most commanding encouraging principle of prayer."

**Verses 4-6**: Henry identifies two particular classes of sinners God abhors: "those that speak leasing" (liars) and "bloody" men (murderers), noting these "resemble the devil and are his children."

**Verse 7**: The phrase "multitude of thy mercy" represents "inexhaustible treasures of mercy" that believers must rely upon rather than personal merit.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to multiple biblical passages: Psalm 19:14 (linking meditation and prayer), Psalm 66:18 (regarding iniquity hindering prayer), Psalm 85:8 and Habakkuk 2:1 (waiting for God's response), Acts 3:4 (steadfast looking), Hebrews 12:28 (worshiping with reverence), and Revelation 21:8 (God's judgment on liars).

The commentary demonstrates how this individual psalm connects to broader biblical themes of prayer, holiness, worship, and divine justice throughout Scripture.

Chapter 6
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 6: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 6 as David's lament during severe physical and spiritual affliction, emphasizing the **psalm's dramatic movement from despair to hope**. The commentary identifies this as a penitential psalm where David experiences three interconnected troubles: bodily sickness, spiritual anguish from sin's burden, and enemies' mockery. Henry notes the psalm's structure mirrors Hannah's experience—beginning with "sorrowful spirit" but ending with joy after prayer.

The central theological theme revolves around **God's discipline versus God's wrath**. David doesn't reject divine correction but pleads: "rebuke me not in thy anger, neither chasten me in thy hot displeasure" (v.1). Henry explains that godly people can accept loving chastisement but cannot bear the withdrawal of God's favor.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For the Afflicted**: "Is any afflicted? Is any sick? Let him sing this psalm." He emphasizes that even godly people like David experience illness and that "great men are men, and subject to the common calamities of human life."

**For Penitents**: True repentance involves genuine grief over sin. David's tears that "water his couch" demonstrate that "sorrow for sin ought to be great sorrow." Henry notes that "true penitents weep in their retirements," contrasting authentic grief with Pharisaical display.

**For Prayer**: The psalm teaches believers to prioritize spiritual concerns over physical ones, seeking God's favor above bodily comfort. Henry observes that "it is an unspeakable privilege that we have a God to go to in our afflictions."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry interprets David's physical weakness ("my bones are vexed") alongside spiritual distress ("my soul is also sore vexed") as interconnected sufferings where sin consciousness intensifies physical pain.

**Verses 4-5**: David's plea "in death there is no remembrance of thee" receives careful attention. Henry argues this refers both to the "second death" where there's no praise, and to earthly service opportunities ending at death. He notes that "services of saints on earth...are more laudable" than heavenly worship because earthly saints fight "against sin and Satan."

**Verses 6-7**: The imagery of tears flooding David's bed prompts Henry to observe that "David had more courage...than to mourn thus for any outward affliction"—indicating the deeper spiritual anguish driving such grief.

**Verses 8-10**: The dramatic shift to confidence demonstrates answered prayer's power: "What a sudden change is here for the better!"

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects Psalm 6 to multiple scriptures:

- **Job's sufferings** for parallel physical/spiritual trials
- **Hannah's prayer** (1 Samuel) for the sorrow-to-joy pattern  
- **Christ's sufferings** (Hebrews 5:7) as the ultimate fulfillment, noting Jesus "often wept" and cried "My soul is exceedingly sorrowful"
- **Other psalms** (30:9, 88:10, 115:17) and **Isaiah 38:18** for similar death/praise themes
- **Peter's denial and tears** for authentic repentance
- **Philippians 1** regarding earthly service versus heavenly joy

Henry emphasizes that "David was a type of Christ" throughout, particularly in experiencing soul-trouble as the deepest suffering. The commentary presents this psalm as both historical record and prophetic pointer to Christ's passion, where spiritual anguish exceeded physical torment.

The overall message encourages believers that sincere prayer transforms despair into confidence, and that God's people can move from weeping to worship through authentic communion with their heavenly Father.

Chapter 7
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 7: Summary

### **Main Theological Themes and Interpretations**

Henry interprets Psalm 7 as David's response to false accusations, likely from "Cush the Benjamite" (possibly Saul or his kinsman). The central theological theme is **divine justice and vindication of the innocent**. Henry emphasizes that "God is the patron of wronged innocency" and serves as the ultimate righteous Judge when earthly courts fail.

A significant interpretive element is Henry's **typological reading**: "In this David was a type of Christ, who was himself, and still is in his members, thus injured, but will certainly be righted at last." This connects David's suffering to Christ's persecution and ultimate vindication.

The psalm demonstrates the proper relationship between **human suffering and divine appeal**. Henry notes that "the injuries men do us should drive us to God" and praises David's composure: his spirit "was not ruffled by it, nor cast down, but so composed and cheerful that he was still in tune for sacred songs."

### **Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons:

1. **Wrongful accusations should drive us to devotion**: "Let the injuries we receive from men, instead of provoking our passions, kindle and excite our devotions."

2. **Clear conscience provides strength**: When falsely accused, "it is a great comfort if our own consciences acquit us" - using the Latin motto: "Be this thy brazen bulwark of defence, Still to preserve thy conscious innocence."

3. **Good deeds become future testimony**: "If we render good for evil, and deny ourselves the gratifications of our passion, our so doing may turn to us for a testimony, more than we think of, another day."

### **Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 1-2**: Henry emphasizes David's threefold plea - his relationship with God ("my God"), his trust in God, and the urgent danger ("tear my soul like a lion"). The lion metaphor shows the enemy's "pride, and pleasure, and power."

**Verses 3-5**: David's oath of innocence includes his evidence - he "delivered him that without cause is mine enemy." Henry sees this referencing David's sparing of Saul's life, proving David harbored no murderous intent.

**Verses 6-9**: The call for God to "awake" doesn't suggest divine negligence but that "delays were no neglects." Henry interprets "return thou on high" as possibly pointing to Christ's resurrection and ascension.

**Verse 9**: The prayer for wickedness to end and righteousness to be established becomes universal - not just about David's enemies but "the conversion of sinners and the establishment of saints."

### **Cross-References to Other Scripture**

Henry connects the psalm to numerous passages:
- **Genesis 15:1** - God as shield
- **1 Samuel 24:4, 26:12** - David sparing Saul's life
- **Acts 24:13** - Paul's defense against false accusations  
- **2 Timothy 4:17** - Paul comparing Nero to a lion
- **1 Chronicles 13:3** - neglect of the ark under Saul
- **Habakkuk 3:1** - explaining "Shiggaion"

The commentary weaves these references to show consistent biblical themes of divine justice, patient endurance under persecution, and ultimate vindication of the righteous.

Chapter 8
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 8: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 8 as "a solemn meditation on, and admiration of, the glory and greatness of God." The psalm's structure demonstrates God's excellence through a progression: it "begins and ends with the same acknowledgment of the transcendent excellency of God's name" as proof of divine glory through divine goodness.

**Christological Application**: Henry emphasizes that this psalm applies prophetically to Christ and redemption. He connects verse 2 to Matthew 21:16 (children crying "Hosanna") and verses 5-6 to Hebrews 2:6-8, showing how Christ was "made a little lower than the angels" in humiliation, then "crowned with glory and honour" in exaltation.

**Kingdom Theology**: Henry identifies three kingdoms where God's glory appears: nature, providence, and grace (the Messiah's kingdom), urging readers that "observing the glory of God in the kingdom of nature and providence...should be led by that...to the contemplation of his glory in the kingdom of grace."

## Key Practical Applications

**Worship and Wonder**: Henry encourages believers to "consider the heavens" as a spiritual discipline, noting that unlike beasts who "look downwards to the earth, man is made erect to look upwards towards heaven" to "set his affections on things above."

**Humility and Gratitude**: The commentary emphasizes human smallness before God's majesty while marveling at divine condescension. Henry asks: "What is man (sinful, weak, miserable man, a creature so forgetful of thee) that thou art thus mindful of him?"

**Gospel Mission**: Henry applies verse 2 to gospel ministry, showing how "by the apostles, who were looked upon but as babes, unlearned and ignorant men...the devil's kingdom should be thrown down as Jericho's walls were by the sound of rams' horns."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: God's name is "excellent in all the earth" both through creation's testimony and gospel proclamation making God's name "great in Israel only" now "great in all the earth."

**Verse 2**: "Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings" demonstrates God's power through weakness across three realms - nature (infant care), providence (using simple people), and grace (gospel preached by "poor fishermen").

**Verses 3-4**: Henry notes David likely wrote this during a "night-walk" under "moon-light," explaining why only moon and stars (not sun) are mentioned.

**Verses 5-8**: Human dominion reflects being "crowned with glory and honour" as God's vice-regents, though Henry sees ultimate fulfillment in Christ's cosmic authority.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout: Romans 1:20 (creation's testimony), Mark 16:15-16 (gospel to all earth), Matthew 21:16 (babes' praise), Hebrews 2:6-8 and 1 Corinthians 15:27 (Christ's exaltation), Acts 4:13 (unlearned apostles), 1 Corinthians 1:27-28 (God using weak things), Isaiah 45:4 (God using those who don't know Him), and Psalm 115:16 (heavens belong to God).

The commentary masterfully balances creation's grandeur with Christ's supremacy, showing how this ancient hymn of praise finds ultimate fulfillment in the gospel story.

Chapter 9
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 9: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Divine Justice and Sovereignty**: Henry emphasizes God's role as eternal judge, stating that "God sits in the throne of judgment" and "we are sure that the judgment of God is according to truth." He presents God as the ultimate arbiter who maintains righteous causes and defeats the wicked systematically through rebuke, destruction, and perpetual infamy.

**Messianic Application**: Henry explicitly connects this psalm to "the kingdom of the Messiah," noting that Christ's enemies "have been in part destroyed already, and shall be yet more and more till they all be made his footstool." This provides both assurance for believers and glory to God.

**God's Eternal Nature vs. Temporal Destruction**: A central contrast emerges between the permanence of God ("the Lord shall endure for ever") and the complete obliteration of the wicked, whose "very memorial has perished."

## Key Practical Applications

**Wholehearted Worship**: Henry emphasizes that acceptable praise requires sincerity—"we must praise him in sincerity, with our hearts, and not only with our lips." He advocates for comprehensive gratitude: "when we give thanks for some one particular mercy we should take occasion thence to remember former mercies."

**Faith and Trust**: "The better God is known the more he is trusted." Henry provides practical examples: those who know God's wisdom "will trust him further than they can see him," while those who know His power "will trust him when creature-confidences fail."

**Joy Centered in God**: "Whatever occurs to make us glad, our joy must pass through it, and terminate in God only: I will be glad and rejoice in thee, not in the gift so much as in the giver."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry outlines eight principles of praise, including that "holy joy is the life of thankful praise" and connects this to Revelation's triumphant passages (Rev. 12:10, 19:5, 15:3-4).

**Verse 3**: He notes how enemies are defeated at God's "very presence," referencing Jesus making enemies "fall back at his presence" (John 18:6).

**Verses 5-6**: Henry describes God's three-step process against the wicked: rebuke (warning), destruction, and burial in "perpetual infamy."

**Verse 10**: He emphasizes the correlation between knowledge and trust: "God never did, nor ever will, disown or desert any that duly seek to him and trust in him."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **2 Samuel 5:8** for historical context of David's victories
- **Esther 6:13** illustrating how retreat becomes ruin for God's enemies  
- **John 18:6** showing Jesus's power over enemies
- **Job 13:15, 35:14** and **2 Chronicles 20:12** demonstrating trust in various circumstances
- **Isaiah 33:1** regarding the reversal of fortunes for Israel's oppressors
- **Psalms 29:10, 93:2** emphasizing God's unshaken sovereignty

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches believers to find refuge in God's eternal nature while anticipating His final judgment that will vindicate the righteous and completely destroy the wicked. The practical result should be wholehearted praise, unwavering trust, and active declaration of God's mighty works among all peoples.

Chapter 10
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 10: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 10 as David's lament over God's apparent withdrawal during times of wickedness and persecution. The psalm addresses **divine hiddenness** and the **temporary prosperity of the wicked**. Henry emphasizes that "God's withdrawings are very grievous to his people at any time, but especially in times of trouble," while noting this often reflects our own "unbelief" rather than God's actual absence.

The commentary presents **pride as the root of all persecution and tyranny**, both in church and state. Henry identifies two primary charges against the wicked: pride and persecution, with "the former the cause of the latter." The theological tension centers on God's justice versus the apparent delay in divine intervention.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**On responding to wickedness**: Rather than "passionate and satirical invectives against bad men," believers should bring concerns "to God in prayer, for he alone can make them better." This reflects a mature approach to confronting evil.

**On spiritual warfare**: When singing or praying this psalm, Henry recommends cultivating "holy indignation at the wickedness of the oppressors, a tender compassion of the miseries of the oppressed, and a pious zeal for the glory and honour of God."

**On divine timing**: Henry counsels patience, emphasizing that believers should maintain "firm belief that he will, in due time, give redress to the injured and reckon with the injurious."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry interprets God "standing afar off" as often reflecting our own distance from God through unbelief rather than God's actual withdrawal.

**Verses 2-3**: The wicked's blessing of the covetous whom "the Lord abhorreth" demonstrates how "God and men differ in their sentiments of persons."

**Verse 4**: "God is not in all his thoughts" defines practical atheism - living without prayer means "living without God."

**Verse 11**: The wicked's claim that "God hath forgotten" represents a threefold blasphemy against God's omniscience, holiness, and justice.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- 2 Timothy 3:1 (perilous times)
- Luke 18:11 (Pharisee's prayer)
- Hosea 12:8 (Ephraim's boasting)
- Psalm 49:13 (approving the wicked)
- Luke 12:20 (God's judgment on the rich fool)
- Proverbs 6:17 (pride in countenance)
- Isaiah 47:7 and Revelation 18:7 (Babylon's false security)
- Ezekiel 8:12, 9:9 (God forsaking the earth)

Henry's commentary reveals deep scriptural interconnectedness while providing practical wisdom for believers facing persecution and apparent divine silence.

Chapter 11
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 11: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 11 as David's **struggle against temptation to distrust God** during Saul's persecution. The central theological conflict is between **human wisdom advocating flight** and **divine trust demanding perseverance**. Henry emphasizes God's **sovereignty, omniscience, and righteous judgment** as the foundation for unwavering faith.

The commentary presents God in multiple roles: as **ruler on His heavenly throne**, **perfect judge who sees all hearts**, and **protector of the righteous**. Henry stresses that God's temple residence (whether heavenly or in His church) means "though he is out of our sight, we are not out of his."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Against Despair in Public Crisis**: "In times of public fear, when the insults of the church's enemies are daring and threatening, it will be profitable to meditate on this psalm."

**Rejecting Cowardly Counsel**: David's friends advised flight, but Henry notes this "would savour of unbelief and would ill become a saint." Believers must distinguish between prudent caution and faithless retreat.

**Maintaining Foundation Principles**: When enemies attack core beliefs, Henry warns: "If you destroy the foundations...you ruin them, and break their hearts indeed." Religious principles must be "held fast against all temptations to infidelity."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry sees David's declaration "In the LORD put I my trust" as a **preemptive strike against temptation**—establishing his position before addressing doubts.

**Verses 2-3**: The "foundations" represent core religious principles. Henry interprets the question "what can the righteous do?" as either friends' despair over political chaos or enemies' mockery of faith.

**Verse 4**: "The shaking of a tree makes it take the deeper and faster root"—opposition strengthens rather than weakens true faith.

**Verses 5-6**: God's hatred of violence leads to inevitable judgment described as "fire, and brimstone, and a horrible tempest," alluding to Sodom and Gomorrah as "a figure of the vengeance of eternal fire."

**Verse 7**: God's favor toward the upright means "His countenance doth behold the upright"—divine pleasure in righteousness guarantees protection.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to numerous passages:

- **Psalm 75:3, 82:5** (foundations of government)
- **Psalm 14:6, 3:2** (enemies mocking God's help)  
- **Psalm 64:3** (words as arrows)
- **Job 38:33; Psalm 103:19** (God's heavenly rule)
- **Daniel 4:26** (heavens ruling)
- **Deuteronomy 8:16** (trials for ultimate good)
- **Jude 7** (eternal fire judgment)
- **Psalm 16:5** (God as portion)

Henry masterfully weaves these references to demonstrate that trusting God despite apparent circumstances is a consistent biblical theme, encouraging believers that their foundation remains unshakeable regardless of temporal troubles.

Chapter 12
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 12 Summary

## Historical Context and Setting
Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 12 as written during "a general decay of honesty and piety both in court and country," likely during Saul's reign when David suffered from treacherous friends and hostile enemies. Henry describes it as "calculated for a bad reign" and quotes "O tempora, O mores!--Oh the times! Oh the manners!" to capture the moral crisis.

## Main Theological Themes

**Divine Sovereignty vs. Human Pride**: Henry emphasizes God's ultimate authority over proud sinners who claim "our lips are our own; who is lord over us?" He notes this reflects "an insolent contempt of God's dominion" and demonstrates the absurdity of rejecting divine authority over those "in whom we live, and move, and have our being."

**God's Faithfulness vs. Human Deception**: A central contrast emerges between unreliable human words and divine reliability. Henry declares that "though men are false, God is faithful" and describes God's words as "pure words...like silver tried in a furnace," emphasizing their sincerity, preciousness, and proven reliability.

**Divine Justice and Protection**: God promises to "arise" for the oppressed and "cut off all flattering lips." Henry presents this as both timely intervention (when oppressors peak in pride and victims reach deepest distress) and effective deliverance.

## Key Practical Applications

**Refuge in Crisis**: When "all other helps and helpers fail," believers must cry "Help, Lord!" Henry encourages turning to God when even godly people seem to disappear from society.

**Discernment in Communication**: Henry warns against the "devil's image complete, a complication of malice and falsehood" seen in flattering speech with double hearts, urging believers to recognize and avoid such deception.

**Hope During Moral Decline**: Despite widespread corruption, Henry assures that "God will secure his chosen remnant to himself" and "preserve that to his heavenly kingdom," encouraging perseverance through difficult times.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry identifies five characteristics of truly bad times: general decay of godly people (v.1), prevalent dissimulation and flattery (v.2), insolent defiance of God (v.4), oppression of the poor (v.5), and elevation of wicked leaders (v.8). He particularly emphasizes that "when the vilest men are exalted" in authority, "wickedness then grows impudent and insolent."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects this psalm to 2 Timothy 3:1 regarding perilous times, Elijah's experience in Romans 11:3, and Jeremiah 5:1 concerning the scarcity of just people. He references Micah 7:5-6 and Jeremiah 9:4-5 regarding the breakdown of trust, and draws parallels to Israel's oppression in Egypt and God's timely deliverance.

This commentary presents Psalm 12 as both a lament over moral decay and a confident declaration of God's ultimate sovereignty and faithfulness to preserve His people through the darkest times.

Chapter 13
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 13: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 13 as "the deserted soul's case and cure," focusing on the spiritual journey from despair to faith-filled triumph. The central theological theme revolves around **God's apparent withdrawal and its ultimate purpose**. Henry emphasizes that even godly believers experience seasons where "God sometimes hides his face from his own children, and leaves them in the dark concerning their interest in him."

A key theological insight is Henry's understanding of David's complaints as both **legitimate expressions of human frailty** and potential spiritual dangers. He notes that while such "peevish expression of prevailing fear" may be "indecent and culpable," God pardons it when it "arises from a high esteem and earnest desire of God's favour."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing spiritual darkness:

**Prayer as transformation**: He demonstrates "the power of faith, the power of prayer, and how good it is to draw near to God." The psalm models how bringing "our cares and griefs to the throne of grace" can dramatically shift our spiritual state.

**Progressive faith**: Henry shows the movement from complaint to praise as a pattern for believers: "If we bring our cares and griefs to the throne of grace, and leave them there, we may go away like Hannah, and our countenance will be no more sad."

**Remembrance as remedy**: The commentary emphasizes celebrating past mercies even in present darkness: "I will sing in remembrance of what he has done formerly."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets the fourfold "How long?" as legitimate spiritual wrestling, comparing David's experience to Christ's cry of abandonment on the cross.

**Verse 3**: "Enlighten mine eyes" receives rich interpretation as strengthening faith, providing guidance, and refreshing the soul with "the joy of thy salvation."

**Verses 5-6**: Henry marvels at the "surprising change" from despair to praise, noting David speaks of deliverance as already accomplished "because by faith he had received the earnest of the salvation."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects this psalm to broader scriptural themes through multiple references:
- **Isaiah 49:14 and 40:27** for similar expressions of feeling forgotten
- **1 Samuel 14:27 and Ezra 9:8** for the concept of enlightened eyes
- **Romans 15:13 and 1 Peter 1:8** linking faith to joy
- **Psalm 147:11** emphasizing God's pleasure in those who hope in His mercy

Henry also draws a significant typological connection, seeing David's experience as prefiguring "Christ upon the cross, crying out, My God, why hast thou forsaken me?"

The commentary concludes with practical counsel for using this psalm, encouraging believers to "thank God" if they lack such complaints, while using it to "sympathize with those that are troubled in mind, and encourage ourselves in our most holy faith and joy."

Chapter 14
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 14: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Henry interprets Psalm 14 as a comprehensive description of **human depravity and universal corruption**. Drawing from Paul's use of this psalm in Romans 3, he presents it as revealing "the sinfulness of the sin we are conceived and born in, and the deplorable corruption of a great part of mankind." The psalm traces all human wickedness to its fountain—"the general corruption of nature"—showing that sin is both "malignant and epidemic."

The central theological concept is **practical atheism**: "there is something of practical atheism at the bottom of all sin." Henry argues that while people may not intellectually deny God's existence, they live as if He doesn't matter, saying in their hearts "There is no God."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**On Human Nature**: Sin has so corrupted humanity that people "have become odious to their Maker and utterly incapable of answering the ends of their creation." This should lead believers to "lament the corruption of our own nature, and see what need we have of the grace of God."

**On Persecution**: Those who "eat up my people as they eat bread" reveal their hatred for God through their treatment of His people. Henry warns that "it is ill jesting with edged-tools and dangerous persecuting those that make God their refuge."

**On Prayer**: The root of wickedness is prayerlessness—"What good can be expected from those that live without prayer?"

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The "fool" who denies God is not expressing intellectual conviction but wishful thinking: "He cannot satisfy himself that there is none, but he wishes there were none." This denial occurs in the heart as "he dares not speak it out, lest he be confuted."

**Verses 2-3**: God's universal survey reveals complete corruption. Henry notes that "whatever good is in any of the children of men...it is not of themselves; it is God's work in them."

**Verses 4-6**: The "workers of iniquity" demonstrate their wickedness through their treatment of God's people and their mockery of religious devotion.

**Verse 7**: Henry sees both immediate and eschatological fulfillment—comfort for David's situation, but ultimately pointing toward Messianic salvation when "the Redeemer shall ascend on high, and lead captivity captive."

## Cross-References

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other scriptures: Romans 3:9-19 for Paul's use of these verses; Genesis 1:31 and 6:5 contrasting God's original "very good" creation with humanity's later "very bad" condition; 1 John 5:19 on the world lying in wickedness; and Romans 11:26 regarding the Redeemer coming to turn away ungodliness from Jacob.

The commentary concludes with eschatological hope: "The second coming of Christ, finally to extinguish the dominion of sin and Satan, will be the completing of this salvation."

Chapter 15
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalms 15 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies Psalm 15's central purpose as showing "the way to heaven" and proving that "if we would be happy, we must be holy and honest." He connects this directly to Christ's teaching in Matthew 19:17: "If thou wilt enter into life, keep the commandments."

The psalm's structure moves from inquiry (verse 1) to instruction (verses 2-5) to assurance (verse 5). Henry interprets the "tabernacle" as representing the church militant—"typified by Moses's tabernacle, fitted to a wilderness-state, mean and movable"—while the "holy hill" symbolizes the church triumphant, alluding to Mount Zion where Solomon's temple would stand.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that **sincerity** is foundational: "I know no religion but sincerity." The true citizen of Zion must be "really what he professes to be" and "an Israelite indeed in whom is no guile."

He stresses **relational ethics**, particularly regarding reputation: one "makes not others' faults the subject of his common talk" and "makes the best of every body, and the worst of nobody." Henry advocates for active protection of others' good names—if given an ill report about a neighbor, "he will disprove it if he can; if not, it shall die with him and go no further."

**Integrity in commitments** receives special attention: even when an oath proves disadvantageous, "he adheres to it and changes not," because "an oath is a sacred thing, which we must not think to play fast and loose with."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry notes this question must be addressed to God himself, as "it is fit he himself should give laws to his servants, and appoint the conditions of his favours."

**Verse 2**: Emphasizes comprehensive righteousness—being "just both to God and man" with prayers and promises that "come not out of feigned lips."

**Verse 3**: Focuses extensively on speech ethics, noting that many "who would not otherwise wrong their neighbours, make nothing of" injuring reputations.

**Verse 4**: Addresses proper evaluation of people based on character rather than status. A godly person "thinks the better of no man's wickedness for his pomp and grandeur" while honoring those who fear the Lord regardless of their social position.

**Verse 5**: Discusses both oath-keeping and economic justice, interpreting usury contextually while emphasizing generosity toward the poor and rejection of bribery.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical texts: Genesis 17:1 (walking uprightly), John 1:47 (Israelite without guile), 2 Corinthians 1:12 (integrity), Luke 18:18 and Acts 16:30 (seeking the way to heaven), Proverbs 25:23 (silencing backbiting tongues), and Psalm 125:1 (Zion's stability).

## Conclusion and Assurance

The psalm concludes with divine assurance: "He that doeth these things shall never be moved." Henry explains this means being "like Zion-hill itself, which cannot be moved" because every true church member "is built upon a rock, which the gates of hell cannot prevail against." This promises both present peace and future bliss, with God's grace preserving the faithful against all temptations and troubles.

Chapter 16
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 16: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry emphasizes that **Psalm 16 contains "something of David in it, but much more of Christ."** The psalm operates on two levels: David speaks as a member of Christ representing all Christians (verses 1-7), and as a prophetic type of Christ himself (verses 8-11). Henry calls it a "golden psalm" that speaks "so plainly of Christ and his resurrection" and represents "the true treasure hidden in the field of the Old Testament."

The commentary establishes that verses 9-11 cannot apply to David literally since "David died, and was buried, and saw corruption," but must refer to Christ's resurrection, as confirmed by Peter and Paul in Acts 2:24 and 13:36.

## Key Practical Applications

**God as Our Portion:** Henry emphasizes choosing "the Lord for the portion of my inheritance and of my cup" rather than worldly pursuits. Believers should find complete satisfaction in God's love, communion, and promises rather than material wealth.

**Service to Saints:** Since our goodness "extends not to God" (who needs nothing), believers must direct their service toward "the saints in the earth" who are God's "excellent ones." This includes both delighting in fellowship with believers and practically helping their needs.

**Rejection of False Worship:** Christians must maintain absolute separation from idolatry, understanding that "those that multiply gods multiply griefs to themselves."

**Conscience and Night Seasons:** Henry highlights how "the reins also instruct me in the night seasons"—believers should use times of solitude for self-examination and spiritual reflection.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 2:** The phrase "O my soul, thou hast said unto the LORD" emphasizes that "covenanting with God must be heart-work." Believers should regularly remind themselves of their commitment to God.

**Verse 3:** Saints are "excellent ones" despite earthly poverty because "God makes them excellent by the grace he gives them...they are his jewels, his peculiar treasure."

**Verse 6:** "The lines have fallen to me in pleasant places" expresses contentment with God's providence. Henry notes that "gracious persons...never covet more than God."

**Verses 8-11:** Applied to Christ, these verses speak of God's "special presence...with the Redeemer in his services and sufferings" and "the prospect which the Redeemer had of his own resurrection and the glory that should follow."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively references **Acts 2:25-31** and **Acts 13:36** as apostolic interpretations proving the Messianic nature of verses 9-11. Other key references include:

- **Job 22:2; 35:7** - God gains nothing from human service
- **1 Chronicles 29:14** - "Of thy own have we given thee"  
- **Psalm 119:63** - David's companionship with God-fearers
- **Proverbs 8:31** - Christ's delight in humanity
- **John 17:19** - "For their sakes I sanctify myself"
- **Jeremiah 17:10** - The reins as conscience

Henry masterfully demonstrates how this psalm bridges personal devotion and prophetic revelation, showing both immediate spiritual application for believers and ultimate fulfillment in Christ's resurrection victory.

Chapter 17
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 17: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 17 as David's prayer during persecution, likely by Saul, while emphasizing its typological significance pointing to Christ, "who was perfectly innocent, and yet was hated and persecuted." The psalm demonstrates God's role as righteous judge and protector of the innocent, with Henry highlighting the contrast between earthly injustice and divine equity.

The commentary emphasizes **God's omniscience as comfort to the righteous**: "God's omniscience is as much the joy of the upright as it is the terror of hypocrites." Henry underscores that sincere believers need not fear divine scrutiny, as God's judgment reveals truth and reverses unrighteous human decisions.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**On Prayer and Sincerity**: Believers must avoid "feigned prayers" which "are fruitless," ensuring their hearts lead their petitions. He emphasizes maintaining consistent prayer habits: "It will be a great comfort to us if trouble, when it comes, find the wheels of prayer a-going."

**On Moral Integrity**: Henry highlights David's "fixed resolution against all sins of the tongue" and avoidance of "paths of the destroyer." He notes that careful speech control evidences spiritual maturity: "If any offend not in word, the same is a perfect man."

**On Dependence on Grace**: The commentary stresses continual reliance on God's sustaining power: "We stand no longer than he is pleased to hold us," encouraging believers to "fetch in daily fresh supplies of grace and strength."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets David's appeal to God's tribunal as refuge from Saul's prejudiced judgment, emphasizing heaven's court as the ultimate authority for the oppressed.

**Verse 3**: The phrase "Thou hast proved mine heart" receives detailed treatment, with Henry explaining God's testing through both conscience ("the candle of the Lord") and providence, including David's opportunities to harm Saul.

**Verses 8-9**: The metaphors "apple of the eye" and "shadow of thy wings" represent God's tender, protective care, which Henry connects to believers committing their souls to divine keeping.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **1 Samuel 24:12** - David's public appeal to God as judge between himself and Saul
- **Psalm 39:1** - David's commitment to bridling his mouth
- **James 3:2** - Perfect man who controls his speech
- **Psalm 119:9** - God's word as guide from evil paths
- **1 John 5:18** - Keeping oneself from the wicked one's touch

The commentary concludes with David's confidence in future vindication and satisfaction in beholding God's face, which Henry sees as both personal hope and prophetic of eternal glory with Christ.

Chapter 18
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 18: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 18 as David's magnificent thanksgiving for divine deliverances, emphasizing that "the piety far exceeds the poetry." The central theological theme is **God's faithful protection of His covenant people**. Henry interprets the psalm as demonstrating God's multiple attributes: His almighty power (making earth tremble), His righteous anger against persecutors, His condescension (bowing heavens to come down personally), and His mysterious yet merciful nature (making "darkness his pavilion" while bringing light).

A crucial theme is **divine reward for righteousness**. Henry explains that God's deliverances vindicated David's integrity, "rewarding him according to his righteousness" - not earning salvation through works, but God confirming the genuine faith of His servant before accusers.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Prayer and Trust**: "This further use we should make of our deliverances, we must not only love God the better, but love prayer the better." He emphasizes that earnest prayer receives direct divine response.

**Gratitude and Love**: The opening "I will love thee, O LORD, my strength" should characterize all believers' response to God's mercies. Henry notes we are "very ungrateful if we grudge him so poor a return."

**Confidence in Trials**: Believers can trust that "God will not only deliver his people out of their troubles in due time, but he will sustain them and bear them up under their troubles in the mean time."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry provides rich exposition of the dramatic theophany (verses 7-15), interpreting the earthquake, fire, and storm imagery as God's powerful intervention. He notes that "Little appeared of man, but much of God, in these deliverances."

On verse 19's "large place," Henry explains this represents both literal relief from constraints and spiritual enlargement: "where I had room, not only to turn, but to thrive in."

Regarding David's claims of righteousness (verses 20-24), Henry carefully explains this refers to David's innocence regarding specific accusations, not sinless perfection.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to **2 Samuel 22** (the original version), **Acts 2:21** ("Whosoever shall call upon the name of the Lord shall be saved"), **Hebrews 5:7** (Christ's prayers in distress), and **Isaiah 45:15** (God hiding Himself yet remaining Savior).

Significantly, Henry applies the psalm **christologically**: "we may apply it to Christ the Son of David. The sorrows of death surrounded him; in his distress he prayed...God brought him out, in his resurrection, into a large place, because he delighted in him."

Henry masterfully balances historical interpretation with devotional application, showing how David's experiences prefigure Christ's victory and provide patterns for Christian trust in divine deliverance.

Chapter 19
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 19: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry frames Psalm 19 around **two excellent books** God has published for human instruction: **the book of creation** (verses 1-6) and **the book of Scripture** (verses 7-14). This dual revelation demonstrates God's progressive disclosure of Himself.

The **natural revelation** through creation serves as a universal witness that "declares the glory of God" and reveals His "invisible things...whose being appears incontestably evident." Henry emphasizes this serves both to "show the folly of atheists" who see effects but deny the cause, and "show the folly of idolaters" who worship creation rather than the Creator.

The **special revelation** through Scripture is presented as superior and necessary because while natural revelation "might have served if man had retained his integrity," fallen humanity requires God's word for restoration. Henry describes Scripture as having divine authority evidenced by the repetition of "Jehovah" six times in verses 7-9.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**From Creation**: Humans should "look upwards" both physically and spiritually, since unlike beasts "made to look downwards," humans are "made erect" because "upwards his spirit must shortly go and his thoughts should now rise."

**From Scripture**: The word serves multiple transformative purposes - it "converts the soul," makes "the simple wise," and provides both "warning" and "great reward" for obedience. Henry emphasizes that humble teachability is essential: "Those that are humbly simple, sensible of their own folly and willing to be taught, shall be made wise by the word of God."

**Personal Application**: The psalm's conclusion (verses 12-14) teaches believers to pray for cleansing from "secret faults" and protection from "presumptuous sins," seeking that our words and meditations be acceptable to God.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: The "day unto day" and "night unto night" succession demonstrates God's covenant faithfulness, referencing God's promise to Noah that "while the earth remains, day and night shall not cease."

**Verses 4-6**: Henry suggests David may have written this "when he had the rising sun in view." The sun imagery depicts it as "a bridegroom coming out of his chamber" and one who "rejoices as a strong man to run a race," which Henry connects to "Christ, the Sun of righteousness" who finished His work with satisfaction.

**Verses 7-9**: Henry meticulously analyzes the six descriptions of God's word, noting each contains "the name Jehovah" with "no vain repetition." He emphasizes the word is "perfect," "sure," "right," "pure," "clean," and "true...altogether."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry makes extensive scriptural connections:

- **Romans 10:18** - Paul uses verse 4 to justify preaching to Gentiles, since creation already witnessed to them
- **Romans 1:20-21** - Creation leaves idolaters "without excuse" 
- **Genesis 8:22** and **Jeremiah 33:20; 31:35** - Day/night succession reflects covenant stability
- **2 Timothy 3:15,17** - Scripture's role in making wise unto salvation and perfecting believers
- **Psalm 8:3** - Creation as "works of his fingers"
- **John 3:29** - Bridegroom imagery connected to Christ
- **John 15:3** - God's word as cleansing agent

Henry concludes that while we benefit from "the lights of heaven," we must always look "above and beyond them to the Sun of righteousness," demonstrating his consistent Christocentric interpretation of Old Testament texts.

Chapter 20
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 20: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Prayer for Authority and Typological Fulfillment**: Henry presents Psalm 20 as both a prayer for King David and a prophetic type pointing to Christ. The psalm demonstrates God's will "that prayers, intercessions, and thanksgivings, should be made, in special manner, for kings and all in authority." Henry emphasizes that "David may well be looked upon as a type of Christ, to whose kingdom and its interests among men the church was, in every age, a hearty well-wisher."

**Divine versus Human Confidence**: A central theological contrast emerges between worldly and godly trust. Henry notes the fundamental difference: "The children of this world trust in second causes...they trust in chariots and in horses," while believers "will remember, and rely upon, the name of the Lord our God."

## Key Practical Applications

**Mutual Prayer Responsibility**: Even great leaders need prayer support. Henry observes that "Even great and good men, and those that know ever so well how to pray for themselves, must not despise, but earnestly desire, the prayers of others for them." Leaders should "esteem and encourage praying people, to reckon them their strength."

**Proper Confidence in Crisis**: Rather than trusting in military might or earthly resources, believers should remember that "A believing obedient trust in God and his name is the surest way both to preferment and to establishment, to rise and to stand upright."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-4** detail five specific prayer requests: that God answer the king's prayers, protect his person, enable his public undertakings with "help out of the sanctuary," accept his sacrifices, and grant success to his noble designs.

**Verse 6** shows David's confidence: "Now know I that the LORD saveth his anointed" - Henry explains this assurance comes because God "hath stirred up the hearts of the seed of Jacob to pray for him."

**Verses 7-8** present the famous contrast where those trusting in chariots and horses "are brought down and fallen" while those trusting God's name "are risen, and stand upright."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to numerous biblical passages: Zechariah 12:5,10 (praying people as strength), Proverbs 18:10 ("The name of the Lord is a strong tower"), 1 Samuel 17:45 (David facing Goliath), Hebrews 5:7 (Christ being heard), and 2 Samuel 8:4 regarding David's victories over Syria's chariots and horsemen.

**Messianic Application**: Henry extensively applies the psalm to Christ's redemptive work, noting how "these prayers for David are prophecies concerning Christ" and were "abundantly answered" in Jesus's victory over "the powers of darkness" and his successful intercession.

Chapter 21
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 21: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 21 as a thanksgiving psalm that complements the petitionary nature of Psalm 20. The central theological theme revolves around **divine sovereignty in kingship** and God's faithfulness in answering prayer. Henry emphasizes that this psalm has both immediate application to David and **prophetic significance pointing to Christ as Messiah**.

The commentary highlights God's "preventing grace" - blessings that come "before we prayed for them, before we were ready for them." Henry notes that "all our blessings are blessings of goodness, and are owing, not at all to any merit of ours, but purely and only to God's goodness."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For Rulers and Citizens**: "Happy the people the character of whose king it is that he makes God's strength his confidence and God's salvation his joy." He emphasizes that subjects should pray for and give thanks for their kings, "in whose prosperity we share."

**For Prayer Life**: "God's gracious returns of prayer do, in a special manner, require our humble returns of praise." Henry teaches that answered prayer creates obligation for thanksgiving.

**For Trust in God**: The commentary stresses that true security comes through divine mercy: "The mercy of the Most High...is enough to secure our happiness, and therefore our trust in that mercy should be enough to silence all our fears."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-6**: Henry sees these as celebrating God's abundant provision that exceeds expectations. On verse 4's "length of days for ever and ever," he notes this points beyond David to Christ's eternal kingdom: "He was dead, indeed, that we might live through him; but he is alive, and lives for evermore."

**Verse 6**: The phrase "made him most blessed for ever" leads Henry to observe that "none besides is blessed for ever, much less a blessing for ever to that eminency that the expression denotes" except Christ.

**Verses 7-13**: These verses address the destruction of God's enemies. Henry emphasizes that opposition to God's anointed is ultimately opposition to God himself: "Those that aimed to un-king David aimed, in effect, to un-God Jehovah."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **2 Samuel 3:36** - on people rejoicing with their king
- **Psalm 20:4** - connecting to the previous psalm's petitions
- **2 Peter 1:17** and **John 17:5** - regarding Christ's glory
- **John 15:23, 25** - on hatred without cause
- **Psalm 2:1** - on vain imaginings against God
- **Luke 19:27** and **Matthew 13:42** - on the destruction of Christ's enemies

The commentary consistently moves between David's historical situation and its fulfillment in Christ, demonstrating Henry's typological interpretation method that sees Old Testament figures as shadows pointing to greater New Testament realities.

Chapter 22
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 22: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 22 as a **prophetic psalm primarily about Christ's sufferings and subsequent glory**. He emphasizes that "The Spirit of Christ, which was in the prophets, testifies in this psalm...the sufferings of Christ and the glory that should follow." While acknowledging David's authorship, Henry sees David as **both a type of Christ and divinely inspired beyond his own understanding** to prophesy about the Messiah.

The psalm is structured in two major movements: **Christ's humiliation (verses 1-21) and Christ's exaltation (verses 22-31)**. Henry notes that Christ's sufferings were necessary because "having made himself sin for us," the Father laid upon him "the present impressions of his wrath and displeasure against sin."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Spiritual desertion is the saints' sorest affliction** - when divine consolations are suspended and communion with God interrupted.

2. **Maintaining faith during trials**: "When we want the faith of assurance we must live by a faith of adherence...though he slay me, yet I trust in him."

3. **The importance of remembering God's past faithfulness**: Believers should take comfort from "the experiences which the saints in former ages had" and remember that God's covenant extends across generations.

4. **Early divine care as encouragement**: God's providential care from birth onward should strengthen our confidence in his continued faithfulness.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1** ("My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?"): Henry notes Christ quoted this on the cross, possibly reciting the entire psalm. Even in abandonment, Christ maintained his relationship with the Father as "his God."

**Verse 3** ("But thou art holy"): Henry emphasizes that despite feeling forsaken, the psalmist affirms God's holiness and notes that God "inhabitest the praises of Israel" - showing God's condescension to receive human worship.

**Verse 6** ("I am a worm, and no man"): Henry sees Christ's extreme humiliation, noting the word refers to "such a worm as was used in dyeing scarlet or purple," possibly alluding to his bloody sufferings.

**Verses 9-10**: Henry highlights God's care "from the womb" as evidence of lifelong divine providence, applicable both to believers generally and specifically to Christ's incarnation under divine protection.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry provides extensive scriptural connections:
- **1 Peter 1:11** for the prophetic nature of the psalm
- **Matthew 27:46** for Christ's quotation on the cross  
- **Hebrews 5:7** regarding Christ's "strong crying and tears"
- **Isaiah 53:10** on God's pleasure in bruising Christ
- **Romans 11:28** about covenant blessings for "the fathers' sake"
- **Hosea 11:1** connecting to Christ's childhood protection in Egypt

Henry concludes that when singing this psalm, believers "must keep our thoughts fixed upon Christ, and be so affected with his sufferings as to experience the fellowship of them, and so affected with his grace as to experience the power and influence of it."

Chapter 23
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 23: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**The Divine Shepherd Relationship**: Henry emphasizes God's covenant care for believers through the shepherd metaphor. He connects this to Christ as "the good shepherd" (John 10:11), noting that while Psalm 22 represents "Christ dying for his sheep," Psalm 23 shows "Christians receiving the benefit of all the care and tenderness of that great and good shepherd."

**Providence and Grace**: Henry sees the psalm addressing both God's providential blessings and "the communications of God's grace, received by a lively faith," which fill believers with "joy unspeakable."

**Three Comfortable Premises and Conclusions**: Henry structures the psalm around three inferences: (1) From God being shepherd, David concludes he "shall not want"; (2) From experiencing God's shepherding care, he "needs not fear any evil"; (3) From God's current bounty, he infers "constancy and perpetuity of his mercy."

## Key Practical Applications

**Believer's Response**: "If God be as a shepherd to us, we must be as sheep, inoffensive, meek, and quiet, silent before the shearers...we must know the shepherd's voice, and follow him."

**Contentment in God's Provision**: Henry teaches that abundance to the wicked is "but a dry pasture," but to the godly, even little becomes "a green pasture" because they "taste the goodness of God in all his enjoyments."

**Courage in Death**: Henry shows how believers can face mortality with "holy security and serenity," noting that "a child of God may meet the messengers of death...with a holy security."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The "I shall not want" promise means not just avoiding starvation, but having "whatever I need...and if I have not everything I desire, I may conclude it is either not fit for me or not good for me."

**Verse 4**: Henry's famous treatment of the "valley of the shadow of death" emphasizes four comforting aspects: it's only death's "shadow" (not substantial evil), it's a "valley" (fruitful), it's a gentle "walk" (not being chased), and it's "through" (leading somewhere better).

**Rod and Staff**: These represent God's "word and Spirit" that comfort dying saints—tools of divine guidance and protection.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple biblical passages:
- **Psalm 78:70-71** (David's shepherd background)
- **John 10:11** (Christ as good shepherd)  
- **Psalm 80:1** (Shepherd of Israel)
- **Isaiah 40:11** (God's care for individuals)
- **Leviticus 27:32** (sheep passing under the rod)
- **Psalm 110:2** (rod of Christ's strength)

Henry masterfully shows how this "psalm of comfort" provides believers with theological foundation for trusting God's care throughout life and into death, grounded in the intimate shepherd-sheep relationship.

Chapter 24
----------------------------------------
# Summary: Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 24

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 24 as fundamentally about "the kingdom of Jesus Christ," dividing it into Christ's providential kingdom (ruling the world) and the kingdom of grace (ruling His church). He suggests David wrote this psalm when bringing the ark to Jerusalem, intending "to lead the people above the pomp of external ceremonies to a holy life and faith in Christ, of whom the ark was a type."

The psalm moves from God's universal sovereignty over creation to the specific requirements for entering His presence, culminating in Christ as the "King of glory." Henry emphasizes that while God owns everything through providence, He seeks a special relationship with those who meet spiritual qualifications.

## Key Practical Applications

**Divine Ownership and Contentment**: Since "the earth is the Lord's and the fulness thereof," believers should find comfort knowing they never leave "their Father's ground" and should be content with their earthly portion rather than envying others.

**Heart Religion Over External Forms**: Henry stresses that true religion requires "heart-work" - it's insufficient to have "clean hands before men" without washing "our hearts from wickedness." Genuine faith demands both outward purity and inward sincerity.

**Seeking God in Community**: True believers "join themselves to the people of God, to seek God with them," incorporating themselves into the community of faith rather than pursuing isolated spirituality.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: God's ownership isn't limited to heaven - even "the worms of this earth are not below his cognizance." The earth's foundation "upon the seas" demonstrates both God's omnipotence and the instability of earthly things: "their foundation is not only sand, but water."

**Verses 3-6**: The question "Who shall ascend into the hill of the LORD?" parallels Psalm 15:1. Henry identifies four characteristics of God's people: clean hands (avoiding gross sin), pure hearts (inward integrity), not lifting souls to vanity (proper priorities), and honest dealing (truthfulness in all relationships).

**Verses 7-10**: The "King of glory" passage shows Christ seeking entrance, with "behold he stands at the door and knocks, ready to come in." The repetition emphasizes the urgency and importance of receiving Christ.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical texts:

- **1 Corinthians 10:26, 28**: Paul quotes verse 1 twice regarding food offered to idols
- **Exodus 19:5 and Psalm 50:12**: Supporting God's ownership despite not needing human service
- **Matthew 5:8**: Connecting pure hearts to seeing God
- **Psalm 15:1**: Paralleling the question about dwelling with God
- **Revelation 3:9 and Zechariah 8:23**: Describing how believers join God's people
- **2 Timothy 4:8**: The "crown of righteousness" as the ultimate blessing

Henry particularly emphasizes how the psalm points forward to Christ's universal dominion, where "the Father loveth the Son and hath given all things into his hand, power over all flesh."

The commentary skillfully weaves together themes of divine sovereignty, personal holiness, and Christological fulfillment, making ancient worship practices relevant to Christian spiritual life.

Chapter 25
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 25

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 25 as a comprehensive guide to prayer and faith, emphasizing the relationship between God's character and human response. Central themes include **divine guidance for sinners**, **God's covenant faithfulness**, and the **paradox of grace** - that God's greatness is magnified in forgiving great sins. Henry notes David's striking plea: "Pardon my iniquity, for it is great," explaining that "the greater it is the more will divine mercy be magnified in the forgiveness of it."

The commentary emphasizes God's dual nature as **"good and upright"** (v. 8), making Him both willing and faithful to teach sinners. Henry sees this psalm as demonstrating that **"all the paths of the Lord are mercy and truth"** (v. 10), showing God's consistent character in both promises and providences.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Prayer Structure**: The psalm teaches "what it is to pray" - lifting up the soul to God with "holy contempt of the world" and "active faith." Prayer is described as "the ascent of the soul to God."

**Trust and Dependence**: Believers should maintain confidence that **"none who, by a believing attendance, wait on God...shall be made ashamed of it."** Henry emphasizes that true faith involves both waiting on God and waiting for Him.

**Repentance**: Old sins require ongoing repentance - **"Our youthful faults and follies should be matter of our repentance and humiliation long after, because time does not wear out the guilt of sin."**

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: "Unto thee, O LORD, do I lift up my soul" - Henry connects this to Psalm 24's call against lifting souls to vanity, showing David's proper redirection of worship.

**Verses 4-5**: On divine instruction, Henry notes that even **"the most intelligent, the most observant, both need and desire to be taught of God."** He distinguishes between enlightening understanding and inclining the will to obedience.

**Verse 11**: Henry finds the plea "for it is great" remarkable, showing how **"the more we see of the heinousness of our sins the better qualified we are to find mercy with God."**

### Cross-References and Scriptural Connections

Henry weaves extensive biblical connections throughout, including:
- **Psalm 24** (lifting up souls/heads)
- **Psalm 119:30** (way of truth and precepts)
- **Exodus 34:7** (God's forgiveness of iniquity, transgression, and sin)
- **Isaiah 43:25** (God's self-motivated forgiveness)
- **Ecclesiastes 2:3** (understanding duty and good works)
