The commentary consistently demonstrates how this psalm anticipates **Christ as "both the way and the truth,"** connecting Old Testament petition with New Testament fulfillment. Henry emphasizes the **"communion of saints"** through mutual prayer and shared faith in God's promises.

Chapter 26
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 26: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 26 as David's solemn self-examination before God and his own conscience regarding his integrity. The central theological theme is **justified self-vindication** - distinguishing between appeals to God's mercy for sin versus appeals to God's justice against false accusations. Henry notes that while David cannot justify himself "against the charge of sin," he can rightfully "justify himself against the charge of hypocrisy."

A significant interpretive insight is Henry's suggestion that David serves as **a type of Christ**, with the psalm's declarations of "spotless innocence" being "fully and eminently true" of Christ alone. This typological reading adds messianic depth to David's protests of integrity.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living:

**Regarding Companions**: "Great care to avoid bad company is both a good evidence of our integrity and a good means to preserve us in it." He emphasizes avoiding not just wicked persons, but even "vain persons" devoted to frivolity, as their influence can be subtly corrupting.

**Regarding Worship Preparation**: David's declaration "I will wash mine hands in innocency" teaches that proper preparation for worship requires both habitual purity ("carefully watch against all sin") and actual preparation through repentance and fresh application of Christ's blood.

**Regarding False Accusations**: Henry comforts believers that "there is a righteous God, who, sooner or later, will clear up their innocency."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry explains David's bold appeal to divine judgment as possible because "he can justify himself against the charge of hypocrisy" while still acknowledging his need for mercy regarding actual sins.

**Verse 3**: "Thy lovingkindness is before mine eyes" demonstrates integrity when our religious actions flow "from a principle of love to God" and "grateful sense of God's goodness."

**Verses 4-5**: Henry provides detailed analysis of David's separation from evil company, noting he avoided not just fellowship but even casual association, declaring "I have hated the congregation of evil doers."

**Verses 6-8**: The "washing hands in innocency" represents both ceremonial preparation (alluding to priestly washing) and spiritual cleansing through repentance.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to numerous biblical passages:
- **Psalm 143:2** and **Psalm 25:7** (appeals to mercy vs. justice)
- **1 Corinthians 11:28** ("Let a man examine himself")
- **Psalm 24:3-4** (clean hands and pure heart)
- **Hebrews 13:10** (Christ as our altar)
- **Acts 2:40** (saving ourselves from "this untoward generation")

## Memorable Phrases and Quotes

Henry offers several memorable formulations:
- "Innocency itself is no fence to the name, though it is to the bosom, against the darts of calumny"
- "He wished he had a window in his bosom, that whoever would might look into his heart"
- "Great care to avoid bad company is both a good evidence of our integrity and a good means to preserve us in it"

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches believers what they "must be and do that we may have the favour of God, and comfort in our own consciences," while ultimately pointing to our completeness in Christ.

Chapter 27
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 27: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 27 as expressing "the pious and devout affections with which gracious souls are carried out towards God at all times, especially in times of trouble." The psalm demonstrates the **courage and holy bravery of faith**, showing how believers can triumph over enemies through divine confidence.

Central to Henry's theology here is God's threefold nature as **light, salvation, and strength**. As he explains: "God is a light to his people, to show them the way when they are in doubt, to comfort and rejoice their hearts when they are in sorrow." God serves not only as protector but as "the strength of my frail weak life, who keeps me from fainting, sinking, and dying away."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Fearless Faith**: "If God be for him, who can be against him?" Henry emphasizes that believers should live with "undaunted courage" because "if Omnipotence be his guard, he has no cause to fear."

**Priority of Worship**: David's "one thing" desire teaches that **communion with God should be our supreme priority**. Henry notes: "If he were to ask but one thing of God, this should be it; for this he had at heart more than any thing."

**Patient Waiting**: The psalm concludes with practical encouragement: "Wait on the LORD: be of good courage." Henry interprets this as both personal comfort and instruction for others facing trials.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 4**: Henry sees David's desire to "dwell in the house of the LORD" as seeking two things: "(1) That he might have the pleasure of meditating upon God" and "(2) That he might have the satisfaction of being instructed in his duty."

**Verse 8**: Henry emphasizes the divine invitation to prayer: "My heart said unto thee...Thy face, LORD, will I seek." He notes this shows "the true nature of religious worship; it is seeking the face of God."

**Verse 10**: The famous passage about parents forsaking becomes, for Henry, assurance that "when all other helps and succours fail, in God we may find more than supply."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry enriches his commentary with numerous biblical connections:
- **2 Samuel 21:17** - David called "the light of Israel"
- **John 18:6** - Christ's enemies falling backward
- **Psalm 84:4** - "Blessed are those that dwell in thy house"
- **2 Corinthians 2:14** - "Thanks be to God, who always causeth us to triumph"
- **Proverbs 18:10** - "The name of the LORD is a strong tower"

Henry particularly connects this psalm to Christ, noting that like Mary choosing "the one thing needful" at Jesus' feet, David prioritizes communion with God above all earthly concerns.

The commentary presents Psalm 27 as a masterclass in faith-based confidence, showing how believers can maintain joy and security even amid life's greatest trials through trust in God's unchanging character.

Chapter 28
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 28: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry structures this psalm around the contrast between **the saint militant** (verses 1-5) and **the saint triumphant** (verses 6-9). The commentary explores whether David wrote this during distress while preparing to praise God by faith, or during triumph while remembering past prayers. This reflects the theological principle that believers can simultaneously experience struggle and victory through faith.

The psalm demonstrates **God's justice and mercy**, showing how divine judgment falls upon the wicked while deliverance comes to the faithful. Henry emphasizes that God's silence represents the ultimate terror for believers, while His response brings life and hope.

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer and Faith**: Henry highlights that "those that pray in faith may rejoice in hope" and "what we win by prayer we must wear by praise." He shows how genuine prayer moves from desperate petition to confident thanksgiving.

**Separation from Evil**: The commentary emphasizes avoiding entanglement with "workers of iniquity" who "speak peace to their neighbours, but mischief is in their hearts." Henry draws three practical applications: avoiding their snares, refusing to imitate their sins, and escaping their ultimate doom.

**Trust in God's Strength**: Henry notes that "the very actings of faith are present aids to a dropping spirit," encouraging believers that trusting God provides immediate help even before deliverance comes.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: "O LORD my rock" demonstrates both God's power and the believer's dependence. Henry explains that being "silent" means God appearing angry at prayers, making the believer "like them that go down into the pit."

**Verse 2**: "Lifting hands toward thy holy oracle" points to Christ as the antitype of the mercy seat, showing that "through him all good comes from God to us."

**Verses 4-5**: The imprecation against the wicked isn't vengeful but prophetic, expressing "zeal for the honour of God's justice." Henry notes that God judges both actual deeds and "the wickedness of their endeavours."

**Verse 7**: The progression from trust to help to joy to praise shows the complete cycle of faith's experience.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively cross-references Scripture throughout. Key connections include:

- **Psalm 80:4** (God being silent to prayers)
- **Zechariah 1:13** (hearing "good words and comfortable words")  
- **Numbers 7:89** (the oracle as God's dwelling place)
- **Hebrews 9:24** (the holy place as figure of heaven)
- **Revelation 18:4** (not partaking in sinners' plagues)
- **1 Corinthians 1:2** (communion with all saints)

Henry particularly emphasizes how the "holy oracle" prefigures both Christ and heaven, making this psalm's prayer life a model for New Testament believers who approach God through Christ as mediator.

The commentary masterfully weaves together personal devotion, theological insight, and practical application while maintaining Henry's characteristic pastoral warmth and scriptural depth.

Chapter 29
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 29

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 29 as David's response to a powerful thunderstorm, using nature's display as a springboard for theological reflection. The central theme is **God's supreme majesty and sovereignty** revealed through natural phenomena. Henry emphasizes three key domains of God's rule: the kingdom of nature (through thunder and storms), the kingdom of providence (His eternal governance), and the kingdom of grace (His special favor to His people).

A notable theological emphasis is Henry's observation that the name "Jehovah" appears **eighteen times** in this short psalm, highlighting God's self-sufficiency and eternal nature. He sees this as deliberate emphasis on God's "I AM that I AM" identity.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For Leaders and Authority Figures**: The repeated call to "give unto the LORD" (verses 1-2) specifically targets "the mighty" - princes and those in power. Henry argues they must acknowledge that their authority comes from God and should be used for His honor: "Give him your crowns; let them be laid at his feet."

**For Worship**: Henry defines worship as "giving to the Lord the glory due to his name" and emphasizes worshipping "in the beauty of holiness" - meaning worship should be characterized by purity, devotion to God's will, and proper reverence.

**For Daily Life**: Henry encourages believers to use natural phenomena as worship prompts: "Whenever it thunders let us think of this psalm" and use God's works in nature to "give unto him the glory due unto his name."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes the threefold repetition of "Give unto the LORD" suggests that "mighty men are backward to this duty" but emphasizes the importance of powerful people submitting to God.

**Verses 3-9**: Henry sees "the voice of the LORD" as literal thunder, describing its effects: breaking cedars, shaking wilderness, causing animals to react in fear. He connects this to God's historical speaking through thunder at Mount Sinai.

**Verse 10**: The phrase "sits upon the flood" Henry interprets both as God's control over Noah's flood specifically and His general sovereignty over all chaos and opposition.

**Verse 11**: Henry contrasts God's terrifying voice in thunder with His gracious provision of "strength" and "peace" to His people.

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Revelation 21:24** - kings bringing glory into the New Jerusalem
- **Revelation 14:6-7** - the everlasting gospel's call to worship
- **2 Chronicles 20:21** - "beauty of holiness"
- **Job 37:2** - thunder as "the noise of his voice"
- **Psalm 93:3-4** - opposition compared to floods
- **Genesis 9:11, Isaiah 54:9** - God's covenant after Noah's flood
- **Psalm 103:19** - God's throne prepared in heaven

Henry concludes by noting that while God's voice in thunder is "dreadful," believers should "bless him that there is another voice of his besides this dreadful one...the still small voice of his gospel, the terror of which shall not make us afraid." This connects the psalm's natural theology to the gentler revelation found in the Gospel.

Chapter 30
----------------------------------------
# Summary: Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 30

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 30 as a thanksgiving psalm for divine deliverance, likely composed upon David's recovery from serious illness during the dedication of his house. The commentary emphasizes **God's sovereignty over human circumstances** and the **transformative nature of divine mercy**. 

A central theological theme is the **temporary nature of God's discipline versus the permanence of His favor**. Henry highlights verse 5: "his anger endureth but a moment; in his favour is life: weeping may endure for a night, but joy cometh in the morning." This illustrates God's covenant faithfulness—while discipline may come, restoration follows for the repentant.

Henry also emphasizes **God's holiness as His memorial attribute** (v. 4), noting that "none of all God's perfections carries in it more terror to the wicked, nor more comfort to the godly, than his holiness."

## Key Practical Applications

**House Dedication**: Henry advocates dedicating new homes to God as "little sanctuaries," committing families and affairs to God's guidance, establishing family worship, and resolving to "put away iniquity far from our tabernacles."

**Prosperity and Security**: Henry warns against overconfidence during good times, noting our tendency to think "when things are well with us, that they will always be so." He counsels believers to "rejoice in our prosperity as though we rejoiced not, because the fashion of it passes away."

**Prayer in Affliction**: When troubles come, Henry emphasizes prayer as "a salve for every sore," encouraging persistent prayer even when God seems distant: "If God, in wisdom and justice, turn from us, yet it will be in us the greatest folly and injustice imaginable if we turn from him."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry notes three aspects magnifying David's deliverance: defeat of enemies, answered prayer, and preservation of life from imminent death. He emphasizes that "a life from the dead ought to be spent in extolling the God of our life."

**Verses 6-7**: Henry observes David's honest self-reflection on his former security, noting how prosperity can breed false confidence. David attributed his stability to God's favor, yet still fell into presumption.

**Verses 8-10**: Henry examines David's prayer strategy, particularly his appeal that God gains nothing from his death and loses praise: "Shall the dust praise thee?" Henry notes "the best pleas in prayer are those that are taken from God's honour."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Deuteronomy 20:5** - regarding house dedication practices
- **2 Samuel 5:11** - David's house construction
- **Exodus 15:26** - "I am the Lord that healeth thee"
- **Isaiah 6:3; Revelation 4:8** - angelic worship of God's holiness
- **Isaiah 54:7-8** - God's momentary wrath versus everlasting kindness
- **Philippians 2:17** - willingness to die in service
- **Hebrews 4:16** - approaching the throne of grace

Henry concludes by encouraging believers to "remember with thankfulness any like deliverances wrought for us" and allow such experiences to engage us "to depend upon him." The psalm serves as both personal testimony and communal encouragement for trusting God through life's inevitable changes.

Chapter 31
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 31: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies this as David's psalm during Saul's persecution, presenting **trust in God's sovereignty amid suffering** as the central theme. The commentary reveals a threefold structure: confident prayer for deliverance (vv. 1-8), sorrowful complaints with continued trust (vv. 9-18), and triumphant praise (vv. 19-24).

The psalm demonstrates that **"Faith and prayer must go together"** - believers must pray with faith and believe while praying, as "the prayer of faith is the prevailing prayer." Henry emphasizes God's dual role as both merciful deliverer and righteous judge who vindicates the innocent against unrighteous persecutors.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Spiritual Priority in Crisis**: "Our outward afflictions should increase our concern for our souls." When external troubles multiply, believers should focus more on their spiritual condition, ensuring "though the outward man perish, the inward man may suffer no damage."

**Complete Surrender**: Regarding verse 5's "Into thy hands I commit my spirit," Henry teaches this represents total consecration - committing our spirits "to be sanctified by his grace, devoted to his honour, employed in his service, and fitted for his kingdom."

**Rejection of False Securities**: Believers must hate "lying vanities" - whether idols, superstitions, or worldly securities - and "trust in God only, and not in any creature."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 5** receives special attention as Christ's words on the cross, showing how David's experience typologically points to Christ's ultimate trust in the Father. Henry notes this teaches believers to follow Stephen's example in committing spirits to Christ at death.

**Verses 9-10** reveal how deep grief affects both soul and body. Henry observes that even David's naturally "cheerful and firm" disposition was broken, warning the "airy and gay" not to "set sorrow at defiance."

**Verse 15's** "My times are in thy hand" emphasizes divine sovereignty over all circumstances and timing.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to Christ's passion, particularly verse 5 with Luke 23:46. He references **Proverbs 18:10** regarding God as our refuge, **2 Corinthians 4:16** about the inner man, and **Luke 21:19** about possessing our souls through patience.

The commentary links David's experiences to **1 Samuel 23** (Keilah incident) and connects the psalm's redemption language to **Genesis 48:16** and **2 Timothy 1:12**, showing how Old Testament redemptions typified Christ's work.

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches believers to find joy in hope that "counterbalances their grievances and silences their griefs," demonstrating how present experiences of God's faithfulness encourage future trust.

Chapter 32
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 32: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Gospel Grace and Justification**: Henry emphasizes this psalm's profound gospel content, noting Paul's reference to it in Romans 4:6 regarding "the blessedness of the man unto whom God imputes righteousness without works." The commentary centers on **the nature of divine forgiveness**, described through three key aspects: transgression being "lifted off" like a heavy burden, sin being "covered" with Christ's righteousness (similar to God clothing Adam and Eve), and iniquity "not being imputed" due to Christ bearing our sins.

**True Blessedness**: Henry argues that genuine happiness consists in God's favor and spiritual blessings, not worldly wealth. The forgiveness of sin becomes "that article of the covenant which is the reason and ground of all the rest."

## Key Practical Applications

**The Necessity of Sincere Confession**: Henry stresses that those seeking pardon must "take shame to themselves by a penitent confession." This involves confessing the fact, fault, and deserved punishment of sin with "shame and holy blushing, with fear and holy trembling."

**The Danger of Spiritual Silence**: Drawing from verses 3-4, Henry warns against those who "stifle their convictions" and "choose rather to pine away in their iniquities than to take the method which God has appointed." He notes: **"He that covers his sin shall not prosper; some inward trouble is required in repentance, but there is much worse in impenitency."**

**Prayer and Divine Guidance**: The commentary emphasizes that "all godly people are praying people" and encourages seeking God "in a time when he will be found."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry explains that the "blessed man" whose sins are forgiven must have "no guile" in his spirit—meaning sincerity rather than sinlessness: "not repent with a purpose to sin again, and then sin with a purpose to repent again."

**Verses 3-4**: Henry powerfully describes the torment of unconfessed sin: David's "smothered convictions will be a fire in their bones, and the wounds of sin, not opened, will fester."

**Verse 5**: Demonstrates God's immediate readiness to forgive: **"I did but say, I will confess, and thou forgavest."** Henry compares this to the prodigal son's father who "saw his returning son when he was yet afar off."

**Verse 7**: God as our "hiding-place" provides both safety from trouble and joy through "songs of deliverance."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: Romans 4:6 (justification), Matthew 11:28 (rest for the weary), Revelation 3:18 (covering nakedness), Hebrews 8:12 (covenant mercy), Acts 9:11 (Paul's conversion and prayer), Luke 22:32 (strengthening brethren), 1 John 5:18 (protection from evil), and 2 Corinthians 6:2 ("now is the accepted time").

The commentary concludes with David's transformation from recipient of grace to instructor of others, emphasizing the believer's responsibility to "strengthen his brethren" after experiencing God's forgiveness.

Chapter 33
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 33: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Henry presents Psalm 33 as a comprehensive call to praise God, emphasizing four central themes: **God's perfect character** (justice, goodness, and truth), **His almighty creative power**, **His sovereign providence over all nations**, and **His special care for His chosen people**. The psalm demonstrates that "The counsel of the LORD standeth for ever" while human plans are brought "to nought," establishing God's absolute sovereignty over history.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Worship and Praise**: He emphasizes that "praise is comely for the upright" and should be done "skilfully and with a warm heart." Believers should "sing unto him a new song" - fresh expressions of gratitude for "new mercies and upon every new occasion."

**Trusting God's Providence**: Since God "over-rules all the counsels of men" and His eternal counsel is never frustrated, believers can find peace knowing "God governs the world." This should make us "easy...at all times."

**Proper Fear and Reverence**: The commentary calls for holy fear, noting "it is dangerous being at war with him who has the host of heaven for his armies and the depths of the sea for his magazines."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry sees these as establishing the foundation of praise through "holy joy," which is "the heart and soul of praise" and should employ our best efforts - "the best both of head and heart."

**Verses 6-9**: On creation, Henry notes how "easily" God created through His Word and Spirit, observing that "with men saying and doing are two things, but it is not so with God." The same power that spoke the world into existence continues to sustain it.

**Verses 13-15**: Henry emphasizes God's intimate knowledge of humanity: "He looks narrowly upon them...not the least thought can escape his observation." God "fashions their hearts alike" as both creator and ongoing sustainer.

**Verses 16-19**: Military might proves futile against divine will - "a horse is a vain thing for safety" - while God's eye rests protectively "upon them that fear him."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: He references **Proverbs 26:7** regarding hypocritical praise, **Daniel 10:21** on God's "scriptures of truth," **Ecclesiastes 3:14** on the permanence of God's works, and **Psalm 119:91** on creation's continued obedience to divine ordinance. He connects creation themes to **Revelation 14:6-7** and God's knowledge of hearts to **Psalm 139**.

The commentary presents this psalm as naturally expressing "the pious affections of a devout soul towards God," offering both theological depth and practical guidance for worship and trust in divine providence.

Chapter 34
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 34: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 34 as both thanksgiving for God's deliverance and universal instruction for believers. The psalm demonstrates God's **faithfulness in answering prayer** and His **sovereign protection of the righteous**. Henry emphasizes that while David's dissimulation before Abimelech was morally questionable, God's mercy transcended David's fault—"we must in every thing give thanks." 

The commentary highlights God's **accessibility to all social classes**, noting that "this poor man cried, and the Lord heard him" (v. 6), proving divine favor isn't reserved for the prominent. Henry presents the **doctrine of angelic protection**, explaining that angels "encampeth round about those that fear him" as spiritual guardians against evil forces.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Constant Praise**: Believers should maintain perpetual gratitude, praising God "at all times, upon all occasions" rather than only during crises.

**Experiential Faith**: The famous exhortation "O taste and see that the Lord is good" calls for personal experience of God's goodness, not merely intellectual acknowledgment.

**Practical Righteousness**: Henry outlines specific moral duties: "Keep thy tongue from evil," "depart from evil, and do good; seek peace, and pursue it." He emphasizes that godly living involves both **avoiding sin and actively pursuing righteousness**.

**Trust Over Self-Reliance**: Those who "live by faith in God's all-sufficiency want nothing," contrasting with "young lions" who "trust to themselves" yet suffer hunger.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry observes the psalm's **alphabetical structure**, noting David's remarkable composure under pressure. On verse 8's "taste and see," he explains this involves both **discovering and delighting** in God's goodness—"relish the goodness of God in all his gifts."

Regarding verse 18, Henry emphasizes God's special care for "them that are of a broken heart," showing divine nearness to the spiritually humble. On verse 20's "not one of them is broken," he sees both literal protection and spiritual preservation.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to broader biblical themes:

- References **1 Peter 2:3** for "taste and see" 
- Links verse 17 to **Psalm 102:17**: "God will regard the prayer of the destitute"
- Connects angelic protection to **Genesis 32:1** (Jacob) and **2 Kings 6:17** (Elisha)
- Cites **Hosea 3:5** on fearing God's goodness
- References **Isaiah 57:15** regarding God's care for the humble

The commentary consistently demonstrates how individual psalms reflect universal biblical principles about God's character, human responsibility, and the contrast between righteous and wicked destinies. Henry concludes that "in singing this psalm, we are both to give glory to God and to teach and admonish ourselves and one another."

Chapter 35
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalms 35: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies this as David's appeal against Saul and his persecutors, but emphasizes its broader typological significance. The psalm represents **the eternal conflict between righteousness and evil**, with David prefiguring Christ and His kingdom. Henry warns against applying this psalm to "little peevish quarrels," instead directing readers to see it as addressing "the public enemies of Christ and his kingdom." The central theme is **God as the righteous Judge** who vindicates the innocent and defeats those who oppose His purposes.

The commentary presents **divine justice as both protective and retributive** - God shields the righteous while ensuring that the wicked fall into their own snares. Henry emphasizes that persecution of the godly is "no new thing" but rather "a fruit of the old enmity in the seed of the serpent against the seed of the woman."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing opposition:

**For Personal Conflicts**: Christians should "take comfort with the testimony of our consciences concerning our innocency" and trust that "God will, in his own way and time, right us." However, believers must not harbor "uncharitable revengeful resentments" but follow Christ's example of forgiving enemies.

**For Spiritual Warfare**: The psalm teaches believers to "resent the indignities done to Christ's honour" and "pray to God to plead the just and injured cause of Christianity and serious godliness."

**For Trust in God's Timing**: Henry emphasizes patience, noting that "those whose souls are sorrowful in the Lord...need not question but that in due time their souls shall be joyful in the Lord."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry presents David's appeal as a model for approaching God when human authorities fail. When "his prince wronged him, he appealed to his God, who is prince and Judge of the kings of the earth."

**Verse 3**: The prayer "say unto my soul, I am thy salvation" represents the believer's need for "inward comfort under outward troubles." Henry notes: "If God, by his Spirit, witness to our spirits that he is our salvation, we have enough."

**Verses 4-8**: Henry interprets David's imprecatory prayers not as personal revenge but as prophetic declarations of divine justice. The phrase "let his net that he hath hid catch himself" was "remarkably fulfilled in the ruin of Saul" who fell to the Philistines by whom he had planned David's destruction.

**Verse 10**: "All my bones shall say, LORD, who is like unto thee?" Henry sees this as praising God "with the whole man" and acknowledging Him as "a non-such" - without equal in defending "oppressed innocency" and punishing "triumphant tyranny."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to broader biblical themes through several references:

- **Romans 11:9-10** - linking David's predictions to the enemies of Christ's kingdom
- **1 Samuel 18:25 and 26:10** - showing prophetic fulfillment in Saul's destruction
- **Exodus 15:3** - establishing God as "a man of war"
- **Psalm 1:4** - comparing the wicked to "chaff before the wind"
- **Deuteronomy 32:35** - referencing divine retribution timing

Henry also alludes to Christ's teaching on forgiveness and connects the psalm to the larger biblical narrative of conflict between good and evil, making it relevant for understanding spiritual warfare in the Christian life.

Chapter 36
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 36 Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry structures this psalm around a stark contrast between **the depravity of sin and the goodness of God**. He identifies the psalm's central theological tension: while the wicked reveal humanity's fallen nature through their contempt of God, God's character remains unchangingly perfect. The commentary emphasizes that **"all the wickedness of the wicked"** stems from a fundamental **"want of due regard"** for God, making the fear of God the cornerstone of moral behavior.

The theological climax appears in Henry's treatment of God's attributes as **transcendent and immutable**—His mercy "in the heavens," faithfulness "unto the clouds," righteousness "like great mountains," and judgments as "a great deep." This emphasizes God's **sovereign perfection** against human corruption.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several pointed applications:

**On Self-Deception**: He warns that **"sinners are self-destroyers by being self-flatterers"** and that **"Satan could not deceive them if they did not deceive themselves."** This highlights the danger of spiritual self-deception.

**On Moral Progression**: Henry notes the downward spiral of sin: **"Those that leave off to do good leave off to be wise"** and **"omissions make way for commissions."** He warns that abandoning good practices opens the door to active evil.

**On God's Providence**: Even amid wickedness, believers should **"never think the worse of God nor of his government"** but instead **"admire his patience."** This calls for trust in God's ultimate justice despite present circumstances.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry sees the **"transgression of the wicked"** as evidence that reveals their heart condition—they have **"no fear of God before his eyes."** The self-flattery described becomes a willful self-deception where sinners **"call evil good and good evil."**

**Verses 3-4**: The progression from corrupt speech to devising **"mischief upon his bed"** shows deliberate, premeditated evil. Henry emphasizes that plotting evil during times meant for meditation reveals **"the sinner's heart fully set in him to do evil."**

**Verses 5-6**: God's attributes are presented in cosmic terms—mercy in the heavens, faithfulness reaching the clouds, righteousness like mountains, and judgments as deep waters. This reveals both God's **transcendence and His comprehensive care**.

**Verses 7-9**: The **"fountain of life"** and **"river of thy pleasures"** represent the abundant spiritual satisfaction found in God, contrasting sharply with the emptiness of wickedness.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Job 20:13-14** on sin's temporary sweetness becoming **"gall of asps"**
- **Hosea 4:1** and **Isaiah 5:7** on the absence of truth and justice among men
- **Isaiah 55:8-9** and **Hosea 11:9** on God's thoughts being higher than human thoughts
- **Psalm 97:2** on **"clouds and darkness"** surrounding God while **"judgment and justice are the habitation of his throne"**
- **Micah 2:1** on devising wickedness upon one's bed

The commentary demonstrates how this psalm connects to broader biblical themes of human depravity, divine perfection, and God's faithful preservation of His people despite surrounding wickedness.

Chapter 37
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 37: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 37 as a **"teaching psalm" (Maschil)** rather than devotional poetry, focusing on instruction for daily living. The central theological challenge addressed is reconciling **God's temporal promises in the Law of Moses** with the reality that "sinners prosper and saints suffer adversity." Henry emphasizes three core theological principles:

**Divine Justice**: Though delayed, God's judgment is certain. The wicked "are ripening apace for ruin" while the righteous are "sure of salvation and protection."

**Providence and Patience**: Believers must trust God's timing and methods, understanding that **"how black soever things may look for the present, it shall be well with those that fear God."**

**Eternal vs. Temporal Perspective**: The prosperity of the wicked is temporary **"like grass and herbs"** while the righteous have an **"inheritance that shall be forever."**

## Key Practical Applications

Henry provides specific remedies for spiritual discontent:

**Trust and Action**: **"We must not think to trust in God and then live as we list... we must both trust in the Lord and do good"** (v. 3).

**Spiritual Contentment**: **"Delight thyself in the LORD; and he shall give thee the desires of thine heart"** (v. 4) - focusing on sanctified desires rather than worldly appetites.

**Surrender of Control**: **"Commit thy way unto the LORD; roll thy way upon the Lord... We must follow Providence, and not force it"** (vv. 5-6).

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry warns against both fretting at God's perceived unfairness and envying the wicked's freedom from moral restraints.

**Verse 6**: **"He shall bring forth thy righteousness as the light"** - God will vindicate the righteous whose reputations are clouded by circumstances or false accusations.

**Verses 16-17**: **"A little that a righteous man hath is better than the riches of many wicked"** demonstrates divine priorities and true prosperity.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to broader biblical themes:
- **Psalm 1:3** (righteous flourishing like fruitful trees vs. grass)
- **Matthew 6:25** (not being anxious about future events)
- **1 Peter 5:7** (casting burdens upon the Lord)
- **Matthew 13:43** (final vindication of the righteous)

The commentary concludes with the assurance that **"if we take care to keep a good conscience, we may leave it to God to take care of our good name,"** encapsulating the psalm's call to faithful patience amid life's apparent injustices.

Chapter 38
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 38: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

This penitential psalm reveals the profound connection between sin, divine discipline, and genuine repentance. Henry emphasizes that David's physical afflictions stem directly from God's displeasure over his sins, likely relating to the Uriah incident. The psalm demonstrates how **"sin is a burden"** - both its power dwelling within us and its guilt pressing upon us. Henry notes that sin becomes either "a burden of repentance" leading to salvation or "a burden of ruin" leading to condemnation.

A key theological insight is the distinction between divine correction and divine wrath. Henry stresses that believers must pray against experiencing God's displeasure more than any outward suffering, seeking to "bear any outward affliction while it comes from, and consists with, the love of God."

## Key Practical Applications

**For Times of Sickness:** Henry teaches that "times of sickness are times to bring to remembrance" - periods for examining our sins and allowing God to humble us. Believers should use illness as opportunity for spiritual inventory and renewed consecration.

**For Dealing with Fair-Weather Friends:** When David's companions "stand aloof from my sore," Henry counsels that "adversity tries friendship, and separates between the precious and the vile." This teaches us to "make sure a friend in heaven" rather than depending solely on human relationships.

**For Handling Criticism:** David's response of remaining silent before his accusers demonstrates wisdom in not defending ourselves when under divine discipline, instead waiting for God's vindication.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2:** Henry explains that God's arrows "stick fast" representing the terrors of conscience that penetrate deeply. Unlike temporary troubles, spiritual conviction remains "till he is pleased to draw them out and bind up with his comforts the wound he has made."

**Verse 4:** Sin as drowning waters that have "gone over my head" illustrates how guilt can overwhelm completely, pressing down "more than I am able to bear."

**Verse 9:** The comfort that "all my desire is before thee" shows how God knows both "the burdens I groan under and the blessings I groan after."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to other biblical passages:
- **Psalm 6:1** - Same opening petition about God's wrath
- **Job 7:4** - Parallel description of divine arrows/terrors  
- **Romans 7:24** - Sin as "body of death" we carry
- **Hebrews 12:1** - Sin as weight hindering spiritual progress
- **Romans 8:26-27** - Unutterable groanings known to God

Henry also draws typological connections, seeing David's sufferings as prefiguring Christ's agony, particularly His abandonment by friends and the cry "My soul is exceedingly sorrowful."

The psalm concludes with urgent prayer for God's presence and help, teaching that even in deepest distress, believers must cast their burdens upon the Lord who searches the heart and understands our deepest needs.

Chapter 39
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 39: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 39 as "a funeral psalm" dealing with the **frailty and mortality of human life**. The central theological theme is the **vanity of earthly existence** - Henry emphasizes that "every man at his best state is altogether vanity." He interprets David's struggle as representing the universal human tension between **grace and corruption, passion and patience** when facing life's trials.

The commentary stresses God's **sovereignty over human life**, noting that "the number of our months is with him" and that our days are "determined" and "measured in the counsel of God." Henry views suffering as potentially sanctifying, aimed at getting "our hearts reconciled to the holy will of God."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Self-Control and Speech**: He emphasizes the importance of **bridling the tongue**, especially "when the wicked is before me." Christians must "take heed to our ways" and make "solemn vows against sin."

**Proper Response to Affliction**: Rather than fretting, believers should use suffering for "spiritual benefit." Henry warns against dwelling on troubles, noting that "while they suffer their thoughts to dwell upon the causes of the calamity, the fire of their discontent is fed with fuel."

**Mortality Meditation**: Christians should regularly contemplate death's certainty and nearness, praying for wisdom to "consider it and improve what I know concerning it." This prepares us to "do what our hand finds to do...with all possible expedition."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry sees David's internal struggle between maintaining silence and growing inner turmoil. He notes that "watchfulness and resolution, in the strength of God's grace" can bridle the tongue, though keeping "silence even from good" was David's weakness.

**Verse 4**: David's prayer to "know my end" isn't seeking prophetic knowledge but wisdom to contemplate mortality. Henry clarifies this doesn't mean knowing when we'll die, but gaining grace to consider death seriously.

**Verse 5**: The "hand-breadth" metaphor emphasizes life's brevity - "We need no rod, no pole, no measuring line...we have the standard of them at our fingers' end."

**Verse 6**: Henry identifies three vanities: "our joys and honours" (walking "in a vain show"), "our griefs and fears" ("disquieted in vain"), and material pursuits ("heapeth up riches").

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout, including:
- **Jeremiah 2:20** on remembering vows against sin
- **Ephesians 4:29** on proper speech
- **Ecclesiastes 9:5** and **7:2** on mortality's universality  
- **Job 14:5** on life's predetermined measure
- **James 4:14** comparing life to vapor
- **Deuteronomy 32:29** on considering our latter end

Henry concludes that this psalm serves those "on whose comforts God has, by death, made breaches," helping them find sanctification through loss and reconciliation to God's will.

Chapter 40
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 40: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 40 as both David's personal testimony of deliverance and a profound messianic prophecy. The psalm operates on dual levels - David's experience of rescue from "some great and pressing trouble" (likely spiritual distress from sin) serves as a type pointing to Christ's redemptive work. Henry emphasizes that "the same Spirit that indited his praises for that deliverance was in him, at the same time, a Spirit of prophecy, testifying of the sufferings of Christ and the glory that should follow."

The central theological theme contrasts the insufficiency of Old Testament sacrifices with Christ's perfect offering. Henry notes that legal sacrifices "could not take away the guilt of sin by satisfying God's justice" since "the life of a sheep...could not pretend to be an equivalent" to human life. These sacrifices were merely "shadows of good things to come" pointing to "the substance...which is Christ."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Patient Waiting on God**: He emphasizes that "those whose expectation is from God may wait with assurance, but must wait with patience." The phrase "waiting, I waited" demonstrates that relief may not come quickly, but persistent faith is required.

**Trust Versus Self-Reliance**: Henry warns against trusting "the proud" who encourage self-dependence, stating "Blessed is the man that makes the Lord his trust" rather than turning "aside to lies" by departing from God.

**Testimony's Power**: Personal experiences of God's deliverance should encourage others, as "David's experience would be an encouragement to many to hope in God."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: The "horrible pit" and "miry clay" represent spiritual despair and despondency. Henry applies this to Christ's agony, noting how Jesus "kept hold of his relation to his Father" even crying "My God, my God."

**Verses 6-8**: These verses receive extensive treatment as messianic prophecy. The phrase "mine ears hast thou opened" alludes to the Hebrew custom of binding servants permanently by boring their ear, suggesting Christ's voluntary, eternal commitment to redemption.

**Verse 5**: God's "wonderful works" are described as products of "his thoughts to us-ward" - not sudden decisions but "the projects of infinite wisdom, the designs of everlasting love."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to the New Testament, particularly **Hebrews 10:5** which quotes verses 6-7 directly regarding Christ's sacrifice. He references **Romans 4:25; 5:1-2** concerning Christ's death and resurrection as encouragement for faith. Other connections include **Hosea 6:1** on God's healing, **Hebrews 5:7** on Christ being "heard in that he feared," and **Ephesians 1:11; 3:11** regarding God's eternal purposes.

The commentary demonstrates Henry's typological interpretation, seeing David's experiences as foreshadowing Christ's work while maintaining practical application for contemporary believers seeking deliverance and learning to trust God's timing and methods.

Chapter 41
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary Summary: Psalm 41

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 41 as a profound meditation on divine faithfulness amid human treachery. The central theme contrasts "God's kindness and truth" with "man's unkindness and treachery," showing how David found his enemies "very barbarous, but his God very gracious" during illness. 

Henry interprets the psalm as demonstrating God's covenantal care for those who show mercy to others, particularly the poor and afflicted. He emphasizes the reciprocal nature of divine mercy: "Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy." The commentary presents suffering as an opportunity for spiritual refinement, where God "will strengthen them, and make them easy, under their bodily pains and sicknesses."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For the afflicted:** "Is any afflicted with sickness? let him sing the beginning of this psalm. Is any persecuted by enemies? let him sing the latter end of it."

**Regarding charity:** Considering the poor involves both material help and compassionate understanding - we must "take notice of their affliction and enquire into their state, must sympathize with them and judge charitably concerning them."

**During illness:** God promises not necessarily healing, but strength to endure: "He has not promised that they shall never be sick...but he has promised to enable them to bear their affliction with patience."

**Prayer accountability:** "It is good for us to keep some account of our prayers, that we may not unsay, in our practices, any thing that we said in our prayers."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3:** Henry sees these as promises to those who consider the poor, noting that "liberality to the poor is the surest and safest way of thriving." God's care extends to minute details - "he will make all his bed from head to foot, so that no part shall be uneasy."

**Verse 4:** David's prayer "Heal my soul" demonstrates that "sin is the sickness of the soul; pardoning mercy heals it; renewing grace heals it."

**Verses 5-9:** Henry details the enemies' cruel behavior, noting their false visits where hearts "gathereth iniquity" and their whispering campaigns. The betrayal by a "familiar friend" prefigures greater treacheries.

**Verse 13:** The concluding doxology shows how personal suffering leads to universal praise of God's faithfulness.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to broader biblical themes through several references:
- Luke 18:13 (the tax collector's prayer for mercy)
- Acts 13:36 (David serving his generation)
- Romans 1:29-30 (whisperers and backbiters among worst sinners)  
- Jeremiah 20:10 (enemies reporting against the faithful)
- 2 Corinthians 6:15 (Belial as Satan)
- Revelation 11:10 (the world's hatred of good men)

The commentary suggests this psalm has messianic implications, particularly in the betrayal theme, though Henry doesn't fully develop this connection in the provided text.

Chapter 42
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 42: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 42 as "a mirror or looking-glass of pious and devout affections," describing it as **a conflict between sense and faith**. The psalm alternates between sense (complaining about present darkness) and faith (offering hope and assurance), with faith ultimately prevailing. Henry identifies this as a dialogue between holy desires and despair, where "gracious hopes and fears, joys and sorrows, are here struggling, but the pleasing passion comes off a conqueror."

The central theological theme is **holy love to God as the chief good**, which Henry calls "the power of godliness, the very life and soul of religion." He emphasizes that external religious performances without this love are merely "a shell and carcase."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**1. Value of Deprivation:** "Sometimes God teaches us effectually to know the worth of mercies by the want of them." Absence from worship can intensify our spiritual hunger.

**2. Faith as Antidote to Depression:** "A believing confidence in God is a sovereign antidote against prevailing despondency and disquietude of spirit." When feeling cast down, we must commune with our hearts and hope in God.

**3. Persistent Mourning for Lost Privileges:** Those deprived of public worship "should constantly mourn for the want of them, till they are restored to them again."

**4. Self-Examination in Sorrow:** When cast down, ask "Is there a cause, a real cause? Have not others more cause, that do not make so much ado?"

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2:** The thirsting represents "love upon the wing, soaring upwards in holy desires." Henry notes the psalmist desires "God, not the ordinances themselves, but the God of the ordinances."

**Verse 3:** The tears being "meat day and night" shows sustained grief over lost worship privileges, not just momentary sadness.

**Verse 4:** The remembrance of former worship experiences creates "painful melting of spirit." Henry emphasizes how the psalmist went "with the multitude" and "thought it no disparagement to his dignity."

**Verse 5:** The self-dialogue "Why art thou cast down, O my soul?" demonstrates necessary self-communion and correction. The cure is: "Hope thou in God, for I shall yet praise him."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to **Psalm 63**, suggesting similar authorship and circumstances. He references **Proverbs 12:25** regarding being "cast down" under burdens. He also alludes to **2 Samuel 6:3** regarding the ark's location during Saul's persecution, and **1 Chronicles 13:3** about David's faithful worship despite others' neglect.

Henry likely attributes this psalm to **David during exile** (either from Saul's persecution or Absalom's rebellion), when he was "driven from the sanctuary and cut off from the privilege of waiting upon God in public ordinances."

The commentary concludes with the assurance that even when external worship is denied, **God remains accessible**: "We know where our God is, and where to find him, when we know not where his ark is."

Chapter 43
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 43: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 43 as a companion piece to Psalm 42, describing it as "an appendix" written on the same occasion. The psalm reveals **five key aspects of David's relationship with God**: as Judge, Strength, Guide, Joy, and Hope. Henry emphasizes that this demonstrates the believer's comprehensive dependence on God in times of distress.

A central theological theme is **God's justice versus human persecution**. Henry notes the tension between trusting in "the almighty power of the church's God" while experiencing "the mighty force of the church's enemies," asserting that "the day will reconcile them when all his enemies shall become his footstool."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers. First, **the proper approach to spiritual enemies**: when facing unjust treatment, believers should appeal to God's supreme court rather than rely on human justice. Second, **prioritizing spiritual privileges**: David's heart was "upon the holy hill and the tabernacles, not upon his family-comforts, his court-preferments, or his diversions."

Most significantly, Henry teaches **self-examination and spiritual discipline**. David "quarrels with himself for his dejections and despondencies, and owns he did ill to yield to them," demonstrating how believers should actively combat discouragement through hope in God.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry interprets David's appeal "Judge me, O God" as both defensive (against false accusers) and offensive (seeking justice against enemies). He distinguishes this from asking God to judge our sins, where we would pray "*Enter not* into judgment with me."

**Verse 3**: The prayer "Send out thy light and thy truth" receives rich interpretation. Henry suggests this may prophetically refer to "Messiah the Prince and Elias his forerunner," while applying it practically to the Spirit's guidance toward heavenly communion.

**Verse 4**: The phrase "God my exceeding joy" is particularly emphasized. Henry notes the Hebrew intensity: "unto God the gladness of my joy, or of my triumph."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to **Matthew 26:44**, noting that Christ himself prayed repeatedly "saying the same words," validating the repetition between Psalms 42 and 43. He references **1 Samuel 16:16, 18** regarding David's musical excellence, emphasizing that "God is to be praised with the best we have."

The commentary suggests this psalm may have been designed "for the use of the Jews in their captivity in Babylon," connecting it to broader themes of exile and restoration throughout Scripture, while maintaining that such scriptures are "not of private interpretation" but have universal application.

Chapter 44
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 44: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 44 as prophetically addressing the Christian church's trials throughout history, noting that Paul applies verse 22 to believers in Romans 8:36. He emphasizes that the church "must enter into the kingdom of heaven through many tribulations." The psalm serves as "a psalm calculated for a day of fasting and humiliation upon occasion of some public calamity."

The central theological tension Henry identifies is between God's past faithfulness and present apparent abandonment. He stresses that victories come "not to their own merit, but to God's favour and free grace" and "not owing to their own might, but to God's power engaged for them."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Generational Testimony**: "It is a debt which every age owes to posterity to keep an account of God's works of wonder, and to transmit the knowledge of them to the next generation."

2. **Prayer and Faith**: Believers should pray for "deliverances" (plural), showing "enlargedness of their desire" and "strength of their faith in the power of God." Henry notes they pray "Command deliverances," emphasizing God's authority and power.

3. **Proper Boasting**: Christians should make God "their joy and praise," declaring "In God we have boasted...all the day long" rather than trusting in human strength.

4. **Perspective in Trials**: "Former experiences of God's power and goodness are strong supports to faith and powerful pleas in prayer under present calamities."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry emphasizes that "the many operations of providence are here spoken of as one work," showing God's unified purpose. The conquest of Canaan prefigures "the planting of the Christian church in the world, by the preaching of the gospel."

**Verse 4**: "Thou art my King, O God" represents both personal and corporate submission to divine authority.

**Verses 9-16**: Henry notes the particular grief of defeat for those "always used...to win the day," making current trials more shameful because "they had boasted in their God with great assurance."

**Verse 11**: The image of being "like sheep appointed for meat" emphasizes complete vulnerability and helplessness.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout his commentary:

- **Romans 8:36** - Paul's application of verse 22 to Christian suffering
- **Ezekiel 10:13** - The unity of God's works ("many wheels make but one wheel")
- **Psalm 145:4** - Generational praise ("one generation praise his works to another")
- **Deuteronomy 9:5-6** - Israel's victories not due to their righteousness
- **Matthew 8:8** - The centurion's faith paralleling "command deliverances"
- **Psalm 80:8** - God planting Israel like a tree

Henry also connects the psalm to the broader biblical narrative of church history, suggesting it may apply to persecution under "antichristian tyranny" and "New-Testament Babylon."

## Structure and Purpose

Henry outlines the psalm's four-part structure: acknowledging past mercies (vv. 1-8), presenting current calamities (vv. 9-16), protesting continued faithfulness (vv. 17-22), and petitioning for relief (vv. 22-26). This progression models how believers should approach God during times of corporate distress while maintaining both historical perspective and present honesty about their suffering.

Chapter 45
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 45: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 45 as **"an illustrious prophecy of Messiah the Prince"** that is **"all over gospel, and points at him only."** He firmly rejects any literal application to Solomon or David's marriages, viewing it as **"purely and only meant of Jesus Christ."** The psalm presents Christ as both bridegroom (espousing the church) and king (ruling in and for the church). Henry connects this to Jesus's parables comparing the kingdom of heaven to royal nuptial celebrations (Matt. 22:2; 25:1).

The commentary establishes a clear structure: Part I focuses on the royal bridegroom (Christ) including his transcendent excellence, victorious glory, righteous government, and splendorous court. Part II addresses the royal bride (the church) covering her consent, the nuptials, and their offspring.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that **"in singing this psalm our hearts must be filled with high thoughts of Christ, with an entire submission to and satisfaction in his government, and with an earnest desire of the enlarging and perpetuating of his church in the world."**

Regarding Christian discourse, he notes: **"Those that speak of Christ speak of a good matter, no subject so noble, so copious, so fruitful, so profitable, and so well-becoming us; it is a shame that this good matter is not more the matter of our discourse."** He encourages believers to speak from hearts that have been "warmed and affected" by Christ.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1:** Henry praises the **"dignity of the subject"** and **"excellency of the management."** He emphasizes that David's tongue was **"as the pen of a ready writer, guided by my heart in every word as the pen is by the hand."**

**Verse 2:** Christ's beauty surpasses all humanity: **"Jesus Christ is in himself, and in the eyes of all believers, more amiable and lovely than the children of men."** The phrase "grace is poured into thy lips" refers to Christ as the great trustee of divine grace, with the gospel flowing from his lips.

**Verses 3-5:** Henry interprets Christ's sword as **"the word of God"** and **"the sword of the Spirit."** The victories are spiritual - converting souls through gospel promises and conquering enemies through divine judgment. The **"terrible things"** include both conviction leading to conversion and ultimate judgment of persistent enemies.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive biblical connections throughout:
- **Hebrews 1:8** - Direct New Testament application to Christ
- **Song of Songs 2:1** - Christ as "rose of Sharon and lily of the valleys"
- **Revelation 14:3-4** - The new song sung by the virgin company
- **1 Peter 1:11** - The Spirit of Christ in the prophets
- **John 1:16** - Receiving from Christ's fullness
- **Luke 4:22** - The gracious words from Christ's lips
- **Revelation 19:15** - The sword proceeding from Christ's mouth
- **Isaiah 52:10-12** - God's promise of Christ's prosperity

The commentary masterfully demonstrates how this Old Testament psalm finds its ultimate fulfillment in the person and work of Jesus Christ, encouraging believers to find in him their supreme object of love and devotion.

Chapter 46
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 46: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 46 as a profound encouragement for believers to trust in God's power, providence, and gracious presence during the worst of times. The psalm likely commemorates David's victories and the peace God granted from surrounding enemies. Henry emphasizes God's threefold role: as **refuge** (protection from pursuit), **strength** (support under burdens), and **present help** (immediate, proven assistance). 

The commentary presents God as both "King of nations" ruling by providence and "King of saints" who specially protects His church. Henry notes that while earthly powers rage and kingdoms shake, God's church remains secure because "God is in the midst of her; she shall not be moved."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

- **Holy fearlessness**: "Those that with a holy reverence fear God need not with any amazement be afraid of the power of hell or earth"
- **Spiritual security over earthly concerns**: Those who "have laid up for themselves treasures in heaven" need not fear when "the earth and all the works that are therein shall be burnt up"
- **The power of spiritual comfort**: "The spiritual comforts which are conveyed to the saints by soft and silent whispers...are sufficient to counterbalance the most loud and noisy threatenings of an angry and malicious world"

Henry famously notes that Luther would say "Come let us sing the forty-sixth psalm" whenever he heard discouraging news, demonstrating the psalm's practical value for maintaining faith during trials.

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry emphasizes God as a "help found" - one proven reliable, "a help on which we may write *Probatum est*—It is tried."

**Verses 2-3**: The imagery of earth being removed and mountains cast into the sea represents the worst possible upheavals, yet believers remain unshaken.

**Verse 4**: The "river" represents "the covenant of grace" with promises as streams, or "the Spirit of grace" bringing gentle comfort, contrasting with the roaring, threatening waters of worldly turmoil.

**Verse 10**: "Be still, and know that I am God" applies both to enemies (who should cease their rage) and to believers (who should remain calm and trust).

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Proverbs 18:10** - God as refuge and strong tower
- **Isaiah 28:16** - Christ as the tried stone
- **Isaiah 8:6-7** - Waters of Siloam flowing gently by Jerusalem
- **John 7:38-39** - The Spirit as rivers of living water
- **Psalm 93:4** - God's sovereignty over raging waters
- **Isaiah 33:21** - God as a place of broad rivers and streams
- **Ezekiel 39:9-10** - Destruction of weapons symbolizing lasting peace

The commentary skillfully connects the psalm to both immediate historical context (David's victories) and broader theological themes of God's eternal kingdom and the church's ultimate security in Him.

Chapter 47
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 47 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies the psalm's scope as a universal call to praise God, interpreting it through both historical and Christological lenses. He suggests the psalm was written for the bringing of the ark to Mount Zion but "looks further, to the ascension of Christ into the heavenly Zion" and "the setting up of his kingdom in the world, to which the heathen should become willing subjects."

The commentary emphasizes God's **threefold majesty**: His awesome power ("The Lord most high is terrible"), His universal sovereignty as "a great King over all the earth," and His special care for His people. Henry consistently applies these themes both to Israel's historical experience and to Christ's spiritual kingdom.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**On worship expression**: He defends enthusiastic worship, noting that "expressions of pious and devout affections...ought not to be hastily censured" because "if they come from an upright heart, God will accept the strength of the affection and excuse the weakness of the expressions."

**On intelligent worship**: He emphasizes singing "with understanding" (v.7), meaning both "intelligently" - understanding why we praise God - and "instructively" - helping others understand God's perfections. This reflects the gospel principle of worshipping "with the spirit and with the understanding also."

**On divine providence**: Henry encourages believers to trust God's choices: "God shall choose my inheritance for me; let him appoint me my lot, and I will acquiesce in the appointment. He knows what is good for me better than I do for myself."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: The call to "all ye people" Henry sees as prophetic of Gentile conversion, referencing Romans 15:11. The clapping and shouting represent genuine joy that should "make all about you hear" God's works.

**Verse 3**: The subduing of nations points to Christ's kingdom, where He "shall bring them in as sheep into the fold...not for slaughter, but for preservation."

**Verse 5**: "God is gone up with a shout" receives dual interpretation - the historical ark's ascension and Christ's ascension, where He went up "as one who, having spoiled principalities and powers, then led captivity captive."

**Verse 9**: The gathering of princes represents both Israel's unified government and the prophetic calling of Gentiles into Christ's church, where kings "bring their glory and power into the New Jerusalem."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive biblical connections throughout: Romans 15:11 (Gentile inclusion), 1 Corinthians 14:15 (intelligent worship), Ephesians 5:19 (heart melody), Acts 1:9-11 (Christ's ascension and return), Psalm 68:18 (leading captivity captive), Hebrews 9:24 (Christ entering heaven), Ephesians 2:12-13 and 3:6 (Gentile fellow-heirs), Galatians 3:14 (Abraham's blessing), and Psalm 110:3 (willing people).

The commentary masterfully bridges Old Testament historical events with New Testament spiritual realities, presenting Psalm 47 as both ancient Israel's victory song and the church's celebration of Christ's universal reign.

Chapter 48
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 48: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Matthew Henry views Psalm 48 as a **triumphant celebration of God's protection over Jerusalem**, both as a historical reality and a prophetic type of the Gospel church. The psalm's central theme is God's sovereignty manifested through His special relationship with His chosen city. Henry emphasizes that "whatever is said to the honour of the church must redound to the honour of the church's God," establishing that all earthly praise must ultimately point to divine glory.

The commentary presents Jerusalem as **"the city of the great King"** - a designation Henry connects to Christ's teaching about swearing by Jerusalem (Matthew 5:35). This earthly city serves as a type of the "heavenly Jerusalem" and "the Jerusalem which is above" (Hebrews 12:22, Galatians 4:26), pointing believers toward the eternal church triumphant.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Cultivating Gratitude and Faith**: The phrase "as we have heard, so have we seen" (v. 8) should encourage believers to compare God's promises with His performances, strengthening faith through observed fulfillments. This creates a cycle where "what we have seen obliges us to believe what we have heard."

**Corporate Worship and Reflection**: When believers "meet in his temple," they should deliberately "think of his lovingkindness" (v. 9). Henry emphasizes that undisturbed worship should prompt gratitude: "When we enjoy the benefit of public ordinances undisturbed...we should take occasion thence to think of his lovingkindness."

**Universal Praise**: Christians must recognize that "as far as his name goes his praise will go" (v. 10), understanding their role in extending God's glory "unto the ends of the earth."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry provides rich exposition on key verses:

**Verses 1-3**: He emphasizes that God's greatness is particularly manifest in places where He is truly known. "Happy the kingdom, the city, the family, the heart, in which God is great, in which he is uppermost."

**Verses 4-7**: The confederate kings' terror demonstrates how "God can dispirit the stoutest of his church's enemies." Their retreat is summarized not as "I came, I saw, I conquered" but "I came, I saw, I was defeated."

**Verse 14**: This concluding promise of eternal guidance becomes, in Henry's interpretation, an assurance of God's perpetual covenant with His people through all generations.

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout his commentary:

- **Psalm 132:13-14** - God's eternal choice of Zion as His dwelling place
- **Matthew 16:18** - The church built on a rock that hell cannot overcome  
- **1 Thessalonians 5:3** - Sudden destruction coming upon the ungodly like birth pains
- **Isaiah 14:32** - "The Lord hath founded Zion"
- **Zechariah 14:20-21** - Holiness inscribed upon Jerusalem

Henry particularly emphasizes how this psalm "might serve any other similar occasion in after-times, and be applicable also to the glories of the gospel church." This typological interpretation allows him to connect Old Testament Jerusalem with the New Testament church, making the psalm perpetually relevant for Christian worship and encouragement.

The commentary concludes with the assurance that while earthly Jerusalem was eventually destroyed, the Gospel church "shall be established for ever" as the true fulfillment of these promises.

Chapter 49
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 49: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry frames Psalm 49 as a **sermon** rather than a prayer or praise, designed to "convince the men of this world of their sin and folly in setting their hearts upon the things of this world." The psalm addresses two primary audiences: worldly people trapped in material security, and godly people troubled by the prosperity of the wicked.

The central theological message is the **vanity of worldly wealth** and its inability to provide true security. Henry emphasizes that riches cannot save from death, secure earthly happiness, or guarantee eternal blessing. In contrast, believers possess "holy gracious security" through trusting God rather than material possessions.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**For the wealthy**: Don't be "proud" of possessions or "secure in the enjoyment of them, but lay them out in doing good." Use wealth to "make to themselves friends" rather than trusting in riches as ultimate security.

**For the poor**: "Be content with their little, and not envy those that have abundance." Henry warns that "poor people are as much in danger from an inordinate desire towards the wealth of the world as rich people from an inordinate delight in it."

**For believers facing persecution**: The psalm provides comfort "against the fear of death" and "against the fear of the prospering power of wicked people."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry notes the universal scope - "all you inhabitants of the world" - showing this truth isn't limited to those with divine revelation but accessible through natural light.

**Verse 4**: The "parable" isn't figurative but represents "a wise discourse." Henry emphasizes that teachers must "first learn themselves" before instructing others.

**Verse 5**: Henry interprets "wherefore should I fear" as both freedom from fearing the wealthy wicked and from sharing their terror of death.

**Verses 6-11**: Henry identifies three characteristics of worldly people: they "repose a confidence in their riches," "take a pride in their riches," and "flatter themselves with an expectation of the perpetuity of their worldly possessions."

**Verse 13**: "This their way is their folly" - Henry declares worldliness "contrary both to right reason and to their true interest," noting that "the love of the world is a disease that runs in the blood."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Job 31:24** - "Their gold is their hope" 
- **Mark 10:24** - Christ's teaching on the difficulty of rich people's salvation
- **1 Timothy 6:17** - Warnings about trusting in riches
- **Luke 12:19-20** - God calling the rich fool foolish
- **Psalm 23:4** - God's presence removing fear of death
- **Isaiah 50:4** - Having the "tongue of the learned"

Henry concludes with the comforting truth that "the children of God, though ever so poor, are in this truly happy, above the most prosperous of the children of this world, that they are well guarded against the terrors of death and the judgment to come."

Chapter 50
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 50: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 50 as a psalm of **instruction and reproof**, addressing three primary concerns: (1) **the inadequacy of ritual religion without practical godliness**, (2) **a prediction of the abolishing of ceremonial law** under the Messiah's kingdom, and (3) **a representation of the day of judgment**. Henry emphasizes that placing religion solely in ritual services while neglecting practical godliness "is as sure a way to ruin" as worldliness.

The psalm reveals **God's threefold nature** ("El, Elohim, Jehovah") and presents Christ as both Lawgiver and Judge. Henry sees the transition from Old Testament ceremonial worship to New Testament spiritual worship, citing John 4:23-24's call to worship "in spirit and in truth."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**For True Worship**: God desires thanksgiving, prayer, and covenant faithfulness over mere sacrifice. "Offer unto God thanksgiving; and pay thy vows unto the most High" represents genuine worship that honors God's character rather than attempting to meet His supposed needs.

**For Religious Hypocrites**: The commentary warns against "expounding the law to others, but living wicked lives themselves." Henry emphasizes that external religious performance cannot compensate for immoral living.

**For All Believers**: The psalm calls for authentic covenant relationship with God "by sacrifice"—ultimately pointing to Christ as "the great sacrifice from whom all the legal sacrifices derived what value they had."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-6**: Henry sees God's majestic appearance with **"Zion, the perfection of beauty"** representing holiness as "the perfection of beauty." The divine court summons all creation as witnesses.

**Verses 7-15**: God declares He doesn't need their sacrifices since "every beast of the forest is mine, and the cattle upon a thousand hills." Henry notes: "What occasion had he for their bullocks and goats who has the command of all the beasts?"
