**Verse 5**: "Gather my saints together unto me; those that have made a covenant with me by sacrifice" refers both to genuine believers and Israel as God's covenant people.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry provides extensive scriptural connections:
- **John 4:23-24** (spiritual worship)
- **2 Chronicles 29:30** (Asaph as seer)
- **Isaiah 2:3, Micah 4:2** (gospel going forth from Zion)
- **Luke 24:47** (preaching beginning at Jerusalem)
- **2 Thessalonians 2:1** (saints gathered to Christ)
- **1 Corinthians 6:2** (saints judging the world)
- **Hebrews 12:22, 28** (Christians coming to Mount Zion)

The commentary connects Old Testament sacrificial imagery to Christ's ultimate sacrifice and the establishment of the gospel kingdom, showing how Psalm 50 bridges the ceremonial law and Christian spiritual worship.

Chapter 51
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 51: A Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Henry presents this psalm as "the most eminent of the penitential psalms," written after David's sin with Bathsheba and murder of Uriah. The core theological themes include:

**Total Depravity**: Henry emphasizes David's confession of original sin ("I was shapen in iniquity"), showing that even great saints struggle with inherent corruption. This acknowledgment prevents presumption and highlights our constant need for grace.

**Divine Mercy as Sole Hope**: Despite David's great merits and services, Henry notes he "does not offer to balance his evil deeds with his good deeds" but "flies to God's infinite mercy." The plea is "Have mercy upon me for mercy's sake," appealing to God's "lovingkindness" and "multitude of tender mercies."

**Sin as Fundamentally Against God**: Henry highlights David's confession "Against thee, thee only, have I sinned," emphasizing that all sin is ultimately an offense against God's truth, authority, and holiness, regardless of human victims involved.

## Key Practical Applications

**Pattern for Repentance**: Henry sees this psalm as providing "apposite words" for all penitents. He encourages believers to "again and again pray over this psalm" when conscience convicts of gross sin.

**Public Confession**: David made his repentance public "that the plaster might be as wide as the wound." Henry notes that true penitents "will not be ashamed to own their repentance" and should covet "the honour of penitents" rather than false innocence.

**Ongoing Self-Examination**: Henry describes how David's sin remained "ever before me," using it for "continual reflection" to stay humble, resist temptation, and remain patient under trials.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: The petition for mercy based solely on God's character, not human merit. Henry emphasizes the imagery of "blotting out" transgressions "as a debt is crossed out of the book" and the need for thorough washing since "the stain is deep."

**Verses 3-4**: True confession involves both acknowledging specific guilt and maintaining continual sorrow. Henry notes David's willingness to "justify God by condemning themselves" in accepting divine judgment.

**Verse 5**: Original sin doesn't excuse actual transgression but explains its root. Henry suggests this should have made David more cautious: "Had I duly considered this before, I should not have made so bold with the temptation."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects this psalm to numerous scriptures:
- **Luke 18:13**: Links David's plea to Christ's parable of the publican: "God be merciful to me a sinner!"
- **Luke 15:18**: Sees David's confession echoed in the prodigal son's return
- **1 Kings 15:5**: Notes this sin as David's only major failure
- **Luke 22:32**: David's example strengthens others in repentance
- **Psalm 32:4-5**: References David's previous experience with confession bringing relief

Henry concludes that this psalm serves both personal and corporate purposes - as David's authentic repentance record and as an instructional pattern, showing how "being converted himself, he thus strengthens his brethren." The commentary emphasizes that through Christ, even the greatest sinners can "find mercy to pardon and grace for seasonable help."

Chapter 52
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 52 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry views Psalm 52 as David's prophetic response to Doeg the Edomite's betrayal, which led to the massacre of the priests at Nob. The central theological themes include:

**Divine Justice vs. Human Wickedness**: Henry presents a stark contrast between God's enduring goodness and human malice. He emphasizes that "The goodness of God endures continually" serves both as a rebuke to the wicked who abuse God's patience and as comfort to the righteous who trust in divine protection.

**The Power and Danger of the Tongue**: The commentary extensively explores the destructive nature of deceitful speech, describing Doeg's tongue as "like a sharp razor, working deceitfully" and emphasizing how "the more there is of craft and contrivance in any wickedness the more there is of the devil in it."

**Divine Retribution**: Henry interprets the psalm as prophetic judgment, stating that "Sons of perdition actively shall be sons of perdition passively" and "Destroyers shall be destroyed."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

1. **Warning Against Boasting in Sin**: "It is bad to do ill, but it is worse to boast of it and glory in it when we have done." He warns that those who "glory in their sin glory in their shame."

2. **The Danger of Trusting in Wealth**: Using Doeg as an example, Henry warns against those who trust "in the abundance of riches" rather than making "God his strength."

3. **Proper Response to God's Judgments**: The righteous should "see and fear" when God judges the wicked, using it as an opportunity to "reverence the justice of God, and stand in awe of him."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry interprets "mighty man" as referring to Doeg's position as "chamberlain of the household," noting the irony that "it is no honour for those that wear a sword to hector those that wear an ephod."

**Verses 2-4**: Henry identifies four charges against Doeg: malice, falsehood, subtlety in sin, and affection to sin. He particularly emphasizes how Doeg "loved lying" and practiced deception while maintaining religious appearances.

**Verse 5**: The judgment involves both spiritual and physical destruction - being "plucked out of the tabernacle" (losing God's favor) and "rooted out of the land of the living."

**Verse 8**: Henry contrasts Doeg with David, noting that while the wicked "flourish like a green bay-tree" (bearing no useful fruit), "the righteous flourish like a green olive-tree, which is fat as well as flourishing."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects this psalm to the historical account in **1 Samuel 22:9-22**, providing crucial context for understanding David's situation. He references **Psalm 10:3** regarding the boasting of the wicked, **Psalm 2:4** about God's laughter at the wicked, and draws parallels to **2 Corinthians 2:14** regarding triumph in tribulation.

The olive tree imagery connects to **Psalm 92:14** (the righteous flourishing), **Judges 9:9** (the olive's honor to God and man), and **Romans 11:17** (drawing nourishment from the good olive).

Henry concludes that believers should live "a life of faith and holy confidence in God" while maintaining "a life of thankfulness and holy joy," trusting in God's mercy "for ever and ever" rather than in temporal wealth or power.

Chapter 53
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary Summary: Psalm 53

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry views Psalm 53 as God's emphatic declaration of human depravity, noting that "God speaks once, yea, twice" since this psalm mirrors Psalm 14 almost verbatim. The repetition serves as divine emphasis, using "line upon line" to drive home humanity's sinful condition. The commentary presents a systematic exposition of universal human corruption, structured around "the fact of sin," "the fault of sin," "the fountain of sin," "the folly of sin," "the filthiness of sin," "the fruit of sin," and divine judgment upon sin.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that "in singing it we ought to lament the corruption of the human nature, and the wretched degeneracy of the world we live in, yet rejoicing in hope of the great salvation." He draws practical lessons about recognizing sin's progression from corrupt thinking to corrupt behavior, noting that "men's bad practices flow from their bad principles." The commentary warns against practical atheism - those who "profess to know God, yet in works, because in thoughts, they deny him."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry identifies the fool as one who practices atheism either "in opinion or practice," calling them "the greatest fools in the world." He explains that corruption stems from denying God's existence or authority.

**Verses 2-3**: God's omniscient survey reveals that "all the sinfulness of their hearts and lives is naked and open before him." Henry notes that those who don't seek God "are like brute-beasts that have no understanding."

**Verse 4**: The "workers of iniquity" demonstrate their ignorance: "those may truly be said to know nothing that do not know God."

**Verse 5**: Henry explains the "great fear where no fear was" as guilty consciences creating terror even without apparent cause, noting "the wicked flees when none pursues."

**Verse 6**: This verse points to messianic hope - both immediate deliverances and ultimate salvation through Christ, "for he shall save his people from their sins" (Matt. 1:21).

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to other biblical passages: Psalm 141:7 (scattered bones), Revelation 20:9 (siege against the saints), and Matthew 1:21 (Jesus saving from sin). He also references the principle "Corruptio optimi est pessima" - that the corruption of the best becomes the worst, particularly applicable to human nature's nobility when depraved.

The commentary concludes with hope, pointing to both temporal deliverances for God's people and the eternal salvation that believers in Old Testament times "looked and hoped, that prayed and waited, for."

Chapter 54
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 54: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 54 as a prayer of distress and deliverance, written when the Ziphites betrayed David to Saul. The central theological theme is **divine faithfulness versus human treachery**. Henry emphasizes that "God is faithful, for men are not to be trusted" (Micah 7:5), contrasting human betrayal with God's unwavering support. 

A key interpretative insight is Henry's **typological reading** - he sees the Ziphites as "types of Judas the traitor" and suggests the psalm may point to Christ, "of whom David was a type." The psalm demonstrates the pattern of God delivering His people from all troubles, ultimately fulfilled in Christ's victory over enemies.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

1. **Expectation of Trials**: "Never let a good man expect to be safe and easy till he comes to heaven." Believers should anticipate opposition even from unexpected sources.

2. **The Danger of Godlessness**: Those who "have not set God before them" become capable of any wickedness, having "broken through the fear of God."

3. **Faithful Prayer**: Even in distress, believers should continually lift prayers to heaven, depending solely on "God's name" and "God's strength" as refuge.

4. **Encouragement from Past Deliverances**: "We should thus, in our greatest straits, encourage ourselves with our past experiences."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry emphasizes David's complete dependence on God's name and strength rather than human help. The betrayal by fellow Israelites was worse than expected Philistine hostility.

**Verses 4-5**: The triumphant "Behold, God is mine helper" represents faith's victory over fear. Henry notes this isn't malicious revenge but faithful prayer based on God's word: "There is truth in God's threatenings as well as in his promises."

**Verses 6-7**: Henry distinguishes between ceremonial sacrifice and heartfelt praise: "A thankful heart, and the calves of our lips giving thanks to his name, are the sacrifices God will accept."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout:
- **Micah 7:5** on human untrustworthiness
- **2 Corinthians 11:26** comparing Paul's and David's persecution by countrymen
- **Psalm 118:7** on God taking our part
- **2 Timothy 4:17-18** on encouragement from past deliverances
- **1 Samuel 23:19, 26:1** providing historical context
- **Ecclesiastes 3:16** on oppression in judgment seats

The commentary demonstrates Henry's characteristic integration of scripture with practical application, presenting the psalm as both historical record and prophetic type pointing to Christ's ultimate deliverance.

Chapter 55
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 55: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry suggests this psalm arose from Absalom's rebellion, with Ahithophel as the treacherous friend. While some see typological connections to Christ's sufferings and Judas's betrayal, Henry notes no New Testament application specifically supports this. The psalm demonstrates the **contrast between human frailty and divine faithfulness** - even David, a man of great courage, experiences overwhelming fear when facing betrayal and conspiracy.

The commentary emphasizes **God's sovereignty in judgment and deliverance**. Henry presents David's imprecations not as personal vengeance but as prophetic prayers aligned with God's justice, showing that ultimate vindication belongs to the Lord.

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer as universal remedy**: Henry declares, "Prayer is a salve for every sore and a relief to the spirit under every burden." He encourages believers to pour out their hearts honestly before God, trusting He will not hide Himself from sincere supplication.

**Handling betrayal**: The deepest wounds come from trusted friends rather than open enemies. Henry notes we can bear opposition from expected sources, but betrayal from intimates - those who "took sweet counsel together, and walked unto the house of God in company" - cuts deepest.

**Longing for peace**: David's desire for "wings like a dove" to escape reflects the legitimate Christian yearning for rest from worldly turmoil. Henry observes that "gracious souls wish to retire from the hurry and bustle of this world, that they may sweetly enjoy God and themselves."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-8**: David's emotional progression from prayer through weeping to trembling demonstrates how "the best men are not always alike strong in faith." His desire to flee reveals that even courage has limits under extreme pressure.

**Verses 9-15**: The corruption of Jerusalem, "the holy city," serves as a sobering reminder that no earthly institution remains immune to decay. Henry warns against surprise at "corruptions and disorders of this church on earth."

**The treacherous friend (v. 13)**: "It was thou, a man mine equal, my guide, and mine acquaintance" - Henry emphasizes this betrayal's unique sting because the traitor's insider knowledge enabled greater damage than any external enemy could inflict.

## Cross-References and Applications

Henry connects this psalm to **Jeremiah 9:2** regarding the desire for wilderness retreat from treacherous people, and **2 Samuel 15** for historical context of David's flight from Absalom.

He draws parallels to Christ's experience with hostile crowds stirred by chief priests, similar to how enemies "stirred up the people to cry out against David."

The commentary concludes with eschatological hope, contrasting earthly Jerusalem's corruption with the New Jerusalem "where there is no violence nor strife, no mischief nor guilt, and into which no unclean thing shall enter."

Henry encourages readers to apply the psalm personally when facing similar troubles, or to "sympathize with those to whose case it comes nearer," anticipating God's ultimate justice for persecutors and salvation for the persecuted.

Chapter 56
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalms 56 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets this psalm as David's expression of faith "throwing himself into the hands of God" even when he had "by his fear and folly thrown himself into the hands of the Philistines." The central theological theme is **divine providence in affliction** - that God maintains perfect awareness and care for His people even in their darkest trials.

The psalm's title "Jonath-elem-rechokim" (silent dove afar off) represents David as **a type of Christ** - innocent, patient, and silent under persecution. Henry emphasizes that believers should be "as silent doves" wherever they are, neither murmuring against God nor railing at their persecutors.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several powerful applications:

**Trust over Fear**: "What time I am afraid, I will trust in thee" demonstrates that in times of special fear, believers must make God their confidence. This practice "will fix the heart and keep it in peace."

**Prayer as Warfare**: Henry notes that "I need no other weapons than prayers and tears" - believers fight best against spiritual enemies "upon our knees." 

**Praising Promises**: Christians should give thanks for God's promises even before their fulfillment, as David declares "In God I will praise his word" - meaning we should glory in God's promises though "not yet performed."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 8** receives Henry's most memorable treatment: God keeps David's tears "in thy bottle" and "in thy book." Henry explains that "God has a bottle and a book for his people's tears" - God observes with compassion, remembers their sorrows, and "what was sown a tear will come up a pearl."

**Verses 1-2**: The enemies' desire to "swallow" David reveals their barbarous nature - "no less would serve" than complete destruction.

**Verse 4**: The phrase "what flesh can do unto me" emphasizes human frailty - "it is but flesh, and cannot do much; nay, it can do nothing but by divine permission."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to multiple biblical passages:
- **1 Samuel 21:10-11** for historical context of David's capture in Gath
- **Psalm 84:3** regarding exile from the sanctuary  
- **Luke 20:20** comparing David's enemies watching him to Christ's enemies
- **2 Kings 20:5** ("I have seen thy tears") showing God's attention to human sorrow
- **Ephesians 6:18** on spiritual warfare through prayer

Henry concludes that this psalm enables "a good Christian" to "rejoice in God, and praise him for what he will do, as well as for what he has done" - demonstrating faith that praises God proactively based on His faithful character rather than merely reacting to circumstances.

Chapter 57
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 57: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 57 as a model of faith transforming affliction into praise. Written when David fled from Saul in the cave, the psalm demonstrates the movement from prayer and complaint to joy and thanksgiving. The title "Al-taschith" (Destroy not) reflects either David's refusal to kill Saul when given opportunity, or God's protective limitation over David's persecution - "he permitted Satan to afflict Job, Only save his life."

Henry emphasizes God's sovereignty over all circumstances, noting that "whatever God performs concerning his people, it will appear, in the issue, to have been performed for them and for their benefit." This reflects the theological principle that divine providence works ultimately for the good of the faithful.

### Key Practical Applications

**Prayer in Affliction**: Henry draws several practical lessons about approaching God in distress:
- Genuine dependence: "My soul trusteth in thee" - not mere profession but sincere reliance
- Confident expectation of deliverance: "these calamities will be over-past; the storm will blow over"
- Priority of God's glory over personal comfort: "When David was in the greatest distress and disgrace he did not pray, Lord, exalt me, but, Lord, exalt thy own name"

**Preparation for Worship**: Henry emphasizes that effective praise requires intentional preparation - "My heart is fixed" both for God's providence and for worship itself, noting "nothing is done to purpose, in religion, further than it is done with the heart."

### Notable Verse-by-verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry highlights the "shadow of thy wings" imagery, connecting it both to a hen protecting chicks and to the cherubim wings over the mercy seat, showing God's protective nature and covenant faithfulness.

**Verse 4**: The description of enemies as lions "set on fire" leads Henry to observe they "were themselves set on fire of hell" (James 3:6), while their "teeth were spears and arrows" shows how "a spiteful tongue is a dangerous weapon."

**Verse 7**: The dramatic shift to "My heart is fixed" prompts Henry's memorable observation: "How strangely is the tune altered here!" showing how "David's prayers and complaints, by the lively actings of faith, are here, all of a sudden, turned into praises and thanksgivings."

### Cross-references to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive biblical connections throughout:
- Links to Luke 18:13 (the publican's prayer) for "Be merciful unto me"
- Connects to Matthew 23:37 regarding God's protective wings
- References John 12:27-28 comparing David's prayer for God's glory to Jesus' similar petition
- Cites Psalm 25:10 on God's mercy and truth
- Uses James 3:6 to explain the enemies' fiery malice

Henry concludes by noting how David's public praise "among the people and nations" foreshadows the universal reach of the psalms themselves, making David's worship perpetually present "in his psalms, which fill the universal church, and will to the end of time."

Chapter 58
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary Summary: Psalm 58

## Historical Context and Interpretation

Henry suggests this psalm arose when Saul legally condemned David as a traitor through the supreme court before pursuing him militarily. David was "proclaimed 'qui caput gerit lupinum--an outlawed wolf,' whom any man might kill and no man might protect." The elders passed this bill of attainder to curry favor with Saul.

## Main Theological Themes

**Divine Justice and Corrupt Authority**: The central theme addresses the corruption of earthly judges who pervert justice while facing ultimate accountability to God. Henry emphasizes that "you are sons of men; mortal and dying, and that you stand upon the same level before God with the meanest of those you trample upon."

**Total Depravity**: The psalm illustrates humanity's fallen nature. "The wicked are estranged from the womb...they go astray as soon as they be born." Henry explains this demonstrates how "wickedness is bred in the bone with them; they brought it into the world with them."

**God as Ultimate Judge**: The psalm affirms that despite earthly injustice, "there is a God that judgeth in the earth" who will vindicate the righteous.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several applications for believers facing injustice:

1. **Self-examination in speech**: "It is good for us often to reflect upon what we say with this serious question, Do we indeed speak righteousness?"

2. **Understanding opposition**: When facing hostile authorities, remember their corruption stems from their fallen nature - they are "like the deaf adder that stoppeth her ear" to God's word.

3. **Faith perspective on enemies**: "Let us by faith then see what they shall be and then we shall not fear what they are."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: The judges' corruption involved both neglecting justice and actively promoting "violence...under colour of justice." They "held the balances (the emblem of justice) in their hands" while practicing oppression "under the pretext of law and right."

**Verses 3-5**: Henry identifies three manifestations of corrupt nature: falsehood ("how soon will little children tell a lie"), malice ("their poison...like the poison of a serpent"), and untractableness (like serpents that stop their ears to avoid being charmed).

**Verses 6-9**: David's imprecations are "actuated by a public spirit...not by any private revenge." The imagery of melting snails represents how the self-consumed "does but consume himself, and will quickly bring himself to nothing."

**Verses 10-11**: The righteous rejoice at divine vengeance not from cruelty but because it "confirms their faith in the providence of God" and demonstrates that divine justice ultimately prevails.

## Cross-References

Henry connects this psalm to:
- 1 Samuel 22:7 (Saul's ability to give rewards)
- Ecclesiastes 3:16 (wickedness in places of judgment)
- Isaiah 48:8 ("transgressors from the womb")
- Jeremiah 9:3 (bending tongues like bows for lies)
- Psalm 73:2,13 (temptation from wicked prosperity)

The commentary emphasizes that "sin appears here both exceedingly sinful and exceedingly dangerous, and God a just avenger of wrong."

Chapter 59
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 59: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies three primary spiritual elements in this psalm: **the language of nature** (complaints about enemies), **prophetical spirit** (looking forward to Christ and his enemies), and **grace and holy faith** (trust in God). He emphasizes that while complaints are natural and prophecy is divinely inspired, only the faith element should be imitated by believers.

The psalm centers on **God's sovereignty over enemies** and His role as defender of the innocent. Henry presents God as both "Lord of hosts" (having power to execute judgment) and "God of Israel" (specially committed to His people's welfare). The commentary reveals a **theology of divine justice** where God laughs at atheistic enemies and uses gradual judgment to create lasting monuments of His justice.

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer Life Under Pressure**: Henry commends David for maintaining "communion with God" and "composure of mind" during crisis, noting "happy are those whose intercourse with heaven is not intercepted by their cares, griefs, or fears." This models how believers should respond to persecution.

**Spiritual Warfare**: Henry draws parallels to Christian experience: "Thus earnestly should we pray daily to be defended and delivered from our spiritual enemies, the temptations of Satan, and the corruptions of our own hearts, which war against our spiritual life."

**Innocence and Suffering**: The commentary teaches that "the innocency of the godly will not secure them from the malignity of the wicked," but provides "great support and comfort under troubles" through clear conscience.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry emphasizes God as "my God, under whose protection I have put myself" - highlighting covenant relationship as basis for protection.

**Verses 6-7**: The enemies' dog-like behavior represents how persecutors use "clamour" and false accusations. Henry notes: "The sword perhaps would not have been in Saul's hand if it had not been first in their lips."

**Verse 11**: "Slay them not, lest my people forget" - Henry explains this as God's wisdom in gradual judgment: "Swift destructions startle men for the present, but they are soon forgotten."

**Verses 16-17**: The morning song represents triumph of faith over fear, showing how believers should "wait upon God" with expectant praise.

## Cross-References and Scripture Connections

Henry connects this psalm to **1 Samuel 19:11** (the historical occasion), **Psalm 22:16** (enemies compared to dogs), and **Isaiah 55:3** ("sure mercies of David"). He links the laughing God motif to **Psalm 2:4** and **Proverbs 1:26**.

The commentary draws parallels between David's enemies and **Christ's persecutors**, suggesting prophetic application beyond the immediate historical context. Henry also references **Psalm 42:10** regarding words that wound like swords, creating a biblical theology of verbal persecution.

Chapter 60
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 60: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 60 as a "day of triumph" composition, written during David's zenith of prosperity after victories over Syrians and Edomites. The central theological theme is **God's sovereignty in both adversity and blessing**. Henry emphasizes that "David, in prosperity, was as devout as David in adversity," highlighting the believer's need to remain dependent on God regardless of circumstances.

The psalm demonstrates the **cyclical nature of divine discipline and restoration**. Henry notes that God's displeasure caused Israel's past hardships, but His mercy now provides hope for complete victory. A key theme is the **balance between human responsibility and divine agency** - while David uses military means, ultimate success depends entirely on God's favor.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Remembrance in prosperity**: When God turns circumstances favorable, believers should "remember our former calamities" to maintain humility, magnify God's goodness, and "rejoice with trembling" rather than becoming secure.

**Unity under divine leadership**: David's banner represents how God-ordained leadership unites scattered people, animates the faithful, and intimidates enemies. Henry applies this to Christ as "given for an ensign of the people."

**Prayer and praise balance**: Henry observes that "we have our cares at the same time that we have our joys" - believers must bring both concerns and celebrations before God with appropriate affections.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry interprets the "wine of astonishment" as believers being "like men intoxicated, and at our wits' end" during divine discipline, unable to reconcile circumstances with God's promises.

**Verse 4**: The banner given to "those that fear thee" represents both unity (gathering people "to their colours") and encouragement, with Christ as the ultimate banner under whom "the church becomes terrible as an army with banners."

**Verse 6**: Henry emphasizes that "God's word of promise, being a firm foundation of hope, is a full fountain of joy." David's confidence stems from God having "spoken in his holiness."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple biblical passages:

- **2 Samuel 8:3, 13** and **1 Chronicles 18:3, 12** for historical context
- **Zechariah 11:14** regarding the "staff of bands" being broken
- **Lamentations 3:19-20** on remembering affliction for humility
- **Isaiah 11:10** connecting David's banner to Christ as "ensign of the people"
- **Psalm 89:35** regarding God's oath to David
- **1 Corinthians 3:22-23** on believers' inheritance in Christ

Henry concludes with the principle that while "vain is the help of man," through God's strength believers "shall do valiantly," as divine power ultimately "tread[s] down our enemies." The commentary masterfully weaves together historical context, theological reflection, and practical Christian living.

Chapter 61
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 61: Summary

## Structure and Context

Matthew Henry notes that David begins this psalm "with a sad heart, but concludes with an air of pleasantness—begins with prayers and tears, but ends with songs of praise." Written during exile (whether from Saul or Absalom), the psalm demonstrates how "the soul, by being lifted up to God, returns to the enjoyment of itself."

## Main Theological Themes

**Divine Protection and Refuge**: The central metaphor is God as "the rock that is higher than I" (v. 2). Henry interprets this as both a place of safety beyond human reach and as Christ himself: "This rock is Christ; those are safe that are in him." God serves as "a shelter" and "strong tower from the enemy."

**Covenant Faithfulness**: Henry emphasizes God's mercy and truth as preserving forces. Past experiences of God's protection become the foundation for future hope: "Past experiences of the benefit of trusting in God...should encourage us to hope that it will not be in vain."

**Messianic Anticipation**: The reference to "the king" (v. 6) points beyond David to "the Messiah, the King of whom he was a type," whose years "would be as many generations."

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer in Distress**: Henry stresses perseverance in prayer regardless of circumstances. Distance from the sanctuary shouldn't hinder prayer since "Heaven is equally accessible from all places." Even when "the heart is overwhelmed," believers should cry to God because "weeping must quicken praying, and not deaden it."

**Faithful Service**: Those expecting God's protection must make His service "their constant work and business." Henry notes that "none but his menial servants have the benefit of his protection."

**Vow-keeping**: Henry emphasizes the importance of performing our vows to God daily, as "God is a witness to all our vows...He keeps an account of them."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: David's commitment to pray despite distance and despair demonstrates that separation from earthly comforts "should drive us so much the nearer to God, the fountain of all comfort."

**Verse 4**: The tabernacle represents both earthly worship and heavenly reality. "Those that dwell in God's tabernacle...during their short ever on earth, shall dwell in that tabernacle which is the house of glory during an endless ever."

**Verse 5**: God hearing our vows means He "has taken notice of them...been well pleased with them...and put me in mind of them."

**Verses 6-7**: Applied messianically, these verses comfort David that "the Mediator shall abide before God for ever" and "ever lives, making intercession."

## Cross-References

Henry connects this psalm to numerous scriptures: James 5:13 (prayer in affliction), Exodus 33:22 (God's protection), Proverbs 18:10 (God's name as refuge), Hebrews 9:8-9,24 (tabernacle typology), Genesis 31:13 and 35:1 (God's reminder of vows), Acts 13:36 (serving one's generation), Micah 7:20 and Luke 1:72-73 (mercy and truth), and Psalm 27:4 (dwelling in God's house).

Henry concludes that this psalm provides "that which is very expressive both of our faith and of our hope, of our prayers and of our praises," making it suitable for believers in all circumstances of distress and deliverance.

Chapter 62
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 62: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 62 as a meditation on **divine dependence and waiting upon God**. The central theological theme is that God alone is humanity's true source of salvation, strength, and security. Henry emphasizes the contrast between God's faithfulness and human unreliability, highlighting that "creatures are insufficient; they are nothing without him."

The psalm demonstrates **progressive faith** - Henry notes how David's confidence grows from "I shall not be greatly moved" (v.2) to "I shall not be moved at all" (v.6), illustrating that "the more faith is acted the more active it is. *Crescit eundo*--It grows by being exercised."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**On Waiting Upon God**: Believers should "cheerfully refer ourselves, and the disposal of all our affairs, to his will and wisdom" and "patiently expect a doubtful event, with an entire satisfaction in his righteousness and goodness."

**On Prayer**: "Pour out your heart before him" means making "penitent confession of sin" and offering "desires to him with all humble freedom, and then entirely refer ourselves to his disposal."

**On Misplaced Trust**: Henry warns against trusting in human power, noting that "men of low degree are vanity" and "men of high degree are a lie" - they will "deceive us if we trust to them." Similarly, believers must not trust in worldly wealth or "riches got by fraud and violence."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets "my soul waiteth upon God" as being "silent to God" - saying "nothing against what he does, but quietly expects what he will do."

**Verses 3-4**: David's enemies are characterized as those who "imagine mischief" and "delight in lies" - "they bless with their mouth, but they curse inwardly." Henry sees them as "a bowing wall and a tottering fence" destined for divine judgment.

**Verses 5-7**: The progression shows David encouraging himself to "continue waiting upon God," with his joy advancing "into a holy triumph" as he declares "In God is my salvation and my glory."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple biblical passages:
- **Jeremiah 3:23** and **Psalm 121:1-2** regarding salvation coming from God alone
- **2 Corinthians 1:10** on trusting God based on past deliverance  
- **2 Corinthians 4:8** on being "perplexed, but not in despair"
- **Isaiah 26:3** on being "kept in perfect peace"
- **Jeremiah 17:5-9** warning against misplaced confidence in human strength

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches believers to "stir up ourselves to wait on God," finding in him both temporal deliverance and eternal salvation.

Chapter 63
----------------------------------------
# Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 63

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry views Psalm 63 as exemplifying devotion born from adversity, noting that "some of the sweetest of David's psalms were those that were penned, as this was, in a wilderness." The central theological theme emphasizes that **true spiritual longing focuses "not on the ordinances, but the God of the ordinances."** 

Henry identifies the psalm's structure as David's spiritual journey: desire towards God, esteem of God, satisfaction in God, secret communion with God, joyful dependence upon God, and holy triumph over enemies. The commentary presents the wilderness experience as a metaphor for spiritual desolation that can actually intensify genuine devotion.

## Key Practical Applications

**Worship in Adversity**: Henry emphasizes that "all the straits and difficulties of a wilderness must not put us out of tune for sacred songs." Christians should maintain "cheerful communion with God" regardless of circumstances.

**Private vs. Public Worship**: When deprived of public ordinances, believers should "desire and endeavour to keep up the same communion with God in our retirements." Henry notes that "a closet may be turned into a little sanctuary."

**Lifelong Gratitude**: The commentary stresses that "praising God must be the work of our whole lives," with believers maintaining thankfulness even "though the days come of which we say, We have no pleasure in them."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry highlights the progression from covenant relationship ("Thou art my God") to active pursuit ("Early will I seek thee"). The "dry and thirsty land" represents both physical wilderness and spiritual distance from God's ordinances.

**Verse 3**: The phrase "lovingkindness is better than life" receives significant attention. Henry explains this means God's favor is "better, a thousand times, to die in God's favour than to live under his wrath."

**Verses 5-6**: Henry emphasizes the "transcendent complacency in God" that provides satisfaction superior to worldly pleasures, comparing it to "marrow and fatness" versus "puddle-water."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to Isaiah 8:19 ("should not a people seek unto their God?"), 2 Corinthians 3:18 (beholding God's glory for transformation), Psalm 105:3 (hearts rejoicing in seeking the Lord), and Isaiah 25:6 (spiritual nourishment imagery). He also references examples of God's presence in exile through Ezekiel in Babylon and John on Patmos.

The commentary concludes with Henry's observation that "a devout and pious soul has little need of direction how to sing this psalm," while "an unsanctified soul...is scarcely capable of singing it with understanding," emphasizing the psalm's requirement for genuine spiritual experience.

Chapter 64
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 64: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 64 through the lens of spiritual warfare between "the seed of the woman against the seed of the serpent." He emphasizes God's sovereignty in protecting the righteous and judging the wicked, asserting that "the serpent's head will be broken, at last, to the honour and joy of the holy seed." 

The commentary presents divine justice as perfectly retributive, where punishment mirrors sin. Henry highlights God's omniscience against human attempts at secrecy, noting that "a practical disbelief of God's omniscience is at the bottom of all the wickedness of the wicked."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:
- **Prayer in persecution**: David's example shows believers should earnestly seek God's protection, even expressing fear without compromising courage
- **Understanding Providence**: Believers must "wisely consider" God's dealings to profit from them spiritually  
- **Testimony and witness**: Christians should "declare the work of God" to edify others and glorify Him
- **Holy confidence**: Observing God's justice should increase both joy in the Lord and trust in His protection

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes that fearing enemies isn't cowardly, citing Jacob's example. David prays for preservation "not only from the thing itself which I fear, but from the disquieting fear of it."

**Verses 3-4**: The tongue-as-sword metaphor reveals how "bitter words are their arrows—scurrilous reflections, opprobrious nicknames, false representations." Henry observes that "the better any man is the more he is envied by those that are themselves bad."

**Verses 7-8**: Divine retribution perfectly matches the crime: "They shot at David secretly and suddenly...but God shall shoot at them." Their own tongues become their downfall, demonstrating that "many have cut their own throats...with their tongues."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: Genesis 32:11 (Jacob's fear), Esther 7:2-4 (pleading for life), James 3:5 (tongue's power), Deuteronomy 27:24 (cursing secret smiters), Numbers 16:27 (fleeing from judgment), 1 Samuel 31:7 (Saul's defeat), Ecclesiastes 7:13 (considering God's work), and Psalm 119:120 (fearing God's judgments).

Henry's commentary presents this psalm as both historical record and prophetic template, showing how God's justice operates against persecutors while encouraging believers to trust in divine protection and declare God's righteous works.

Chapter 65
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 65 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry divides Psalm 65 into two primary manifestations of God's glory: the **kingdom of grace** (verses 1-5) and the **kingdom of providence** (verses 6-13). He emphasizes God's dual nature as both the God of grace who communes with believers and the God of nature who sovereignly controls creation.

**Grace and Salvation**: Henry highlights God as fundamentally a prayer-hearing, sin-pardoning God. He notes the profound theological truth that "iniquities prevail against us" yet God "purges them away" through His "own free mercy and for the sake of a righteousness of thy own providing."

**Divine Sovereignty**: The commentary emphasizes God's absolute control over both spiritual and natural realms - from pardoning sin to "girding with power" the mountains and stilling tempestuous seas.

## Key Practical Applications

**Prayer Life**: Henry encourages believers that since God is "a God hearing prayer," we should "come, and come boldly" to Him. He emphasizes that God "hears prayer" as one of His honored titles.

**Worship and Gratitude**: The concept that "praise waiteth for thee, O God, in Zion" teaches that genuine worship involves patient expectancy and readiness to receive God's mercies with thanksgiving. Henry warns that "we shall not be accepted in our thanksgivings to God for the mercies we have received unless we make conscience of paying the vows which we made."

**Communion with God**: Henry describes true blessing as being "chosen" by God and "caused to approach" Him, emphasizing that spiritual satisfaction comes from dwelling in God's courts rather than worldly pursuits.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry offers a striking interpretation of "praise waiteth" as "praise is silent unto thee" - describing worship that transcends words: "there are holy adorings which cannot be uttered, and yet shall be accepted by him that searches the heart."

**Verse 2**: The phrase "unto thee shall all flesh come" is interpreted both presently (in times of distress) and prophetically (when God's house becomes "a house of prayer to all people").

**Verse 4**: Henry beautifully describes God's house: "God keeps a good house. There is abundance of goodness in his house, righteousness, grace, and all the comforts of the everlasting covenant; there is enough for all, enough for each."

**Verse 11**: "Thou crownest the year with thy goodness" demonstrates God's providential care in the natural order.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Ephesians 3:20** - God's ability to do "more than we are able to ask or think"
- **Romans 10:12-13** - Universal access to God's grace
- **John 6:44** - God's drawing of believers to Himself
- **Isaiah 54:10** - God's covenant standing firmer than mountains
- **Psalm 107:27-29** - God's care for those "afar off upon the sea"
- **Revelation 14:1,3** - The redeemed singing on Mount Zion

## Key Memorable Phrases

Henry's eloquent language includes: "By faith and prayer we may keep up our communion with God, and fetch in comfort from him, wherever we are" and his description of satisfied souls: "Let them have the pleasure of communion with God, and that suffices them; they have enough, they desire no more."

The commentary concludes with a vivid picture of creation itself praising God, where "the pastures are clothed with flocks" and valleys "shout for joy, they also sing," demonstrating God's comprehensive care over both spiritual and material realms.

Chapter 66
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 66: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry views Psalm 66 as a "thanksgiving-psalm" with **three progressive movements**: universal praise for God's sovereign dominion (vv. 1-7), special gratitude for God's favor to His covenant people (vv. 8-12), and personal testimony of answered prayer (vv. 13-20). 

A central theological theme is **God's universal sovereignty**. The call for "all lands" to praise God reflects both God's worthiness to be praised by all creation and "a prediction of the conversion of the Gentiles to the faith of Christ." Henry sees prophetic significance: "the time should come when all lands should praise God" through the gospel.

Another key theme is **divine providence in affliction**. Henry emphasizes that trials serve God's refining purposes: "By afflictions we are proved as silver in the fire" to make our graces "more evident" and "more strong and active."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Public and heartfelt worship**: Believers "must be hearty and zealous" and "open and public" in praise, "not ashamed of our Master." The "joyful noise" represents both zealous devotion and public testimony.

**Observing God's works**: "The reason why we do not praise him more and better is because we do not duly and attentively observe" God's works. Christians must actively "come and see the works of God" to fuel their worship.

**Perspective in trials**: When facing affliction, believers should recognize God's hand: "We are never in the net but God brings us into it, never under affliction but God lays it upon us," understanding that trials serve to prove and refine faith.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 3**: Henry notes that enemies may "submit themselves" to God through fear, but "subjection extorted by fear is seldom sincere" - highlighting the difference between forced compliance and genuine faith.

**Verse 6**: The Red Sea crossing becomes a model for corporate memory: "There did we rejoice; that is, our ancestors did, and we in their loins. The joys of our fathers were our joys."

**Verse 9**: God "holdeth our soul in life" through "continued creation" - His providence represents "a constant renewed act" that "upholds us in our being."

**Verse 18**: "If I regard iniquity in my heart, the Lord will not hear me" - Henry emphasizes that unconfessed sin blocks prayer, making this a crucial principle for effective intercession.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: **Exodus 15:11** ("fearful in praises"), **Isaiah 43:1** (God's presence through fire and water), **1 Peter 1:7** (trials proving faith), **Revelation 14:6-7** (universal gospel proclamation), **Hebrews 12:10** (partaking in God's holiness through discipline), and **Matthew 10:17** ("Beware of men").

The commentary demonstrates Henry's method of interpreting individual psalms within the broader biblical narrative, seeing both immediate historical application and prophetic fulfillment in the gospel age.

Chapter 67
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalms 67: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 67 as prophetically anticipating the expansion of God's kingdom from Israel to encompass all nations. The commentary emphasizes three progressive movements: **prayer for Israel's prosperity, prayer for Gentile conversion, and a prophetic vision of universal blessing**. Henry sees this psalm as foretelling "the glorious estate of the Christian church, in which Jews and Gentiles should unite in one flock."

The central theological theme is the **universality of God's salvation**. Henry notes how Old Testament saints desired to share rather than monopolize church privileges, contrasting this with the exclusivist spirit of Jews in Christ's time who "were enraged at the preaching of the gospel to the Gentiles."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Corporate Prayer**: The psalmist prays "God be merciful to us" rather than "to me," teaching that "we ought to pray with and for others" and "be willing and glad to take our lot with them."

**Missionary Vision**: Believers should be "public-spirited" in prayer, desiring that God's way and salvation "may be known to all" nations. Those who have "experimentally known the pleasantness of God's ways" cannot help but pray for others to experience the same.

**Comprehensive Blessing**: Henry emphasizes that "God bless us is a comprehensive prayer; it is a pity such excellent words should ever be used slightly and carelessly, and as a byword."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry identifies three progressive elements of divine blessing: mercy (pardoning sins), blessing (conferring promises), and God's shining face (tokens of favor). He notes the margin reading "shine with us" suggests God crowning our endeavors with success.

**Verses 2-5**: The prayer for Gentile conversion encompasses both God's "way" (rule of duty from law) and "saving health" (salvation from gospel). Henry emphasizes the repeated call for praise, noting it reflects the psalmist's heart being "very much upon" universal worship.

**Verses 6-7**: The earth yielding increase represents both material blessing and spiritual transformation, where "the meat and the drink then became a meat-offering and a drink-offering to the Lord."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout:

- **Luke 18:13** - for God's mercy to sinners
- **Zechariah 8:23** - nations joining God's people 
- **Deuteronomy 4:8** - Israel's righteous statutes as the envy of neighbors
- **Psalm 50:23** - God showing salvation to those who walk in His way
- **Acts 14:17** - God's provision even to those in darkness
- **Matthew 28:19** - discipling all nations
- **Revelation 11:17-18** - God's reign and the nations' response

Henry particularly emphasizes how "many scripture-prophecies and promises are wrapped up in prayers, to intimate that the answer of the church's prayer is as sure as the performance of God's promises."

The commentary concludes with the vision that gospel success brings both earthly prosperity and heavenly favor, ultimately drawing "all the ends of the earth" to worship God.

Chapter 68
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 68 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies this psalm as both historical and prophetic, beginning with David's transportation of the ark to Zion but extending to "speak glorious things concerning the Messiah, his ascension into heaven, and the setting up of his kingdom in the world." The psalm presents God as simultaneously transcendent ("He rides upon the heavens by his name JAH") and compassionate, emphasizing how "God's goodness is his glory."

The commentary highlights God's sovereignty over enemies - both historical foes of Israel and spiritual enemies of Christ's kingdom. Henry connects the psalm's opening words to Moses' prayer for the ark's movement (Numbers 10:35), showing continuity between Old Testament worship and Messianic fulfillment.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For the afflicted:** God serves as "a Father of the fatherless, and a judge of the widows," providing comfort to those who have lost family leadership. "Happy are those that have an interest in such a God as this."

**For worship:** Believers should "rejoice in God" with "exceeding joy," understanding that "Holy joy in God will very well consist with that reverence and godly fear wherewith we ought to worship him."

**For prayer:** While Christians should pray for personal enemies, "we are to pray against God's enemies as such, against their enmity to him and all their attempts upon his kingdom."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2:** Henry interprets the scattering of enemies as smoke driven by wind and wax melting before fire, looking toward final judgment when ungodly men "shall melt like wax before that flaming fire."

**Verse 4:** The phrase about God riding "upon the heavens by his name JAH" demonstrates His supreme command over celestial influences and His swift help for His people.

**Verses 5-6:** Henry emphasizes the beautiful contrast between God's cosmic power and intimate care: "He that rides on the heavens...rather glories in, that he is a Father of the fatherless."

**Verses 7-8:** The wilderness journey and Sinai's shaking serve as encouragement that "he that could move Sinai itself could remove" any obstacles facing God's people.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- Numbers 10:35 (ark's movement)
- 2 Peter 3:7 and 2 Thessalonians 1:8 (final judgment)
- Deuteronomy 33:26 (God riding heavens for His people's help)
- Multiple Psalm references (9:4,7; 146:9; 10:14,18)
- Isaiah 45:8 and Psalm 72:6 (Christ as spiritual rain)

The commentary demonstrates how "Fresh mercies should put us in mind of former mercies" and shows the psalm's fulfillment in both historical Israel and the church age.

Chapter 69
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 69: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry emphasizes that **David serves as a type of Christ** throughout this psalm. He notes that "divers passages in this psalm are applied to Christ in the New Testament" (verses 4, 9, 21, 22), making this psalm prophetic of Christ's sufferings and subsequent glory. Like Psalm 22, "it begins with the humiliation and ends with the exaltation of Christ." 

The psalm follows a three-part structure: complaints and petitions for relief (vv. 1-21), imprecations against persecutors (vv. 22-29), and concluding praise with assurance of God's help (vv. 30-36). Henry interprets the imprecations as **predictions of judgment** upon Christ's enemies, particularly referencing the destruction of the Jewish nation for persecuting Christ.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**On bearing suffering**: When facing unjust persecution, we should "bear it patiently, because then we suffer as Christ did." He emphasizes keeping troubles from overwhelming our hearts: "We shall bear up under our troubles if we can but keep them from our hearts."

**On maintaining faith during trials**: Even when prayer seems ineffective and hope fails, believers must persist. David's "throat is dried, but his heart is not; his eyes fail, but his faith does not."

**On confession**: "Even when, as to men's unjust accusations, we plead Not guilty, yet, before God, we must acknowledge ourselves to have deserved all that is brought upon us." This represents "the genuine confession of a penitent."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: The "waters of affliction" represent troubles that "not only threaten my life, but disquiet my mind," pointing to Christ's soul-sufferings when he became "an offering for sin."

**Verse 4**: "They hate me without a cause" - directly applied by Jesus to himself (John 15:25), demonstrating the fulfillment of prophetic scripture.

**Verse 9**: "The zeal of thine house hath eaten me up" - Henry connects this to Christ's devotion and the reproaches he bore for God's sake.

**Verses 10-12**: Henry notes the irony that David was mocked for his acts of devotion - fasting, wearing sackcloth, and humbling himself before God became sources of ridicule from both the honorable ("those that sit in the gate") and the despicable (drunkards).

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to New Testament passages:
- **John 15:25** - Christ's application of verse 4 to himself
- **John 7:5** and **John 1:11** - regarding Christ being rejected by his own
- **Hebrews 5:7** - Christ being "heard in that he feared"
- **2 Timothy 3:3** and **Hosea 7:5** - connecting drunkenness to scorning the righteous

He also references **Psalm 22** as a parallel structure of humiliation followed by exaltation, and **Job 30:8** regarding the "children of base men" who mock the righteous.

Henry concludes that believers should view this psalm through the lens of "the sufferings of Christ, and the glory that followed, not forgetting the sufferings of Christians too, and the glory that shall follow them."

Chapter 70
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 70: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 70 as **"a salve for every sore"** - a universal prayer adapted for any state of affliction. He notes this psalm is copied almost word-for-word from Psalm 40's final verses, emphasizing its title "to bring to remembrance." Henry explains this remembrance serves two purposes: putting God in remembrance of His mercy and promises through faithful prayer, and putting believers in remembrance of former afflictions and devotions to prepare for future trials.

The commentary emphasizes God's sovereignty over timing, noting that "sometimes God seems to delay helping his own people, that he may excite such earnest desires." Henry distinguishes between faithful urgency and faithless haste, explaining that true believers don't "outrun the divine counsels" but may earnestly seek God's hastened intervention.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry identifies three primary prayer requests that apply to all believers:
1. **Help for ourselves** in times of distress
2. **Shame for enemies** - both for their repentance and the frustration of their evil designs  
3. **Joy for God's friends** who seek Him faithfully

He emphasizes that **poverty and necessity are "very good pleas in prayer to a God of infinite mercy."** The commentary encourages believers to reuse previous prayers with "new affections," following Christ's example of praying "the same words" during His agony.

Henry calls believers to make "the service of God our great business and the favour of God our great delight," promising that faithful seekers will find "the joy of the Lord shall fill our minds and the high praises of the Lord shall fill our mouths."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1 & 5**: Henry highlights David's repeated urgency ("Make haste") as legitimate spiritual desperation, grounded in recognition of God as "my help and my deliverer."

**Verses 2-3**: The enemies are described as seeking both physical destruction ("his soul—his life") and spiritual corruption ("to draw him from God to sin and to despair"). Their mocking "Aha, aha!" reveals their spiteful insolence.

**Verse 4**: Henry emphasizes that those who "seek God and love his salvation" will have "continual matter for joy and praise." He notes that loving God's salvation enough to "cheerfully quit all rather than hazard our salvation" provides "good evidence of our interest in it."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves in several biblical connections:
- **Isaiah 43:26** - regarding putting God in remembrance through prayer
- **Psalm 83:16** - enemies being brought to seek God's name through shame
- **Isaiah 26:11** - enemies being ashamed of their envy
- **Psalm 105:3** - the joy of seeking God as evidence of His good-will
- Reference to Christ's threefold prayer in Gethsemane as precedent for repeated prayers

The commentary positions this psalm within the broader biblical theme that "God is the rewarder of all those that diligently seek him," making it both deeply personal and universally applicable for Christian devotion.

Chapter 71
----------------------------------------
# Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 71

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 71 as distinctly suited for "old disciples of Jesus Christ," written by David in his declining years, possibly during Absalom's rebellion. The psalm embodies two primary movements: **believing prayers** (verses 1-13) and **believing praises** (verses 14-24). Henry emphasizes that God's faithfulness extends throughout life's stages, from youth to old age, making this psalm particularly relevant for believers facing the vulnerabilities of aging.

The central theological theme revolves around **covenant faithfulness**—God as an unchanging refuge who "will not cast off old servants." Henry stresses that true faith involves "dealing upon trust" with God, where believers find their ultimate security not in human strength but in divine promises.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living:

**For Aging Believers**: The commentary emphasizes that God's care in infancy should "engage us to an early piety and constant devotedness to his honour." He encourages believers that "the faithful servants of God may be comfortably assured that he will not cast them off in old age."

**For Prayer Life**: Henry advocates for bold approach to God's throne, noting that "every true believer may come boldly" because "God will never disappoint the hope that is of his own raising."

**For Praise**: The psalm teaches believers to desire continuous worship, "not only seven times a day but all the day, to intermix with all they say something or other that may redound to the honour and praise of God."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 3**: "Be thou my strong habitation" - Henry explains this as finding a home in God through "a life of communion with him and confidence in him," always welcome to "resort to him continually."

**Verses 5-6**: Henry highlights how David's lifelong trust stems from God's care "from the womb," noting that "he that was our help from our birth ought to be our hope from our youth."

**Verse 9**: The prayer "Cast me not off in the time of old age" receives particular attention as Henry discusses the "natural sense of the infirmities of age" while affirming God's continued presence.

**Verse 18**: Henry sees David's desire to "show thy strength unto this generation" as a call for elderly believers to mentor others, demonstrating God's faithfulness across generations.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves several scriptural connections throughout his commentary:

- **1 Corinthians 10:13** - Supporting the promise that "God will, with the temptation, make a way to escape"
- **Psalm 116:16** - Referencing covenant relationship as "a son of thy handmaid, and born in thy house"
- **Psalm 119:164** - Connecting to the theme of continual praise
- **Psalm 90:10** - Acknowledging the struggles of aging
- **Isaiah 36:10** - Comparing enemies' false claims about divine abandonment

Henry concludes that this psalm demonstrates how faith transforms both present struggles and future hopes, showing believers how to move from complaint to praise through confident trust in God's unchanging character. The commentary presents Psalm 71 as a masterclass in mature faith that sustains believers through life's inevitable transitions and trials.

Chapter 72
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 72: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 72 as fundamentally **Messianic**, viewing Solomon's reign as a type and figure of Christ's eternal kingdom. While written as David's prayer for his successor Solomon, Henry emphasizes that "Christ's kingdom is here prophesied of under the type and figure of Solomon's." The commentary establishes that many passages "cannot be applied to the reign of Solomon" since Solomon's kingdom was temporary while this psalm describes an eternal reign "as long as the sun."

The central theological theme is **Christ as the righteous King** who establishes a government based on divine justice, peace, and protection of the vulnerable. Henry sees this as fulfillment of God's promise to David that "of the fruit of his loins, according to the flesh, he would raise up Christ to sit on his throne."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For Parents and Leaders**: David's prayer models how "the best thing we can ask of God for our children is that God will give them wisdom and grace to know and do their duty; that is better than gold." Parents should prioritize spiritual legacy over material inheritance.

**For Citizens**: The commentary emphasizes that "those who would live quiet and peaceable lives must pray for kings and all in authority, that God would give them his judgments and righteousness."

**For the Church**: Henry stresses concern for future generations, criticizing those who "have little love either to God or man, and are of a very narrow selfish spirit, who care not what becomes of the world and the church when they are gone."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry interprets the opening prayer three ways - as a father's blessing, a king's concern for succession, and the church's prayer for their sovereign.

**Verses 2-4**: The emphasis on justice for the poor reveals that "Christ will be sure to carry his cause on behalf of his injured poor" and "Christ is the poor man's King."

**Verse 6**: The beautiful metaphor of rain on mown grass represents how "the gospel of Christ distils as the rain, which softens the ground that was hard, moistens that which was dry."

**Verse 14**: Henry notes that Christ values his people's lives so highly that "precious shall their blood be in his sight" - no suffering goes unnoticed or uncompensated.

## Cross-References and Scripture Connections

Henry skillfully weaves numerous biblical references throughout:

- **Acts 2:30** - connecting to God's promise about David's lineage
- **Psalm 2:6-7** - linking to Christ as God's Son and appointed King  
- **Isaiah 11:4** - paralleling the righteous judgment theme
- **2 Chronicles 9:8** - showing God's purpose in establishing righteous rulers
- **Revelation 14:7** - connecting to the eternal gospel and fear of God

## Memorable Phrases and Key Quotes

Henry concludes with the powerful reminder that "in singing this psalm we must have an eye to Christ, praising him as a King, and pleasing ourselves with our happiness as his subjects." His commentary transforms an ancient royal prayer into a living testament to Christ's eternal reign, where righteousness, peace, and divine justice converge in the ultimate kingdom that "shall endure forever."

The commentary masterfully balances historical context with prophetic fulfillment, showing how David's earthly concerns for good government find their ultimate expression in Christ's eternal, righteous rule over all nations and generations.

Chapter 73
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 73: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 73 as addressing one of faith's most challenging trials—the temptation to envy the prosperity of the wicked. The psalm establishes a foundational theological principle in verse 1: **"Truly God is good to Israel, even to such as are of a clean heart."** Henry emphasizes that this sacred principle anchors the believer during spiritual struggle.

The commentary identifies **God's distinguishing goodness** as the central theme, noting that "those are the Israel of God that are of a clean heart, purified by the blood of Christ." Henry stresses that despite apparent contradictions in Providence, believers must hold fast to the truth that **"God, who is good to all, is in a special manner good to his church and people."**

## Key Practical Applications

Henry structures the psalm around three movements that provide practical guidance:
1. **How the psalmist got into temptation** (verses 2-14)
2. How he gained victory over it (verses 15-20) 
3. How he became better through it (verses 21-23)

The commentary warns that **"the faith even of strong believers may sometimes be sorely shaken and ready to fail them"** while encouraging that **"many a precious soul, that shall live for ever, had once a very narrow turn for its life."** Henry advocates using this psalm to **"fortify ourselves against the like temptation,"** emphasizing that **"the experiences of others should be our instructions."**

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 2-3**: Henry notes the psalmist's honest confession—**"my feet were almost gone; my steps had well-nigh slipped"**—showing how proximity to spiritual disaster paradoxically demonstrates divine grace in preservation.

**Verses 4-7**: The commentary details how the wicked appear exempt from common troubles, living with such excess **"their eyes stand out with fatness"** and having **"more than heart could wish."**

**Verse 9**: Henry powerfully describes their blasphemy: **"They set their mouth against the heavens...their tongue also walks through the earth"**—unable to reach heaven with their hands to shake God's throne, they show their ill-will through words.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry enriches his interpretation with extensive biblical connections:

- **Job 24:1** and **Jeremiah 12:1** for similar struggles with divine justice
- **Habakkuk 1:13** on God's holiness as an anchor during doubt  
- **2 Chronicles 29:30** establishing Asaph's role as seer and psalmist
- **Job 21:23-25** comparing the deaths of the wicked and righteous
- **Genesis 6:11-13** linking contemporary oppression to antediluvian violence

The commentary demonstrates how this ancient struggle with theodicy connects believers across Scripture, providing both historical precedent and theological framework for understanding divine goodness amid apparent contradictions in Providence.

Chapter 74
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 74: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets this psalm as describing Jerusalem's destruction by Nebuchadnezzar and the Chaldeans, though he notes uncertainty about authorship—whether by David/Asaph prophetically, or by a later writer during the captivity. The psalm follows a three-part structure: **complaining pleas** (vv. 1-11), **comfortable pleas** for encouragement (vv. 12-17), and **petitions for deliverance** (vv. 18-23).

The central theological theme emphasizes looking beyond human instruments of suffering to God's sovereignty. Henry notes how the people "look above the instruments of their trouble...and keep their eye upon God, by whose determined counsel they were delivered up."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

- **Priority of spiritual concerns**: The psalmist complains only about damage to sanctuary and synagogues, not secular losses. "The concerns of religion should lie nearer our hearts and affect us more than any worldly concern whatsoever."

- **Proper response to affliction**: The psalm teaches "that when we are, upon any account, in distress, it is our wisdom and duty to apply to God by faithful and fervent prayer."

- **Perspective on suffering**: "The people of God must not think that because they are cast down they are therefore cast off...because he seems to cast them off for a time therefore they are really cast off for ever."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry addresses the bold question "Why hast thou cast us off forever?" comparing it to Christ's cry of abandonment, showing this represents "present dark and melancholy apprehensions" rather than theological reality.

**Verses 2-3**: The people plead their special relationship with God—"the sheep of thy pasture," "thy congregation," and "the rod of thy inheritance"—emphasizing both God's investment in them and their proximity to Him through Mount Zion.

**Verses 4-7**: Henry vividly describes the enemies' desecration: they "roared in the midst of God's congregations" and destroyed the temple's "carved work" with the same casual brutality as "woodcutters...hewing trees in the forest."

**Verse 9**: The absence of prophets represents the greatest distress—"no prophets, none to tell us how long"—showing their concern for divine communication over military strength.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical passages:

- **Romans 11:1** ("Has God cast away his people? God forbid") to counter despair about permanent rejection
- **Deuteronomy 29:24** regarding God's smoking anger
- **Isaiah 43:3-4** concerning God's costly redemption of Israel
- **Daniel 9:17** on sanctuary desolations
- **Lamentations 3:21** about hope through remembrance
- **2 Chronicles 36:19** and **Matthew 24:2** describing temple destruction

Henry concludes that this psalm applies to "any present distresses or desolations of any part of the Christian church," making it relevant beyond its historical context for ongoing spiritual struggles.

Chapter 75
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 75: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 75 as David's response to his accession to the throne after Saul's death, though attributed to Asaph. The central theological theme is **God's sovereign dominion over all earthly powers**. Henry emphasizes that "promotion cometh neither from the east, nor from the west, nor from the south" but from God alone, who "putteth down one, and setteth up another." 

A second major theme is **divine judgment**, illustrated through the metaphor of God's cup of wrath. Henry explains that while all may taste from this cup, "the dregs thereof, all the wicked of the earth shall wring them out, and drink them," emphasizing that ultimate judgment belongs to God alone.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for both rulers and citizens:

**For Leaders**: Those in authority must remember that "posts of honour" are actually "posts of service." David's promise to "judge uprightly" demonstrates that public trusts must be managed with integrity. Henry notes that when David declares "When I shall receive the congregation I will judge uprightly," he commits to active, righteous governance rather than taking "ease" or "state."

**For Citizens**: Henry warns against both foolishness and pride. To "the fools" he counsels "Deal not foolishly," while to "the wicked" he warns "Lift not up the horn" and "speak not with a stiff neck." These represent warnings against both passive corruption and active rebellion against God-ordained authority.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry emphasizes the repetition in "unto thee do we give thanks, unto thee do we give thanks," noting that "our thanksgivings must be often repeated" just as prayers are repeated when seeking mercy.

**Verse 3**: The phrase "I bear up the pillars of it" leads Henry to observe how "much one head frequently holds up" in preventing societal collapse. He applies this christologically, noting that "Christ bore up the pillars" when "the world and all inhabitants were dissolved by sin."

**Verses 6-7**: Henry provides both negative and positive aspects of divine sovereignty. Promotion comes not from human direction or astrological forces, but "God is the judge" who acts "by prerogative" according to his "infinitely wise, holy, and good" character.

## Cross-References and Applications

Henry connects this psalm to broader biblical themes through several references:

- **Hebrews 1:3**: Linking David's role in upholding society to Christ who "upholds all things by the word of his power"
- **Isaiah 40:4**: Connecting the warning against pride to the prophecy that "every mountain will be brought low" before Christ
- **Matthew 20:22 and John 18:11**: Relating God's cup of judgment to Christ's cup of suffering
- **Revelation 15:7, 16:1**: Connecting the imagery of God's cup to the apocalyptic vials of wrath

Henry concludes with the practical application that "in singing this psalm we must give to God the glory of all the revolutions of states and kingdoms, believing that they are all according to his counsel and that he will make them all to work for the good of his church."
