The commentary ultimately presents Psalm 75 as both a historical celebration of David's righteous kingship and a typological foreshadowing of Christ's eternal kingdom, where divine justice and mercy perfectly converge.

Chapter 76
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 76 - Summary

## Historical Context and Purpose
Matthew Henry suggests this psalm commemorates a great military victory for Israel, possibly Sennacherib's army being destroyed during Hezekiah's reign or the triumph in Jehoshaphat's time. He notes it serves as "a psalm proper for a thanksgiving day" and sees these Old Testament victories as types of Christ's victory over "the powers of darkness."

## Main Theological Themes

**God's Special Presence with His People (vv. 1-3)**
Henry emphasizes the privilege of divine revelation: "It is the honour and privilege of Judah and Israel that among them God is known, and where he is known his name will be great." He highlights how God dwelt among them through his ordinances - "there he kept court; there he received the homage of his people."

**Divine Sovereignty Over Human Power (vv. 4-6)**
The commentary celebrates God's supremacy over earthly rulers, described as "mountains of prey, oppressive to all about them." Henry notes how God can render the mightiest enemies powerless: "The stouthearted have despoiled and disarmed themselves" and "the strong men are lamed, and cannot so much as find their hands."

## Key Practical Applications

**Fear and Reverence of God (vv. 7-9)**
Henry draws the lesson that "all the world learn by this event to stand in awe of the great God." He emphasizes God's protection of "the meek of the earth" - those who "can bear any wrong, but do none."

**Trust in Divine Providence (v. 10)**
A central application concerns God's sovereignty over human anger: "Surely the wrath of man shall praise thee: the remainder of wrath shalt thou restrain." Henry explains this means God either uses human wrath for His purposes or restrains what won't serve His glory.

**Covenant Faithfulness (vv. 11-12)**
Believers should "Vow, and pay unto the LORD your God" - maintaining faithful allegiance and bringing "presents" of prayers, praises, and hearts to God.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 2**: God's dwelling is "a tabernacle, a movable dwelling. Yet a little while is that light with us" - highlighting the temporary nature of God's institutional presence.

**Verse 8**: "When God is speaking judgment from heaven it is time for the earth to compose itself into an awful and reverent silence."

**Verse 12**: God "shall cut off the spirit of princes; he shall slip it off as easily as we slip off a flower from the stalk" - emphasizing how effortlessly God humbles the proud.

## Cross-References and Connections

Henry connects this psalm to numerous scriptures:
- **Psalm 147:19-20** on Israel's unique privilege of knowing God
- **2 Chronicles 20:5,14** linking sanctuary prayer to military success
- **Psalm 20:7** on trusting chariots versus trusting God
- **Revelation 11:17-18** on heavenly thanksgiving for God's reign
- **Isaiah 37:29** on God restraining Sennacherib's rage

## Christological Application

Henry concludes by applying the psalm to Christ: "It is through him that God is known; it is in him that God's name is great... He it was that vanquished the strong man armed, spoiled principalities and powers, and made a show of them openly." This demonstrates his consistent typological interpretation of Old Testament victories as prefiguring Christ's ultimate triumph.

The commentary presents this psalm as both historical celebration and prophetic anticipation of God's ultimate victory through the Messiah.

Chapter 77
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 77: Summary

## **Theological Themes and Interpretations**

Henry interprets this psalm as a spiritual journey from despair to hope, presenting "the lively portraiture of a good man under prevailing melancholy." The central theological tension explores whether God has permanently abandoned His people or if temporary darkness precedes restoration. Henry emphasizes that "spiritual trouble is of all trouble most grievous to a gracious soul" and that even faithful believers may face seasons where God appears absent.

The psalm's structure moves from "sorrowful complaints" to "comfortable encouragements," teaching that personal struggles should be viewed through the lens of God's historical faithfulness to His church. Henry notes this reflects how "particular believers" can find comfort in what "God has wrought for his church in general."

## **Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons for struggling believers:

**On Prayer in Distress**: "Days of trouble must be days of prayer, days of inward trouble especially." He emphasizes that those "under trouble of mind must not think to drink it away, or laugh it away, but must pray it away."

**On Refusing Comfort**: Henry warns that those in sorrow "do not only prejudice themselves, but affront God, if they refuse to be comforted," highlighting the danger of indulging in melancholy.

**On Fighting Unbelief**: When overwhelmed by doubts, believers must "argue down the insurrections of unbelief" and recognize that "despondency of spirit, and distrust of God, under affliction, are too often the infirmities of good people."

## **Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 1-4**: Henry describes the psalmist's sleepless anguish, noting how "grief never preys so much upon the spirits as when it is thus smothered and pent up."

**Verses 7-9**: These questioning verses represent "the language of a disconsolate deserted soul," but Henry provides biblical answers to each despairing question, showing that God's mercy endures forever and His promises never fail.

**Verse 10**: The turning point comes with self-recognition: "This is my infirmity." Henry explains this as acknowledging that "these doubts and fears proceed from the want and weakness of faith."

**Verses 11-20**: The remedy involves deliberately remembering "the years of the right hand of the Most High," focusing on God's supernatural interventions in Israel's history, particularly the Exodus.

## **Cross-References and Biblical Connections**

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout, including:
- James 5:13 on prayer in affliction
- Romans 11:1 and Psalm 94:14 against God's permanent rejection
- Lamentations 3:32 on God's compassion
- Hebrews 6:18 on God's unchanging promises
- Job's struggles with God's presence (Job 23:15)

He particularly emphasizes how the psalm's structure mirrors other biblical patterns of complaint followed by comfort, and connects the historical remembrance to the broader scriptural theme of God's faithfulness across generations.

The commentary concludes that believers should "take shame to ourselves for all our sinful distrusts of God" while giving Him "glory of his power and goodness by a thankful commemoration of what he has done."

Chapter 78
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 78: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Historical Instruction for Spiritual Formation**: Henry presents Psalm 78 as "Maschil—a psalm to give instruction," emphasizing that Israel's history serves as both warning and encouragement. The psalm demonstrates God's faithfulness despite human unfaithfulness, showing how "good God had been to them" while exposing "how basely they had conducted themselves towards God."

**Intergenerational Faith Transmission**: A central theme is the divine mandate to pass down religious knowledge. Henry notes God's design that "as one generation of God's servants and worshippers passes away, another generation may come, and the church...may abide for ever." This ensures "God's name among men may be as the days of heaven."

**Divine Patience and Justice**: The commentary reveals God's character through His repeated mercy toward rebellious Israel, forgiving them "formerly" and returning "in mercy to them, notwithstanding their provocations," while also justly punishing their sins.

## Key Practical Applications

**Parental Responsibility**: Henry emphasizes that parents must transmit both "the law of God" and "the providences of God" to their children. "Our great care must be to lodge our religion, that great deposit, pure and entire in the hands of those that succeed us."

**Learning from Ancestral Failures**: The commentary warns against following negative examples: "Those that have descended from wicked and ungodly ancestors...will see reason enough not to tread in their steps." Past failures should serve as "admonition to us."

**Genuine vs. Counterfeit Faith**: Henry warns against superficial repentance, noting how Israel "did but counterfeit repentance and submission when he punished them." True faith requires hearts "set aright" and spirits "stedfast with God."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-8**: The preface demands attention because these are "weighty" matters deserving consideration. Henry calls them "dark sayings," not because they're hard to understand, but because "they are greatly to be admired and carefully to be looked into."

**The Ephraim Reference (v.9)**: Though the text is truncated, Henry uses Israel's military failures as emblematic of spiritual cowardice—being "armed" yet turning back due to spiritual unfaithfulness.

**Wilderness Provocations (vv.17-22)**: Henry highlights how Israel "tempted God in their heart" and questioned His provision despite witnessing His power. Their fundamental sin was unbelief: "they believed not in God, and trusted not in his salvation."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to **1 Corinthians 10:11** and **Hebrews 4:11**, noting that "These things happened unto them for ensamples." He references **Matthew 13:35**, showing how Christ's parables fulfilled the psalm's prophecy about speaking in parables.

The commentary links to **Deuteronomy 6:7, 20** regarding parental instruction duties, and **1 Peter 1:18** warning against following corrupt traditions. **Revelation 2:7** appears in the context of spiritual hearing: "Let him that has an ear thus hear what the Spirit saith unto the churches."

Henry presents this psalm as essential instruction where "the particulars also may be of use to us, for warning against those sins of unbelief and ingratitude which Israel of old was notoriously guilty of."

Chapter 79
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 79: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 79 as relating to **Jerusalem's destruction by the Babylonians under Nebuchadnezzar**, noting its connection to Jeremiah's Lamentations. The psalm represents the church's suffering "not only with [the world], but from it, as a lily among thorns." 

Central theological themes include:
- **Divine sovereignty over nations**: God is "King of nations, to overrule them...and King of saints, to favour and protect them"
- **The relationship between sin and judgment**: God's people had first defiled the temple through sin, so "God suffered their enemies to defile it by their insolence"
- **God's covenant faithfulness**: The land is precious as "God's inheritance" rather than merely their own possession

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that **"injuries done to religion should grieve us more than even those done to common right."** Believers should be more concerned with God's honor than personal suffering.

The commentary stresses proper prayer priorities: **"we should be more earnest with God in prayer for the removal of our sins than for the removal of our afflictions."** Deliverance means nothing without forgiveness as its foundation.

For times of church prosperity, this psalm should inspire gratitude that "we are not thus trampled on and insulted," while in persecution it serves "for the exciting of our desires towards God and the encouragement of our faith."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: The enemies' systematic destruction of places (holy land, city, temple) and persons reveals their complete malice. They **"shed blood with no more reluctancy...than if they had spilt so much water,"** yet God will reckon for every drop.

**Verses 6-7**: The prayer against enemies targets those **"who have not known God, nor called upon his name"** - their ignorance of God explains their neglect of prayer.

**Verses 8-9**: The plea **"remember not against us former iniquities"** encompasses both personal past sins and ancestral guilt, showing how repentance can "cut off the entail of the parents' sin."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to **Jeremiah 10:25**, where the prophet borrows verses 6-7. He references **Psalm 78:55** to show the contrast between God's past favor and present judgment.

Other key connections include **Deuteronomy 21:23** (regarding proper treatment of bodies), **Revelation 11:9** (persecution of witnesses), and **Jeremiah 31:29-30** (God's promise not to punish children for parents' sins).

The commentary emphasizes that **"the best encouragements in prayer are those that are taken from God only"** - believers must appeal to God's character and promises rather than their own merit when seeking deliverance.

Chapter 80
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 80: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Henry interprets this psalm as a **lament for God's people under divine displeasure**, likely written during the captivity of the northern tribes. The central theological framework revolves around **covenant relationship** - God as Shepherd, Israel as His flock and vineyard. Henry emphasizes that "the public interests of God's Israel ought to lie nearer to our hearts than any secular interest of our own."

The psalm develops a crucial **conversion theology**: "No salvation but from God's favour... No obtaining favour with God unless we be converted to him... No conversion to God but by his own grace." Henry stresses that restoration requires divine initiative, as believers must pray "Turn thou me, and I shall be turned."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for corporate worship and national spiritual life:

**Prayer in Corporate Distress**: The psalm "is proper to be sung in the day of Jacob's trouble," emphasizing communal prayer during national crises. Believers should "express a true love to the church and a hearty concern for its interest."

**National Conversion**: Henry advocates for systematic spiritual renewal: "The prayer here is for a national conversion; in this method we must pray for national mercies, that what is amiss may be amended... National holiness would secure national happiness."

**Perseverance in Prayer**: When God seems "angry against the prayer of his people," Henry counsels examining motives and methods, as God may "try their patience and perseverance in prayer."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry highlights God's role as "Shepherd of Israel" who "dwells between the cherubim," emphasizing the mercy-seat as "the propitiatory" where believers approach "a throne of grace."

**Verses 8-16**: The **vine metaphor** receives extensive treatment. Henry notes the vine is praised for "its shadow, its boughs, and its branches, but not a word of its fruit, for Israel was an empty vine." This becomes a warning: "if a vine do not bring forth fruit, no tree so useless, so worthless."

**Verse 16**: "They perish not through the rage of the wild beast... but at the rebuke of thy countenance" - Henry emphasizes divine sovereignty over all circumstances.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive biblical connections throughout:
- **John 15:5** (Christ as vine, believers as branches)
- **Romans 11:18** (Christ as root of the vine)
- **James 4:3** (asking amiss in prayer)
- **Jeremiah 2:21** (degenerate vine imagery)
- **Isaiah 5:2-5** (God seeking fruit from His vineyard)
- **Job 1:10** (God's protective hedge)
- **Hosea 5:4** and **Proverbs 1:23** (concerning conversion)

Henry concludes with the repeated refrain "Turn us again... cause thy face to shine; and we shall be saved," emphasizing that divine favor remains the ultimate source of restoration for God's people.

Chapter 81
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 81

### **Main Theological Themes**

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 81 as a liturgical piece designed for temple worship during the feast of trumpets or new moon celebrations. The psalm serves dual purposes: **"to give glory to God and to receive instruction from God, to 'behold the beauty of the Lord and to enquire in his temple.'"** 

The central theological theme emphasizes God's covenant relationship with Israel, highlighting Him as **"our strength"** and **"the God of Jacob"** - both protector of the nation and enabler of individual believers. Henry stresses that God remains faithful despite human unfaithfulness, demonstrating divine patience through Israel's wilderness provocations.

### **Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian worship and life:

**Worship Must Be Intentional:** Believers "do not go to church to sleep nor to be idle" - worship requires active participation with both **"cheerfulness and joy with reverence and godly fear."**

**Remembrance as Spiritual Discipline:** Just as Israel recalled their Egyptian deliverance on feast days, **"much more ought we, on the Christian sabbath, to call to mind a more glorious redemption wrought out for us by Jesus Christ from worse than Egyptian bondage."**

**Enlarging Our Expectations of God:** Henry emphasizes the promise "Open thy mouth wide and I will fill it," teaching that **"We cannot look for too little from the creature nor too much from the Creator."**

### **Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 1-3:** Musical worship should involve **"singing aloud and making a joyful noise"** showing we are **"not ashamed to own our dependence on him and obligations to him."**

**Verses 4-7:** The divine institutions of worship carry legal authority - **"it is at our peril if we neglect it."** God's past deliverances (Egyptian bondage, Red Sea crossing, Sinai revelation) provide warrant for continued trust.

**Verses 8-12:** God's tragic lament **"My people would not hearken to my voice"** demonstrates that rebellion results in divine abandonment: **"I gave them up unto their own hearts' lust."**

**Verses 13-16:** The conditional promise reveals what might have been - complete victory over enemies and abundance like **"honey out of the rock."**

### **Cross-References to Scripture**

Henry connects this psalm to numerous biblical passages: Leviticus 23:24 and Numbers 29:1 (feast of trumpets), Exodus 12:23-24 (Passover institution), Deuteronomy 4:7 (God's nearness), and the preface to the Ten Commandments. He links the Egyptian deliverance to Christian redemption, suggesting this psalm, **"though spoken primarily of Israel of old, is written for our learning."**

The commentary emphasizes that God's covenant faithfulness persists despite human failure, offering hope for restoration when His people return to obedience.

Chapter 82
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 82

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents **God's supreme sovereignty over all earthly authority** as the central theme. He emphasizes that magistrates derive their power from God and remain accountable to Him, stating that "God stands, he judges among them; they have their power from him and are accountable to him." 

The commentary explores the **dual nature of magistracy** - its divine dignity and human frailty. Magistrates are called "gods" because they serve as God's representatives in earthly governance, yet "shall die like men." Henry interprets this as both a warning against pride and a reminder of mortality's humbling reality.

The psalm concludes with **messianic hope**, as Henry sees verse 8 pointing toward Christ's kingdom: "It is a prayer for the hastening of that, that Christ would come, who is to judge the earth."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes magistrates' duty to **protect the vulnerable**: "Defend the poor, who have no money wherewith to make friends or fee counsel, and the fatherless." He stresses that judges must serve those from whom "there is nothing to be got by, no pay for serving them."

For subjects, Henry offers **comfort in divine oversight**: "good princes and good judges, who mean well, are under a divine direction, and bad ones, who mean ever so ill, are under a divine restraint."

He warns against **the corruption of willful ignorance**: "None so blind as those that will not see" - explaining how unjust judges "know not because they will not understand."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Establishes God's presence "in the congregation of the mighty," showing divine oversight of all governmental councils.

**Verses 3-4**: Define the magistrate's core duties as defending the poor, fatherless, afflicted, and needy - emphasizing justice for the powerless.

**Verse 5**: Henry notes the destructive consequences of judicial corruption: "All the foundations of the earth are out of course" when justice is perverted.

**Verses 6-7**: The famous "gods/die like men" paradox, which Henry interprets as both divine commission and mortal limitation.

### Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other scriptures:
- **John 10:35** for Jesus's interpretation of "gods"
- **Proverbs 16:10, 21:1, 28:15** regarding divine influence on rulers
- **Romans 13:4** on magistrates as God's servants
- **Psalm 2:8, 22:28** connecting to messianic inheritance
- **Revelation 19:6, 22:20** for ultimate divine justice

Henry concludes with profound comfort: "There are two words with which we may comfort ourselves...in reference to the mismanagements of power among men: one is Rev. xix. 6, Hallelujah, the Lord God omnipotent reigneth; the other is Rev. xxii. 20, Surely, I come quickly."

Chapter 83
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 83: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 83 as a prayer concerning the confederacy of nations against God's people Israel, likely during Jehoshaphat's time when Moabites and Ammonites invaded Judah (2 Chronicles 20). However, he emphasizes its broader prophetic application: **"This prophecy reaches to all the enemies of the gospel-church; whoever they be that oppose the kingdom of Christ, here they may read their doom."**

The central theological theme is that attacks against God's people are ultimately attacks against God Himself. Henry stresses that **"they are confederate against thee"** (v.5), showing how persecution of the church is fundamentally rebellion against divine authority.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**1. Perseverance in Prayer During Crisis:** Even in distress, "their prayer is called, A song or psalm; for singing psalms is not unseasonable, no, not when the harps are hung upon the willow-trees."

**2. Unity Among God's People:** He challenges believers with the enemies' example: "Do the enemies of the church act with one consent to destroy it? And shall not the church's friends be unanimous in serving her interests?"

**3. Confidence in Divine Protection:** God's people are His **"hidden ones"** - hidden both in secrecy ("their life is hid with Christ in God") and safety ("God takes them under his special protection").

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3:** When God "keeps silence," believers may cry out for Him to speak through providence and prophets. **"God's speaking is his acting; for with him saying and doing are the same thing."**

**Verses 4-5:** The enemies' goal of complete extermination (**"that the name of Israel may be no more in remembrance"**) represents the serpent's seed's enmity against God's people throughout history.

**Verses 6-8:** Henry notes that some enemies were Abraham's descendants (Edomites, Ishmaelites), proving **"there are no bonds of nature so strong but the spirit of persecution has broken through them."**

**Verses 9-12:** The prayer for judgment follows historical precedents (Midianites, Sisera), showing **"God is the same still that ever he was, the same to his people and the same against his enemies."**

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **2 Chronicles 20** (Jehoshaphat's crisis)
- **Psalm 2:1** ("The heathen rage")
- **Judges 4-8** (Historical precedents of Sisera, Gideon's victories)
- **Revelation 11:18** ("The nations are angry")
- **Jeremiah 12:9** (God's heritage as "a speckled bird")

The commentary emphasizes God's sovereignty over all confederacies against His church, with the ultimate assurance that **"he that sits in heaven shall laugh at them"** and that **"the gates of hell shall not prevail against the church."**

Chapter 84
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 84: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 84 as expressing "the pious breathings of a gracious soul after God and communion with him." The commentary reveals two central theological themes: **devotion to divine ordinances** and **devotion to the God of those ordinances**. Henry believes David penned this psalm during Absalom's rebellion, lamenting his absence from Jerusalem not as a royal city, but as "the holy city."

The psalm emphasizes that **spiritual beauty transcends physical circumstances**. Though the tabernacle was "a mean habitation," Henry notes that "the beauty of holiness is spiritual, and their glory is within." This reflects the theology that God's presence, not external grandeur, makes worship spaces sacred.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living:

**Prioritizing Spiritual over Material Pursuits**: David "would rather live in a bird's nest nigh God's altars than in a palace at a distance from them," demonstrating that proximity to God's presence should be valued above earthly comfort.

**Perseverance Through Difficulties**: The commentary emphasizes that true worshippers "will not be kept back by bad weather, or bad ways" when attending worship. Henry notes that "difficulties in the way of duty are designed to try our resolution."

**Active Preparation for Worship**: Just as travelers maintained roads leading to Jerusalem, Henry suggests "care should be taken to keep those roads in repair that lead to church, as well as those that lead to market."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The "amiable tabernacles" represent how "gracious souls see a wonderful, an inexpressible, beauty in holiness."

**Verses 2-3**: David's intense longing is described as encompassing his entire being—"body, soul, and spirit concurred in it." The bird imagery shows David envying creatures that nest near God's altars, emphasizing that "ordinances are empty things if we meet not with God in the ordinances."

**Verses 4-7**: Henry distinguishes between blessed ministers who "dwell in thy house" and blessed people who have "liberty of access." The "valley of Baca" (weeping valley) represents life's difficulties that can be transformed: "Our way to heaven lies through a valley of Baca, but even that may be made a well."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm with numerous biblical passages: **Psalm 63** (similar spirit of longing), **1 John 1:3** (communion with God), **Proverbs 18:10** (God as strong tower), **Isaiah 40:31** (renewed strength), **John 1:16** (grace upon grace), and **2 Corinthians 3:18** (transformation from glory to glory).

The commentary demonstrates how Old Testament worship prefigures New Testament realities, particularly in referencing Christ's satisfaction and intercession represented by the sacrificial and incense altars.

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches us to "spend as much of our time as may be in that blessed work in which we hope to spend a joyful eternity"—the continual praise of God.

Chapter 85
----------------------------------------
## Summary: Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 85

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 85 as written after the Jews' return from Babylonian captivity, when they still experienced God's displeasure. The psalm follows a pattern of prayer and prophetic response, which Henry illustrates through a powerful metaphor: "The church is like Noah in the ark, between life and death, between hope and fear; being so... Here is the dove sent forth in prayer... Here is the dove returning with an olive branch of peace."

Central themes include God's covenant faithfulness, the relationship between divine mercy and human repentance, and the certainty of God's salvation for those who fear Him. Henry emphasizes that true peace comes only after conversion: "First turn us to thee, and then cause thy anger to turn from us."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Balanced perspective in affliction**: "The sense of present afflictions should not drown the remembrance of former mercies"
2. **Proper prayer preparation**: After praying, believers should "look after our prayers, and stay for an answer"
3. **The necessity of perseverance**: God speaks peace to His people, "but let them not turn again to folly"
4. **Active listening to God**: "If we would have God to hear what we say to him by prayer, we must be ready to hear what he says to us by his word"

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry highlights God's past faithfulness—showing favor to the land, bringing back captivity, and forgiving iniquity. When God forgives, "he covers it all."

**Verses 4-7**: The prayer for restoration follows proper order—first conversion ("Turn us"), then removal of God's anger, followed by requests for mercy and salvation.

**Verse 8**: Henry emphasizes the "stilling of passions" and "raising of expectation" when waiting for God's response.

**Verses 10-11**: The famous passage about mercy, truth, righteousness, and peace meeting represents both governmental reform and the return of God's favor, potentially pointing to the Messianic kingdom.

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to numerous passages:
- Psalm 44:3 (God's favor in possessing Canaan)
- Ezra 9:8 (reviving in bondage)
- Ezekiel 37:11-12 (resurrection imagery for return from exile)
- Habakkuk 2:1-2 and 3:2 (waiting for God's response)
- John 1:4 and Isaiah 8:8 (Messianic applications)
- Hosea 4:1 and Isaiah 59:14-15 (importance of truth and mercy in governance)

Henry concludes that this psalm serves as both historical prayer for Israel's restoration and prophetic vision of Christ's kingdom, offering comfort to "all who fear God and work righteousness."

Chapter 86
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 86 Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Divine Condescension and Grace**: Henry emphasizes God's remarkable condescension in hearing prayer, noting that "when God hears our prayers it is fitly said that he bows down his ear to them, for it is admirable condescension in God that he is pleased to take notice of such mean creatures as we are."

**God's Uniqueness and Sovereignty**: The commentary stresses God's incomparability - "Among the gods...there is none like unto thee, O Lord" - highlighting His absolute sovereignty and role as "God alone." Henry sees this as foundational to worship and trust.

**Universal Worship**: Henry interprets verses 8-10 as prophetic of the Messianic age, noting this "was to have its full accomplishment in the days of the Messiah, when some out of every kingdom and nation should be effectually brought in to praise God."

### Key Practical Applications

**Prayer as Soul-Lifting**: Henry emphasizes that prayer must involve lifting the soul to God "upon the wings of faith and holy desire." True prayer engages the whole person, not just words.

**Persistent Prayer Life**: He advocates for constant prayer, noting David "cry unto thee daily" and stressing that "it is comfortable if an affliction finds the wheels of prayer a-going, and that they are not then to be set a-going."

**Balanced Self-Assessment**: Henry draws from David's example of being both "needy" yet "holy" - acknowledging our poverty while recognizing God's sanctifying grace. This prevents both pride and despair.

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-7**: Henry notes the psalm's deliberately plain language, observing that "the flourishes of wit are not the proper ornaments of prayer." He emphasizes that preservation of the soul encompasses both protection from sin and spiritual preservation.

**Verse 11**: Henry highlights the prayer "unite my heart to fear thy name" as crucial for spiritual integrity, showing the need for focused devotion rather than divided loyalties.

**Verses 8-10**: These verses demonstrate God's incomparability through His works, leading to the conclusion that universal worship is both logical and prophetic.

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to **Hebrews 5:7**, suggesting David wrote "as a type of Christ, who in the days of his flesh offered up strong cries." He also references **Psalm 25:1** for the concept of lifting up the soul, **Exodus 34:6-7** for God's self-proclaimed attributes of mercy, **John 11:41-42** for confident prayer, and **Revelation 7:9** for the fulfillment of universal worship.

The commentary presents this psalm as both a personal prayer model and a prophetic glimpse of Messianic worship, emphasizing God's condescending grace and our need for persistent, soul-engaging communion with Him.

Chapter 87
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 87: Summary

**Main Theological Themes**

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 87 as "an encomium of Zion, as a type and figure of the gospel-church." The primary theological theme centers on God's special love for His dwelling place and the superiority of public worship over private devotion. Henry emphasizes that while "God has a love for the dwellings of Jacob" (family worship), "yet he loves the gates of Zion better" - establishing that corporate worship takes precedence when both are possible.

The commentary presents the church as having an unshakable foundation built "upon the everlasting mountains and the perpetual hills," with Christ as the ultimate foundation. Henry notes that "holiness is the strength and stability of the church" rather than mere physical elevation.

**Prophetic and Christological Elements**

Henry sees prophetic significance in the psalm regarding Gentile inclusion in the church. He interprets verses 4-6 as God saying, "I will reckon Egypt and Babylon with those that know me" when they receive the gospel, making "a Gentile convert shall stand upon a level with a native Jew." 

Most notably, Henry applies "A man, a man was born in her" to Christ himself - "that man, that son of man, who is fairer than the children of men" - calling this "the greatest honour that ever was put upon the Jewish nation."

**Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Church Loyalty**: Christians should never "be ashamed of the church of Christ in its meanest condition" since "glorious things are spoken of it" in Scripture.

2. **Worship Priorities**: While family worship remains essential ("family-duty, which must by no means be neglected"), public worship should be preferred when both compete.

3. **Spiritual Accountability**: Those "born in Zion" who enjoy gospel privileges face greater responsibility - "to them much was given, and therefore of them much will be required."

4. **Joy in Worship**: The church should be characterized by celebratory worship with "singers as the players on instruments" serving God "with rejoicing."

**Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 1-3**: Henry emphasizes the contrast between the world's foundation "upon the seas" (unstable) versus the church built "upon the everlasting mountains." He notes God's declaration that His "eyes and my heart shall be there perpetually" regarding the temple.

**Verses 4-6**: Henry provides two interpretations - either acknowledging that pagan nations produce occasional notable figures, or prophesying Gentile conversion where foreigners become "fellow-citizens with the saints."

**Verse 7**: The concluding phrase "All my springs are in thee" receives rich treatment. Henry explains that while "the springs of the joy of a carnal worldling lie in wealth and pleasure," for believers "the springs of the joy of a gracious soul lie in the word of God and prayer."

**Significant Cross-References**

Henry connects this psalm to numerous scriptures: Isaiah 2:2 (mountain of the Lord's house), Psalm 24:2 (world founded on seas), Isaiah 64:10 (God's unshakeable covenant), 2 Chronicles 7:16 (God's perpetual presence), Ephesians 2:19 (Gentile inclusion), Isaiah 19:23-25 (Egypt and Assyria blessed), Romans 9:4-5 (Jewish privileges and Christ's incarnation), and Psalm 46:4 (streams making glad God's city).

The commentary concludes with the powerful statement that "Christ is the true temple; all our springs are in him, and from him all our streams flow," connecting the Old Testament type to its New Testament fulfillment.

Chapter 88
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalms 88 Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

This psalm represents one of Scripture's darkest expressions of spiritual anguish, offering no comfort or resolution from beginning to end. Henry identifies it as a **penitential psalm** that demonstrates how "our fears are thus turned into the right channel, and we take occasion from our worldly grievances to sorrow after a godly sort." 

The commentary explores the **tension between faith and despair**, noting that even the opening address to "God of my salvation" reveals that despite overwhelming darkness, the psalmist "looked for salvation" and "depended upon [God] to be the author of it." Henry suggests this psalm may serve as **a type of Christ**, drawing parallels between the psalmist's suffering and Christ's agony, where both cry out about souls "full of troubles" and lives drawing "nigh unto the grave."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes several spiritual disciplines emerging from affliction:

**Persistent Prayer**: The psalmist demonstrates being "very earnest," "very frequent and constant" in prayer, crying "day and night." Henry notes: "For thus men ought always to pray, and not to faint." Even when answers seem delayed, believers must continue because "God delays the answer in order that he may try our patience and perseverance in prayer."

**Morning Devotion**: The phrase "in the morning shall my prayer prevent thee" teaches believers to seek God early, "when spirits were lively, and before the business of the day began to crowd in."

**Godly Response to Abandonment**: When friends desert us, Henry counsels accepting this "with the same patient submission to the divine will that we do other afflictions," recognizing that "every creature is that to us (and no more) that God makes it to be."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 3-6**: Henry describes the psalmist as feeling "free among the dead," interpreting this as being beyond earthly help, "cut off from thy hand" that once provided support. The "lowest pit" represents not just external circumstances but internal spiritual desolation.

**Verse 7**: "Thy wrath lies hard upon me" receives particular attention. Henry observes that God's perceived displeasure "infused the wormwood and the gall into the affliction," noting that troubles become bearable when we "discern the favour and love of God," but unbearable when we sense His wrath.

**Verse 10-12**: The rhetorical questions about whether the dead can praise God serve as urgent pleading: "Lord, if it be not hastened, it will come too late; for I shall be dead and past relief." Henry clarifies this isn't despair or denial of resurrection, but earnest supplication for timely deliverance.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Job 19:13** regarding friends becoming strangers
- **Job 7:8; 10:21** for similar urgent pleas about death's approach  
- **1 Chronicles 25:5** identifying Heman as "the king's seer"
- **1 Kings 4:31** noting Heman's renowned wisdom
- Christ's passion narratives, particularly "My soul is exceedingly sorrowful even unto death"

## Concluding Wisdom

Henry offers pastoral insight for both sufferers and the blessed: "Those who are in trouble of mind may sing this psalm feelingly; those that are not ought to sing it thankfully, blessing God that it is not their case." This dual application transforms even Scripture's darkest passages into vehicles for either empathetic expression or grateful worship, demonstrating the Bible's comprehensive ministry to human experience.

Chapter 89
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 89: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**The Paradox of Faith in Suffering**: Henry notes this psalm's unique structure—beginning with joy and praise but ending in "sad complaints and petitions." This reflects the tension between God's eternal promises and present trials, teaching believers to "glorify the Lord in the fire."

**God's Covenant Faithfulness**: The central theme is God's unbreakable covenant with David, which Henry interprets as pointing to Christ. He emphasizes that "God's mercies are inexhaustible and his truth is inviolable," even when circumstances suggest otherwise.

**Divine Sovereignty and Omnipotence**: Henry highlights God's absolute supremacy over all creation, noting that even angels cannot compare to Him: "Who in the heaven can be compared with the Lord?"

## Key Practical Applications

**Worship in Adversity**: Henry advises, "Let our complaints therefore be turned into thanksgivings; and in these verses we find that which will be matter of praise and thanksgiving for us in the worst of times." He suggests we "get joy, by praising" rather than merely complaining.

**Corporate Worship**: Emphasizing the importance of congregational praise, Henry notes that "Saints should assemble for religious worship" and approach God "with reverence and godly fear," maintaining both intimacy and holy awe.

**Faith Anchored in Promise**: Believers should anchor hope in God's covenant promises, which are "established in the very heavens" and "above the changes of this lower region."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-4**: Henry sees the psalmist's declaration of eternal praise as a model: "We may be for ever singing God's mercies, and yet the subject will not be drawn dry." The covenant with David represents God's immutable counsel.

**Verses 5-14**: In describing God's praise by angels and saints, Henry observes that "the works of God are wonders even to those that are best acquainted and most intimately conversant with them."

**Verses 38-45**: The lament section reflects how believers can honestly express grief while maintaining faith in God's character and promises.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple biblical passages:
- Links the Davidic covenant to **Jeremiah 33:20-21** regarding God's promise that David shall not lack a descendant
- References **Amos 9:11** about raising David's fallen tabernacle
- Connects to **Hebrews 12:28** regarding serving God with reverence
- Cites **Psalm 78:6** about teaching future generations God's faithfulness

**Christological Interpretation**: Henry sees the covenant's ultimate fulfillment in Christ: "This has its accomplishment only in Christ, of the seed of David, who lives for ever, to whom God has given the throne of his father David."

The commentary emphasizes that believers must maintain "high thoughts of God, a lively faith in his covenant with the Redeemer, and a sympathy with the afflicted parts of the church" when engaging with this psalm.

Chapter 90
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary Summary: Psalm 90

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 90 as Moses's prayer written during Israel's wilderness wandering after God's judgment that their generation would die before entering Canaan. The commentary establishes four central theological tensions: **God's eternality versus human frailty**, **divine wrath versus mercy**, **temporal judgment versus eternal hope**, and **human mortality as consequence of sin rather than mere natural law**.

Henry emphasizes that death is "not so much a debt owing to nature" but rather "a debt to the justice of God" since "sin entered into the world, and death by sin." This challenges any purely naturalistic view of mortality, grounding it firmly in the fall and divine justice.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing mortality and divine discipline. He encourages finding comfort in God as our "dwelling-place," noting that "true believers are at home in God, and that is their comfort in reference to all the toils and tribulations they meet with in this world."

The commentary advocates honest confession of both open sins and "secret sins" that God sees, while submitting to divine discipline. Henry recommends the psalm for funeral meditations, suggesting it helps believers "apply it to the years of our passage through the wilderness of this world."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry highlights the contrast between God as eternal refuge and human transience. God's eternality means He exists "without beginning of days, or end of life, or change of time."

**Verses 3-6**: The phrase "Return, ye children of men" receives multiple interpretations - as a call to repentance, restoration to life, the general death sentence ("dust to dust"), and future resurrection hope.

**Verse 4**: Henry explains that while "a thousand years are nothing to God's eternity," all events across millennia "are as present to the Eternal Mind as what was done yesterday."

**Verses 7-11**: These verses address Israel's specific judgment, where their suffering comes "by thy anger, by thy wrath" due to both public rebellion and private heart sins that God "set in the light of thy countenance."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: **Exodus 15** and **Deuteronomy 32** (Moses's other songs), **Numbers 14** (the wilderness judgment story), **Revelation 15:3** (song of Moses), **2 Peter 3:8** (thousand years as one day), **Genesis 3:19** (dust to dust), **Ecclesiastes 12:7** (spirit returning to God), and **Job 14:14-15** (resurrection hope).

He also references **Jeremiah 4:1** for the call to return, and **Proverbs 8:26** regarding creation's order, demonstrating how this ancient psalm connects with broader scriptural themes of judgment, mercy, and eternal hope.

The commentary presents Psalm 90 as both historically specific to Israel's wilderness experience and universally applicable to human mortality and divine justice.

Chapter 91
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 91: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 91 as **"a writ of protection for all true believers"** written **"in the name of the King of kings, and under the broad seal of Heaven."** He emphasizes that divine protection is conditional upon **dwelling "in the secret place of the Most High"** - characterized as living in intimate communion with God through **"inward religion"** and **"heart-work of the service of God."**

Henry suggests these promises apply primarily to **Christ as Mediator**, making them **"more sweet and sure to all believers"** when received through Him. The psalm's central theological premise is that **those who live a life of communion with God are constantly safe under his protection.**

### Key Practical Applications

**Personal Faith Response**: Henry emphasizes the believer's active choice in verse 2: **"I choose him as such, and confide in him"** - making God one's refuge requires personal decision and trust.

**Freedom from Fear**: A major application is deliverance from **"disquieting distrustful fear"** during dangers. Henry teaches that **"faith shall keep thee from being inordinately afraid"** because believers know that even death **"cannot hurt thee"** spiritually.

**Daily Security**: The commentary addresses both **natural life protection** (from diseases, dangers) and **spiritual life protection** (from Satan's temptations and sin's contagion), encouraging believers to **"shelter themselves under, and then solace themselves in, the divine protection."**

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: **"Dwelling"** means being **"at home in God"** - requiring intimate, consistent relationship, not casual acquaintance.

**Verse 4**: The **"feathers and wings"** imagery shows God's **"greatest tenderness and affection"** like **"the hen gathering her chickens,"** while **"shield and buckler"** demonstrates His **"greatest power and efficacy."**

**Verses 5-6**: Henry notes we face dangers **"night and day"** but promises **"great security"** - **"Every bullet has its commission"** and operates under **"divine direction."**

**Verse 7**: In **"distinguishing way"** protection during **"general desolation,"** believers can trust that **"those who preserve their purity in times of general corruption may trust God with their safety."**

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple scriptures: **Ruth 2:12** (trusting under God's wings), **Matthew 23:37** (hen and chickens), **Hebrews 2:15** (bondage through fear of death), **Isaiah 26:3** (perfect peace), and **Psalm 27:5** (divine hiding). He references **Daniel 11:39** contrasting false **"Mahuzzim"** (strongholds) with God as true fortress, and notes the **sprinkling of blood** that **"secured the first-born of Israel"** as a type of divine protection.

The commentary presents Psalm 91 as both practical encouragement for daily faith and profound theology about God's covenant protection for those who genuinely abide in Him.

Chapter 92
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 92: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Sabbath Worship and Eternal Rest**: Henry views this psalm as perfectly suited for Sabbath observance, calling it a day of "holy rest" and "holy work." He connects it to both God's rest from creation and the eschatological sabbath, referencing one Jewish writer who called it "A psalm or song for the age to come, which shall be all sabbath." The Sabbath points to "that sabbatism which remains for the people of God" (Heb. 4:9).

**Divine Justice and Providence**: The commentary emphasizes God's ultimate justice through the contrast between the temporary prosperity of the wicked and God's eternal sovereignty. Henry notes that when "the wicked spring as the grass," their flourishing is actually "that they shall be destroyed for ever" - their prosperity being preparation for judgment.

**God's Transcendent Wisdom**: Henry highlights the "depth of God's thoughts" versus human understanding, emphasizing that "God's counsels as much exceed the contrivances of our wisdom as his works do the efforts of our power."

## Key Practical Applications

**Daily Praise Practice**: Christians must "begin and end every day with praising God" - morning thanksgiving for "mercies of the night" and evening gratitude for "mercies of the day." Henry emphasizes that praise should be constant, "not only on sabbath days, but every day."

**Proper Response to God's Works**: Believers should study and celebrate God's works rather than remaining like "brutish" people who "regard not the work of the Lord." Henry warns that those who don't understand God's works "are fools" who miss the meaning of both prosperity and adversity.

**Spiritual Perspective on Enemies**: Rather than fearing opposition, believers should maintain confidence in God's ultimate victory, knowing that "all the impenitent workers of iniquity shall be deemed and taken as God's enemies."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry calls praise "good work" that is "its own wages" and "the work of angels." He emphasizes showing forth God's "lovingkindness and faithfulness" with "solemn sound," not frivolous music.

**Verse 4**: Personal experience of God's joy becomes the basis for recommending praise to others: "Those can best recommend to others the duty of praise who have themselves experienced the pleasantness of it."

**Verses 7-9**: The wicked's prosperity is deceptive - "The sheep that are designed for the slaughter are put into the fattest pasture." Meanwhile, God remains "most high for evermore."

**Verses 12-15**: The righteous "flourish like the palm tree" and "bring forth fruit in old age," demonstrating that "the LORD is upright."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Hebrews 4:9** - the eternal sabbath rest
- **Isaiah 55:9** - God's thoughts above our thoughts  
- **Romans 11:33** - "O the depth of God's designs!"
- **Proverbs 1:32** - "prosperity of fools shall slay them"
- **Psalm 148:14** - God as "praise of all his saints"
- **Isaiah 51:7-8** - contrasting temporary human pride with eternal righteousness

Henry particularly connects verse 11 to Christ's victory: "This perhaps has reference to Christ, to his victory over Satan, death, and hell" and the final judgment when Christ's enemies "will fall before him and be made his footstool."

The commentary presents Psalm 92 as both a practical guide for Sabbath worship and a theological meditation on God's eternal justice, encouraging believers to find joy in praising God while maintaining confidence in His ultimate triumph over evil.

Chapter 93
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 93: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 93 as a celebration of God's sovereign kingdom, operating through both **providence** (governing the world) and **grace** (securing the church). He emphasizes that this psalm points directly to the Messiah, "to whom the Father has given all power both in heaven and in earth," noting that "In singing this psalm we forget ourselves if we forget Christ."

The commentary centers on the declaration **"The LORD reigns"** as fundamental truth, second only to God's existence itself. Henry connects this to Revelation 19:6 - "the Lord God omnipotent reigns" - showing continuity from Old Testament worship to the glorified church.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

- **Fear not earthly powers**: Since God's "majesty will eclipse" that of His enemies, Christians shouldn't fear opposition to God's kingdom
- **Trust in divine preservation**: If God "established the world," He will certainly "establish his church, that it cannot be moved"
- **Find peace in storms**: When facing "tumults...in our own bosoms" or external persecution, remember that God "is mightier" and can "command peace to the church...peace in the soul"
- **Pursue holiness**: "Holiness always becomes God's house" - it is both the church's "beauty" and its "strength and safety"

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: God is "clothed with majesty" and "strength" - Henry contrasts earthly majesty as "glimmerings of a glow-worm" compared to God's glory like "the brightness of the sun."

**Verse 2**: God's throne is "established of old" because His "eternal counsels" preceded all creation, demonstrating that "in an eternal mind there could not but be eternal thoughts."

**Verses 3-4**: The "floods" represent both external enemies and internal spiritual struggles. Henry notes that enemy power is often "more of sound than substance" - "there is more of noise than substance in it."

**Verse 5**: Divine testimonies are "very sure" while "holiness becomes thy house...for ever" - emphasizing that unlike changing fashions, "holiness is perpetually decent."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Revelation 19:6** - "Hallelujah; the Lord God omnipotent reigns"
- **1 Corinthians 15:24** - Christ's eternal kingdom outlasting all other powers
- **Isaiah 57:20** and **Psalm 2:1** - the wicked compared to tempestuous seas
- **Job 26:7** - God "hanged the earth upon nothing"
- **Psalm 46:1-2** - God's sovereignty as encouragement amid worldly troubles
- **Isaiah 26:3** - divine peace for trusting souls

The commentary consistently points readers back to Christ as the ultimate fulfillment of divine kingship, making this ancient psalm immediately relevant to New Testament believers.

Chapter 94
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 94

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 94 as a two-part response to religious persecution: divine judgment for oppressors and comfort for the faithful. The psalm addresses "the church of God under hatches, oppressed and persecuted" and serves as "an appeal to God, as the judge of heaven and earth." 

Key theological themes include **God's supreme sovereignty as judge**, with Henry emphasizing that God is "judge of the earth, of the whole earth: none are exempt from his jurisdiction." The psalm also confronts **atheistic thinking**, where persecutors foolishly believe "The Lord shall not see, neither shall the God of Jacob regard it."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Patient endurance under persecution**: Believers should "bear [wrong] with silence, committing themselves to him who judges righteously" rather than seeking personal vengeance.

2. **Proper prayer in persecution**: The psalm models how to appeal to God as the righteous judge, with Henry noting that persecutors "have the prayers of God's people against them, who cry day and night to him."

3. **Divine discipline as blessing**: Verse 12's "Blessed is the man whom thou chastenest" teaches that God's correction serves to instruct and ultimately protect his people.

4. **Guarding one's thoughts**: Since "The Lord knows the thoughts of man," Henry emphasizes the need to "keep a strict guard upon our thoughts, because God takes particular notice of them."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

On verses 1-2, Henry highlights the repeated phrase "O God, to whom vengeance belongeth" as emphasizing both God's authority and justice: "it is daring presumption to usurp his prerogative and step into his throne."

Verses 9-10 receive particular attention for their argument from creation to God's omniscience: "He that planted the ear, shall he not hear? he that formed the eye, shall he not see?" Henry notes this proves "that God has infinitely and transcendently in himself all those perfections that are in any creature."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical passages:
- **Luke 18:3,7** for God avenging his people speedily
- **Romans 13:4** on magistrates as God's avengers
- **Romans 12:19** on leaving vengeance to God
- **Jude 15** on judgment for "hard speeches"
- **Exodus 4:11** on God as source of human faculties
- **Job 28:23,28** on God as the source of all knowledge

Henry concludes that believers should "look abroad upon the pride of oppressors with a holy indignation, and the tears of the oppressed with a holy compassion; but, at the same time, look upwards to the righteous Judge with an entire satisfaction."

Chapter 95
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 95 Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 95 as both Davidic and messianic, drawing heavily from Hebrews 3-4 to establish its gospel significance. He identifies the psalm as "calculated for the days of the Messiah," where God speaks through His Son and offers a rest beyond Canaan. The commentary emphasizes two primary purposes for singing psalms: making "melody unto the Lord" and providing mutual teaching and admonition among believers.

Central to Henry's interpretation is the identification of Christ as the ultimate object of praise. He writes that "as the eternal Word, all things were made" through Christ, making Him "the restorer and reconciler of all who was the Creator of all." The psalm must be sung "with a holy reverence of God's majesty and a dread of his justice."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that worship requires both joyful celebration and humble reverence. True praise involves "holy joy and delight" expressed through "joyful noise," while simultaneously maintaining "humble reverence, and a holy awe." He stresses corporate worship, noting we must "praise God in concert, in the solemn assemblies" - "not others without me, nor I alone, but others with me."

The commentary warns against spiritual procrastination, declaring that "nothing is more dangerous than delay" when responding to God's voice. Henry applies this urgently to gospel reception: "To-day, if by faith you accept the gospel offer, well and good, but to-morrow it may be too late."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry emphasizes that praise must combine vocal expression with heartfelt emotion - we must "sing forth his praises out of the abundance of a heart filled with love, and joy, and thankfulness."

**Verses 3-5**: God's greatness encompasses both infinite power as "great King above all gods" and universal ownership. Henry notes that even "the deep places of the earth, which are out of our sight" and "the height of the hills which are out of our reach" belong to Him.

**Verses 7-8**: The transition from praise to warning occurs mid-verse, emphasizing urgency. Henry interprets "today" as the gospel day, stressing immediate response to Christ's voice.

**Verses 9-11**: The wilderness generation serves as a cautionary example, where "hardness of heart is at the bottom of all our distrusts of God and quarrels with him."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively references **Hebrews 3-4** to establish the psalm's gospel application and the concept of spiritual rest. He connects verse 1's "rock of our salvation" to **1 Corinthians 10:4** - "That rock is Christ."

The shepherd imagery in verse 7 links to **John 10:27** ("My sheep hear my voice") and **Ephesians 3:21** regarding glory in the churches. The creation theme references **John 1:3** and **Colossians 1:16, 20**. 

For the warning section, Henry cites **1 Corinthians 10:11** about Israel's sins being "written for our admonition," **Luke 19:42** for the urgency of spiritual opportunity, and **Psalm 78:8** warning against being "a stubborn and rebellious generation."

Henry masterfully weaves Old Testament historical warnings with New Testament gospel applications, presenting Psalm 95 as both ancient liturgy and contemporary spiritual instruction for the church age.

Chapter 96
----------------------------------------
# Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 96

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 96 as fundamentally Messianic, looking beyond its immediate context of bringing the ark to David's city to "the kingdom of Christ." The psalm's central theme is **the accession of the Gentiles** to God's kingdom through the gospel. Henry emphasizes this represents a prophetic vision of Christ's reign, noting that while originally sung for the ark's translation, "it looks further, to the kingdom of Christ, and is designed to celebrate the glories of that kingdom."

The commentary presents God's **universal sovereignty** over all false gods, declaring that "all the gods of the nations are idols" while "the LORD made the heavens." Henry sees this as essential preparation for the Gentiles to embrace the true God.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian worship and evangelism:

**For Worship**: Christians should sing with "new affections, clothed with new expressions" - not just occasionally but "from day to day" since gospel devotion "can never be exhausted." He emphasizes worshipping "in the beauty of holiness" with "holy hearts, sanctified by the grace of God."

**For Evangelism**: The command to "declare his glory among the heathen" becomes the apostolic commission to "preach the gospel to every creature." Henry notes that what was once "declared only among the Jews" is "now declared among the heathen, among all people."

**For Perspective**: Believers should maintain "great and high thoughts of the glory of God and the grace of the gospel" with "entire satisfaction in Christ's sovereign dominion."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: The "new song" represents "New-Testament song, a song of praise for the new covenant" that is "ever new, and shall never wax old." The call for "all the earth" to sing prophesies "the calling of the Gentiles."

**Verse 10**: Henry highlights the ancient textual addition "The Lord reigneth from the tree," referring to Christ's crucifixion, noting it was "because he became obedient to death, even the death of the cross, that God exalted him."

**Verses 11-13**: The cosmic celebration anticipates both Christ's present reign and future judgment, establishing that "the world also shall be established that it shall not be moved" through Christ's mediation.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to broader biblical themes: Revelation 14:3 (the new song), Acts 10:42 (Peter's preaching to Gentiles), Romans 15:16 (offering of the Gentiles), Hebrews 13:15 (sacrifices of praise), and multiple references linking to Malachi 1:11, Zechariah 14:17, and Isaiah 66:23 regarding universal worship.

The commentary presents Psalm 96 as a bridge between Old Testament worship and New Testament evangelism, celebrating God's eternal reign while prophetically embracing the inclusion of all nations in His kingdom.

Chapter 97
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 97: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry centers this psalm entirely on **Christ's reign and kingdom**, viewing it as a companion to Psalm 96. The primary theological theme is that "The Lord reigns" - specifically referring to both God's providential kingdom and Christ's mediatorial kingdom. Henry emphasizes that **"Christ is the Alpha and Omega"** of this psalm, and that Christ's government encompasses both heaven and earth, with "angels, authorities, and powers, being made subject unto him."

The commentary presents Christ's kingdom as having **dual aspects**: terror for enemies and comfort for faithful subjects, likened to "the pillar of cloud and fire" that had "a dark side towards the Egyptians" but "a bright side towards the Israel of God."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications:

1. **Universal Joy in Christ's Reign**: All earth should rejoice in Christ's government, including "the multitude of isles" (applicable to Gentile nations and specifically Britain as "a great isle").

2. **Trust in Divine Justice**: Even when **"clouds and darkness are round about him"** and His ways seem mysterious, believers can trust that **"righteousness and judgment are the habitation of his throne."**

3. **Proper Response to God's Holiness**: Saints should **"hate evil"** and find comfort in God's protection, while **"rejoicing in the Lord"** and giving **"thanks at the remembrance of his holiness."**

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 2-3**: Henry explains that while God's dispensations may be "altogether unaccountable," justice runs through all His administration like "a golden thread."

**Verses 4-5**: The opposition to Christ's kingdom (symbolized as "hills") will be destroyed like **"wax before consuming fire,"** fulfilled historically in Jerusalem's destruction and the persecution of early Christians.

**Verse 7**: Henry interprets "worship him, all ye gods" as referring to angels worshipping Christ (citing Hebrews 1:6), demonstrating Christ's authority over both earthly and heavenly realms.

**Verse 11**: The promise that **"Light is sown for the righteous"** refers to future joy treasured up for believers, emphasizing hope amid present trials.

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical passages:
- **Hebrews 1:6** for angelic worship of Christ
- **Psalm 96:10** for the establishment of earth through Christ's reign
- **1 Thessalonians 2:15-16** regarding judgment on those opposing the gospel
- **Zechariah 9:9** for Zion's joy in her coming King
- **2 Timothy 4:18** for Christ's preservation of saints' souls

Henry concludes that this psalm calls believers to **"be affected with the glory of the exalted Redeemer"** and to consider themselves blessed if they are among those that **"kiss the son"** in submission to Christ's righteous rule.

Chapter 98
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 98: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 98 as **a prophetic psalm about the Messiah's kingdom** and the inclusion of Gentiles in God's salvation. He identifies two primary themes: **the glory of the Redeemer (verses 1-3) and the joy of the redeemed (verses 4-9)**. 

Henry emphasizes that this "new song" represents something unprecedented—"a mystery which was hidden from ages and generations." He notes that **converts sing a new song because "if the grace of God put a new heart into our breasts, it will therewith put a new song into our mouths."**

The commentary highlights **four reasons for praise**: God's marvelous works in salvation, Christ's complete victory over Satan and death, the revelation of salvation to all nations, and the fulfillment of Old Testament promises to Israel.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for believers:

1. **Wholehearted worship**: Christians should use "all ways and means possible to express our joy" in Christ's kingdom, combining vocal praise with instrumental music in "solemn religious manner."

2. **Evangelistic enthusiasm**: Believers should be "affected with those glad tidings and desirous to affect others with them," proclaiming the gospel with enthusiasm.

3. **Universal scope**: The call to praise extends to "all the earth," emphasizing the global nature of Christ's salvation and the church's missionary mandate.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The "new song" is "a song of songs" for redeeming love—the most excellent song possible. Henry emphasizes that salvation by Christ is "all his doing and it is marvellous in our eyes."

**Verses 2-3**: God's salvation and righteousness are revealed "not in types and figures as under the law, but written as with a sunbeam." The discovery extends "in the sight of the heathen," fulfilling God's promises to Israel while blessing all nations.

**Verses 4-6**: The celebration should include both "soft and gentle melody of the harp" and "martial sound of the trumpet and cornet" befitting a "victorious King."

**Verses 7-9**: Henry uniquely suggests that when creation "rejoices," it **"would shame the stupidity and ingratitude of mankind"** who fail to properly honor the Redeemer.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical passages:

- **Psalm 96:1** (parallel structure of "new song")
- **Colossians 2:15** (Christ's victory over principalities and powers)
- **Isaiah 49:24** (taking prey from the mighty)
- **Romans 1:17** (righteousness revealed through faith)
- **Luke 1:72** (God's covenant faithfulness)
- **Revelation 5:8** (songs of praise for opening the sealed book)

He also makes an intriguing connection to **Virgil's Fourth Eclogue**, suggesting the Roman poet may have drawn from biblical prophecies about a divine child who would restore the golden age and free the world from sin.

Henry concludes that this psalm may ultimately point to **Christ's second coming**, when creation itself will be renewed and "he shall come to judge the world with righteousness."

Chapter 99
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 99: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**Divine Sovereignty and Kingship**: Henry centers his interpretation on the foundational truth that "the Lord reigns," emphasizing God's governance over both providence and the church through His Son. He distinguishes between Old Testament rule through "holy fear" versus New Testament rule through "holy love," noting that while enemies will tremble, those who submit find establishment.

**Theocratic Government**: Henry presents Israel's government as a divine theocracy where God presided over both religious and civil affairs. He sees David as a king "whose strength loves judgment" and views this as typological of Christ's future reign on David's throne.

**Divine Holiness**: God's holiness emerges as the central attribute that makes His name both "great to his friends and terrible to his enemies." Henry emphasizes this as the reason for reverential worship, stating "his holiness should strike an awe upon us, as it does on the angels themselves."

## Key Practical Applications

**Worship and Reverence**: Henry calls believers to "exalt the name of God" with hearts filled with high thoughts of Him. He emphasizes that "the more we abase ourselves, and the more prostrate we are before God, the more we exalt him."

**Public Gratitude**: He argues that "the greater the public mercies are which we have a share in the more we are obliged to bear a part in the public homage paid to God," particularly regarding Christ's kingdom.

**Prayer and Obedience**: Using Moses, Aaron, and Samuel as examples, Henry demonstrates that effective prayer must be coupled with obedience: "they made God's word and law their rule, as knowing that unless they did so they could not expect their prayers should be answered."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets God "sitting between the cherubim" as presiding over both oracular law-giving and mercy-seat petitions, making the temple God's "royal palace" and the Holy of Holies His "presence-chamber."

**Verses 6-8**: He presents Moses, Aaron, and Samuel as having extraordinary "intercourse with Heaven," noting their prayers obtained miraculous answers and their obedience to God's testimonies. Henry emphasizes God's mercy in forgiving Israel through their intercession even while taking "vengeance of their inventions."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Romans 9:4** - regarding Israel's privileges under the law
- **Hebrews 12:18, 27** - contrasting Old Testament terror with New Testament grace
- **Psalm 96:10** - on the stability of God's kingdom versus the shaking of opposition
- **2 Chronicles 19:6** - on judges ruling for God
- **1 Samuel 8:7** - on Israel's rejection of theocracy

Henry concludes that gospel-era believers have "much more reason" to exalt God's name than those under the law, seeing the ceremonial services as "types and figures of evangelical worship" administered by the eternal Word before His incarnation.

Chapter 100
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 100: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 100 as a universal call to worship that transcends the Jewish economy and extends to the gospel church. The central theological theme is **joyful worship rooted in divine relationship**. Henry emphasizes that true worship flows from knowledge: "Knowledge is the mother of devotion and of all obedience: blind sacrifices will never please a seeing God."

The commentary reveals **seven foundational truths about God**: His deity, creative power, ownership rights, sovereign rule, benevolent care, infinite mercy, and faithful truth. These form the theological foundation for worship, moving from God's transcendent nature to His intimate relationship with His people.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry strongly advocates for **cheerful worship**, stating "Gospel-worshippers should be joyful worshippers." He prescribes that believers should "serve the Lord with gladness" and approach God "as to God our exceeding Joy." This isn't mere emotionalism but recognition that "by holy joy we do really serve God; it is an honour to him to rejoice in him."

The commentary emphasizes **corporate worship**, explaining that while we serve God privately, "it is in public worship especially that we enter into his gates and into his courts." Henry also insists that **thanksgiving must permeate all worship**: "This golden thread must run through every duty...In every thing give thanks, in every ordinance, as well as in every providence."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1-2**: Henry sees the call to "all lands" as prophetic of gospel expansion: "When all nations shall be discipled, and the gospel preached to every creature, then this summons will be fully answered."

**Verse 3**: The critical verse receives extensive treatment through seven theological points. Henry notes the Masoretic textual variant ("his we are" vs. "not we ourselves"), concluding: "because God made us, and not we ourselves, therefore we are not our own, but his."

**Verse 4**: Public worship is emphasized as entering God's courts, with thanksgiving as the proper entrance attitude.

**Verse 5**: God's character provides the ultimate motivation for praise - His goodness, everlasting mercy, and enduring truth across generations.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: Psalm 122:1 (gladness in going to God's house), Psalm 43:4 (God as "exceeding Joy"), Hebrews 13:15 (sacrifice of praise), and Psalm 95:7 (sheep of his pasture). These references demonstrate how Psalm 100 connects to broader biblical themes of worship, joy, and divine relationship.

The commentary presents this psalm as both immediately practical for worship and prophetically significant for the church age, making it a bridge between Old Testament temple worship and New Testament spiritual worship.

Chapter 101
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 101: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 101 as David's "solemn vow" when taking charge of his kingdom and household, calling it "the householder's psalm."
