The commentary presents this as both a practical guide for godly leadership and a typological foreshadowing of Christ's governance of His church. Henry emphasizes that the psalm establishes a pattern for "good government" where power becomes "a terror to evil-doers, but a praise to those that do well."

The overarching theme centers on **mercy and judgment** (verse 1), which Henry interprets as both God's providential dealings with David and David's commitment to rule with justice and compassion. He notes that "God's providences concerning his people are commonly mixed—mercy and judgment," requiring believers to "sing unto him" in both prosperity and adversity.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws extensive practical applications for **family governance and leadership**. He emphasizes that the psalm applies to "all that are in any sphere of power, whether larger or narrower." Key applications include:

- **Private conduct matters as much as public**: David resolves to "walk within my house with a perfect heart," showing that leaders must govern themselves privately before governing others publicly.
- **Careful selection of associates**: Henry stresses the importance of choosing faithful servants and avoiding those characterized by pride, deceit, slander, and malice.
- **Proactive reformation**: The call to "early destroy all the wicked of the land" represents zealous commitment to moral reform, beginning immediately and thoroughly.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 2**: Henry highlights David's prayer "O when wilt thou come unto me?" noting that "those may expect God's presence that walk with a perfect heart in a perfect way."

**Verse 3**: The phrase "I will set no wicked thing before mine eyes" receives careful attention as avoiding not just gross sins but maintaining pure motives in governance—never seeking "that which we cannot have without sin."

**Verses 4-5, 7**: Henry notes David doesn't mention obvious sins like murder or adultery, but focuses on "less scandalous, but no less dangerous" sins like a "froward heart," slander, pride, and deceit—character flaws particularly dangerous in positions of influence.

**Verse 6**: The emphasis on seeking "the faithful of the land" leads Henry to observe that "those are commonly most fit for places of honour and trust that are least fond of them."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout:
- **1 Timothy 3:4** regarding ruling one's house well
- **2 Samuel 6:20** showing David's return to bless his household
- **Isaiah 33:15** on shutting eyes from seeing evil
- **1 Samuel 18:14** demonstrating how David fulfilled his vow to behave wisely
- **Proverbs 29:12** warning that when rulers listen to lies, "all his servants are wicked"
- **Revelation 22:15** connecting to Christ's final judgment against those who "love and make a lie"

The commentary consistently connects David's earthly kingship to Christ's eternal reign, noting that "the Son of David has his eyes upon the faithful in the land; his secret is with them, and they shall dwell with him."

Henry concludes that this psalm serves as both historical record and ongoing instruction for "families, both governors and governed" to maintain godly order and secure God's presence through righteous living.

Chapter 102
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 102: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies this as a **Messianic psalm**, noting that verses 25-26 are applied to Christ in Hebrews 1:10-12. The psalm speaks either of Christ's afflictions or "the afflictions of his church for his sake." Henry sees it as written by someone "afflicted with the church and for it," emphasizing that "on those that are of a public spirit afflictions of that kind lie heavier than any other."

The central theological tension is between **human frailty and divine eternality**. While the psalmist laments his transient suffering ("My days are like a shadow that declineth"), he finds comfort in God's permanence: "But thou, O LORD, shalt endure for ever."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**For the Afflicted:** "When our state is afflicted, and our spirits are overwhelmed, it is our duty and interest to pray, and by prayer to pour out our complaints before the Lord." He emphasizes that God gives us "liberty of speech" and that prayer provides "ease to an afflicted spirit to unburden itself."

**For Prayer Life:** Henry warns against excessive isolation in sorrow, noting that while we should "sit alone to consider our ways," we must not "sit alone to indulge an inordinate grief." He advocates pressing prayer with "humility and patience."

**For Perspective:** He teaches that "a good man loves God better than himself, and therefore can balance his own sorrow and death with the pleasing thought of the unchangeable blessedness of the Eternal Mind."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 3-11:** Henry interprets the physical descriptions metaphorically - "My bones are burned as a hearth" represents complete desolation. The imagery of being "like a pelican of the wilderness" and "an owl of the desert" depicts chosen solitude in grief.

**Verse 10:** "Thou hast lifted me up, and cast me down" - Henry explains this as God lifting "first, that we may throw it down with the more violence" or as remembrance of former prosperity that "aggravates the present grief."

**Verses 13-16:** The "set time" for Zion's favor refers to the seventy-year Babylonian captivity prophesied by Daniel. Henry emphasizes that deliverance comes at God's "appointed time" which is both "the best time" and "a certain time."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry provides extensive scriptural connections:
- **Hebrews 1:10-12** (application to Christ)
- **Daniel 9:2, 17** (understanding the times, sanctuary restoration)
- **Hosea 14:2** ("Take with you words" - about prepared prayers)
- **Psalm 18:6** (prayers reaching God's ears)
- **Psalm 30:7** (God hiding his face causing trouble)
- **Lamentations 3:28** (sitting alone to consider one's ways)

Henry notes the psalm's structure mirrors **Psalm 77**, where personal complaint transitions to comfort through contemplating "God's eternity, and the church's prosperity and perpetuity." He concludes that this psalm teaches believers to "sympathize with those that have" occasion for such complaints, making "the comfortable part of this psalm...the more comfortable to us."

Chapter 103
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 103: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry emphasizes **divine mercy and forgiveness** as the psalm's central theme. He presents God's character as fundamentally compassionate, highlighting the progression from sin's pardon to spiritual healing: "If God take away the guilt of sin by pardoning mercy, he will break the power of it by renewing grace." The commentary underscores God's **covenant faithfulness**, contrasting human frailty ("dust") with divine eternality. Henry also emphasizes **comprehensive redemption** - God doesn't work "by halves" but provides complete salvation addressing both guilt and corruption.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Personal Devotion**: "We make nothing of our religious performances if we do not make heart-work of them" - emphasizing that worship must engage "all that is within us." 

**Gratitude Practice**: Believers must actively combat spiritual amnesia: "If we do not give thanks for them, we do forget them; and that is unjust as well as unkind."

**Self-Examination**: David's example shows wisdom in "communing with his own heart" and stirring up one's soul to praise.

**Corporate Worship**: Christians should "rejoice and give thanks" for God's work in others' lives, "all the saints being fed at a common table."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry emphasizes the necessity of wholehearted worship and deliberate remembrance of God's benefits.

**Verses 3-5**: He connects forgiveness and healing as inseparable works, with the "eagle's youth" representing spiritual renewal through God's grace.

**Verses 11-12**: Henry highlights the immeasurable nature of God's mercy - "so high that the earth is but a point to the vast expanse" and forgiveness so complete that sins "shall be sought for, and not found."

**Verses 13-14**: God's fatherly compassion stems from His intimate knowledge of human frailty: "He has reason to know our frame, for he framed us."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry weaves extensive scriptural support throughout, notably connecting to **Exodus 34:6-7** (God's self-revelation to Moses), **1 Corinthians 1:30** (Christ as righteousness and sanctification), and **Isaiah 40:6-7** (human frailty as grass). He references **Job 33:24-25** regarding redemption and renewal, and **Romans 2:4** on God's patience leading to repentance. The commentary demonstrates how this psalm synthesizes major biblical themes of grace, redemption, and covenant love found throughout Scripture.

Chapter 104
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 104 Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies Psalm 104 as a majestic celebration of God's **sovereignty over creation and providence**. Unlike the previous psalm which focused on covenant mercy, this psalm praises "God as the God of nature." The central theme is **God's greatness manifested through His works** - from the splendor of His heavenly majesty to His providential care for all creatures.

Henry emphasizes **God's absolute dominion** over creation, noting how even "fluid bodies" like water and air obey divine commands. The psalm reveals God as both transcendently great ("O Lord my God! thou art very great!") and intimately involved in sustaining His creation. A key theological insight is that **God's glory is visible through His works**, making creation a revelation of divine attributes.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Obedience and Submission**: "Let the obsequiousness even of the unstable waters teach us obedience to the word and will of God; for shall man alone of all the creatures be obstinate?" If waters obey God's boundaries, how much more should rational beings submit to His will.

**Contentment with Providence**: The waters "retiring to and resting in the place assigned them teach us to acquiesce in the disposals of that wise providence which appoints us the bounds of our habitation."

**Worship and Reverence**: Henry emphasizes beginning worship with intentionality: "Come, my soul, where art thou? What art thou thinking of? Here is work to be done, good work, angels' work; set about it in good earnest."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-4**: Henry presents an elaborate metaphor of God as a divine king whose "robes" are light itself, whose "palace" is the stretched heavens, whose "chariot" is the clouds, and whose "retinue" are angels described as "spirits" and "flaming fire."

**Verses 5-9**: The earth's foundation demonstrates God's power - "though he has hung it upon nothing," it remains stable. Henry quotes Dr. Hammond's striking observation that the earth "being a heavy body, one would think it should fall every minute; and yet...it must, contrary to the nature of such a body, fall upwards."

**Verses 10-18**: God's provision extends to all creatures, even "wild asses, though untameable and therefore of no use to man, are welcome to quench their thirst."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to numerous biblical passages:

- **Psalm 19 and 8** for comparison of creation themes
- **1 John 1:5, James 1:17, 1 Timothy 6:16** regarding God as light
- **Genesis 1:3, 7, 9** for creation parallels
- **Hebrews 1:7** where verse 4 about angels is quoted
- **Job 26:2, 38:8** for earth's foundations and God's control of seas
- **Habakkuk 3:8, 13** and **Jeremiah 5:22** regarding God's power over waters

Henry notes this psalm's literary excellence, stating it "greatly excels...not only for piety and devotion...but for flight of fancy, brightness of ideas, surprising turns, and all the beauties and ornaments of expression, the Greek and Latin poets upon any subject of this nature."

The commentary emphasizes that recognizing God's greatness in creation should lead to humble worship and obedient living, as all creation demonstrates perfect submission to divine authority.

Chapter 105
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 105: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 105 as a celebration of God's **special covenant faithfulness** to His church, contrasting it with Psalm 104's focus on general providence. The central theme is God's unwavering commitment to His promises across generations. Henry emphasizes that "God will not forget [His covenant] and therefore we must not," highlighting the **perpetuity of divine promises**. 

The psalm demonstrates God's **sovereign orchestration of history**, where even seemingly negative circumstances (like Joseph's imprisonment) serve His ultimate purposes. Henry notes that God's covenant represents not just divine favor but also human obligation, as it "was made sure by all that is sacred."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian devotion:

**Worship Practices**: He encourages flexibility in prayer length, advising believers to "be more observant how our hearts work than how the time passes." The goal is authentic engagement rather than prescribed duration.

**Evangelistic Witness**: Christians should "make known his deeds" and discuss God's works in "familiar discourses with our families and friends," making divine matters part of everyday conversation "with due reverence."

**Historical Perspective**: Believers should "look a great way back" in praise, learning from Old Testament examples while recognizing that "our histories greatly outshine theirs" in the Christian era.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-7**: Henry identifies seven worship duties: giving thanks, calling on God's name, making known His deeds, singing psalms, glorying in His name, seeking Him, and rejoicing. He particularly emphasizes "seek his face evermore" as pursuing God's favor "in an infinite progression."

**Verses 8-11**: The covenant with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob receives triple confirmation—by oath, law, and everlasting covenant. Henry notes this promise of Canaan typifies "the promise of eternal life made in Christ to all believers."

**Verses 12-24**: Henry observes how God protected the patriarchs when they were "few, very few, and strangers," calling them His "anointed" and "prophets," demonstrating that God's protection extends even to those in vulnerable circumstances.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry provides extensive scriptural connections throughout his commentary:

- Links to **1 Chronicles 16:7** for the psalm's historical context and authorship
- References **Hebrews 11:8-16** regarding the patriarchs' faith and God's promises
- Connects the Canaan promise to **Ephesians 1:11** and **1 John 2:25** concerning eternal inheritance
- Cites **Jeremiah 9:23-24** on proper boasting and **Deuteronomy 6:7** on discussing God's works
- References **Hosea 12:3** regarding Jacob's strength in seeking God

Henry consistently demonstrates how Old Testament narratives prefigure New Testament realities, encouraging Christians to see themselves as "concerned in the affairs of the Old-Testament church" since "these things happened to it for ensamples."

Chapter 106
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 106: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 106 as a powerful study in divine grace contrasted with human rebellion. The central theological theme is that "our badness makes his goodness appear the more illustrious, as his goodness makes our badness the more heinous and scandalous." Henry emphasizes that even confessions of sin should not diminish praise to God—hence the psalm's framework of "Hallelujah" beginning and ending.

The commentary highlights God's covenant faithfulness despite Israel's repeated provocations. Henry notes that God saved Israel "for his name's sake" rather than their merit, demonstrating that divine purposes transcend human failure. He presents this as an encouragement that "though we are justly corrected for our sins, yet we shall not be utterly abandoned."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**On Praise and Worship**: Christians should give thanks not only for God's goodness but acknowledge their own sinfulness as a "foil" that makes divine mercy more brilliant. He emphasizes doing "all we can" in praise, even though "who can show forth all" God's mighty acts.

**On Righteous Living**: True blessedness belongs to "those that keep judgment" and "do righteousness at all times"—maintaining consistent godliness "in all manner of conversation, at every turn, in every instance."

**On Historical Memory**: Henry urges believers to learn from Israel's failures, noting that "we lose the benefit of providences for want of understanding them." The psalm should humble us regarding "our sins, the sins of our land, and the sins of our fathers."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-5**: Henry sees these as teaching three essential postures: blessing God for His greatness and goodness, recognizing the happiness of the righteous, and seeking God's favor as our ultimate good. He particularly notes the believer's desire to "see the good of thy chosen" as the highest aspiration.

**Verses 6-12**: The confession "We have sinned with our fathers" Henry interprets both as imitation of ancestral sins and participation in hereditary guilt. He emphasizes Israel's "strange stupidity"—they saw God's wonders but "understood not" their meaning, demonstrating how "blessed are those that have not seen, and yet have understood."

**Verses 13-15**: Henry highlights the tragic irony that God gave them "their request; but sent leanness into their soul," showing how getting what we sinfully desire can be spiritual impoverishment.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout his commentary:

- Links the psalm's opening and closing verses to **1 Chronicles 16:34-36**, suggesting Davidic authorship
- Connects Israel's sin-pattern to **Numbers 32:14** and **Matthew 23:32** regarding filling up ancestral iniquity
- References **Exodus 14:11-12** and **14:30-31** for the Red Sea narrative details
- Cites **Psalm 104:7** regarding God's rebuke of waters in creation
- Draws on **Isaiah 63:12-14** for the wilderness guidance imagery
- References **Isaiah 29:24** about those who "erred in spirit" coming to understanding

Henry's treatment demonstrates how Israel's history serves as both warning and encouragement—their failures reveal human depravity, while God's persistent mercy offers hope to all who, like Israel, repeatedly fail yet continue to seek divine grace.

Chapter 107
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 107: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents **God's universal providence** as the central theme, emphasizing that God is "not only King of saints, but King of nations, not only the God of Israel, but the God of the whole earth, and a common Father to all mankind." The psalm demonstrates God's care extending beyond Israel to all who call upon Him in distress.

Henry identifies a **fourfold pattern of divine deliverance**: banishment/dispersion (vv. 2-9), captivity/imprisonment (vv. 10-16), sickness (vv. 17-22), and maritime dangers (vv. 23-32). Each section follows the pattern of distress, crying to the Lord, divine intervention, and the call for thanksgiving.

The commentary emphasizes **sin as the root of suffering**, particularly in the captivity and sickness sections, where Henry notes that affliction serves both as punishment for past sins and correction from future ones.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons: **Prayer as the universal response to trouble** - "When troubles become extreme that is man's time to cry; those who but whispered prayer before then cry aloud, and then it is God's time to succour."

He emphasizes **gratitude as obligation**: "It is expected of those who receive mercy from God that they return praise to him." This includes acknowledging "God's goodness to the children of men as well as to the children of God, to others as well as to ourselves."

The commentary applies the wandering motif spiritually: "we are here as in a wilderness, have here no continuing city, but dwell in tents as strangers and pilgrims. But we are under the guidance of his wise and good providence."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry notes that God's providence in travel deserves recognition: "We ought to take notice of the good hand of God's providence over us in our journeys, going out and coming in, directing us in our way." 

Regarding imprisonment, he observes that "Prisoners have time to pray, who, when they were at liberty, could not find time; they see they have need of God's help."

On divine power overcoming obstacles: "When God will work deliverance the greatest difficulties that lie in the way shall be made nothing of. Gates of brass and bars of iron...cannot keep them in when the time, the set-time, for their enlargement, comes."

## Cross-References to Scripture

Henry references **John 11:52** and **Matthew 24:31** regarding the gathering of God's scattered children. He cites **Zechariah 4:6** about deliverance "not by might or power" and **Isaiah 45:13** about redemption without "price or reward."

Other references include **Deuteronomy 30:4** and **Ezekiel 34:12** on gathering the dispersed, and **Deuteronomy 32:10** regarding God's guidance in the wilderness.

This psalm serves as both historical reflection on Israel's experiences and universal comfort for all who face life's common calamities, with Henry concluding that whether in distress or not, "others are, and have been, of whose deliverances it becomes us to give God the glory, for we are members one of another."

Chapter 108
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 108: Summary

## Structure and Composition

Matthew Henry notes that Psalm 108 uniquely combines material from two earlier psalms—verses 1-5 from Psalm 57 and verses 6-13 from Psalm 60. He observes this teaches us "that we may in prayer use the same words that we have formerly used, provided it be with new affections." The psalm divides into thanksgiving (vv. 1-5) and prayer (vv. 6-13), with "faith at work in both."

## Main Theological Themes

**God's Mercy and Truth**: Henry emphasizes these as central divine attributes worthy of praise. He notes that God's "mercy in promising, truth in performing" exceeds even the vastness of creation: "The heavens are vast, but the mercy of God is more capacious; the skies are high and bright, but the truth of God is more eminent, more illustrious."

**Divine Sovereignty**: The commentary stresses God's ultimate control over nations and enemies, with Henry drawing connections to Christ's eventual dominion over all things.

**Faith and Assurance**: Henry highlights how believers can "rejoice in what God has said, though it be not yet done; for with him saying and doing are not two things."

## Key Practical Applications

**Wholehearted Worship**: Henry instructs that praise must involve "fixedness of heart" where "wandering straggling thoughts must be gathered in, and kept close to the business." He advocates for worship with "fervency of affection" and "freeness of expression."

**Public Testimony**: Believers should praise God openly, "as those that are not ashamed to own our obligations to him." Henry encourages singing psalms even when "our neighbours hear us, for it looks like being ashamed of our Master."

**Persistent Prayer**: Even when "Providence has in some instances frowned upon us," believers shouldn't be "discouraged in prayer, nor beaten off from our hold of God."

**Dependence on God**: Henry emphasizes complete reliance on divine help: "Vain is the help of man" and "it is only through God that we shall do valiantly."

## Notable Cross-References

Henry connects the psalm to several key scriptures: Psalm 45:1 regarding the tongue as "the pen of a ready writer," John 6:37 about Christ not losing those given to him, Hebrews 2:8 concerning Christ's ultimate victory, Philippians 4:13 on doing all things through Christ's strength, and Genesis 27:37 regarding Jacob's blessing over Esau.

## Conclusion

Henry presents this psalm as a model for balanced spiritual life—beginning with heartfelt praise and moving to confident prayer. He emphasizes that both acts require faith, with worship demanding our complete devotion and prayer requiring unwavering trust in God's promises and power.

Chapter 109
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 109 Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 109 as both a historical psalm about David's persecution and a prophetic psalm pointing to Christ's sufferings. The commentary emphasizes that David's imprecations were "not of passion, but of the Spirit of prophecy," specifically noting that verse 8 is applied to Judas in Acts 1:20. Henry presents this as a psalm of divine justice, where God's righteous judgment falls upon the enemies of His people.

The psalm is structured around four movements: complaint against enemies, imprecatory prayers, personal petitions for help, and confident expectation of divine vindication. Henry sees this pattern as both David's experience and a foreshadowing of Christ's persecution and ultimate triumph.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Prayer as Primary Response**: When facing persecution, David "gives himself unto prayer" (v. 4). Henry notes: "A good man is made up of prayer, gives himself to prayer, as the apostles." This demonstrates that prayer should be our primary response to opposition, not revenge.

**Maintaining Devotion Despite Opposition**: Even when enemies mocked David's religion, "he kept close to it; they laughed at him for his devotion, but they could not laugh him out of it." Believers must persevere in faith regardless of persecution.

**Trusting God's Justice**: The psalm teaches believers to commit their cause to God rather than seeking personal vengeance, finding "comfort with the believing foresight of the certain destruction of all the enemies of Christ and his church."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry highlights David's address to the "God of my praise," explaining that "forasmuch as God is the God of our mercies we must make him the God of our praises; if all is of him and from him, all must be to him and for him."

**Verses 6-20**: Henry emphasizes these imprecations are prophetic, particularly noting the application to Judas. He warns against misusing these verses for personal vendettas, calling such practice "detestable sacrilege."

**Verse 16**: The justification for judgment centers on the enemy's lack of mercy: "he remembered not to show mercy, but persecuted the poor and needy man."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry provides several scriptural connections:

- **Acts 1:20**: Direct application of verse 8 to Judas's replacement
- **Psalm 17:2**: Parallel appeal for God's judgment
- **Acts 6:4**: Apostolic dedication to prayer
- **1 Samuel 12:23**: Duty to pray for enemies
- **John 10:32**: Christ's persecution despite good works
- **Zechariah 3:1-2**: Satan as accuser

## Key Memorable Phrases

Henry offers several striking observations: He describes the enemies as showing "implacable enmity to a good man because of his goodness" and notes the tragic irony that "the more he endeavoured to gratify them the more they hated him." 

Regarding prayer, Henry beautifully states that a believer "is in his element when he is at prayer." He also warns against the "detestable piece of sacrilege" of using these imprecations for personal revenge, calling it an attempt "to kindle strife with coals snatched from God's altar."

The commentary concludes with assurance that believers should find comfort in "the certain destruction of all the enemies of Christ and his church, and the certain salvation of all those that trust in God and keep close to him."

Chapter 110
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 110: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies Psalm 110 as "pure gospel" that speaks "only, and wholly, concerning Christ, the Messiah." He calls it "David's creed" because it contains "almost all the articles of the Christian faith." The psalm demonstrates Christ's threefold office as **prophet** (v.2), **priest** (v.4), and **king** (vv.1,3,5,6), encompassing both his humiliation and exaltation.

The central mystery Henry explores is how David could call his descendant "Lord"—a question Jesus himself posed to the Pharisees. This establishes Christ's divine nature and authority even over his earthly lineage.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry emphasizes that believers must **"act faith upon Christ, submit ourselves entirely to him"** and **"triumph in him as our prophet, priest, and king."** He stresses that **"the dew of our youth, even in the morning of our days, ought to be consecrated to our Lord Jesus."**

The commentary calls Christians to be a **"willing people"** who serve Christ voluntarily, not under compulsion. Henry notes: **"Christ's people are a willing people. The conversion of a soul consists in its being willing to be Christ's, coming under his yoke and into his interests, with an entire compliancy and satisfaction."**

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Christ's session at God's right hand represents rest after his labors, ruling authority, and permanent dominion. His enemies will become his "footstool"—subdued easily and permanently.

**Verse 2**: The "rod of strength" represents the gospel's power proceeding from Zion (Jerusalem), combining both a royal scepter and shepherd's staff, showing Christ's authority and tender care.

**Verse 3**: Christ's people will serve "in the beauty of holiness"—both attracted by holiness and clothed in it. The "dew of youth" imagery suggests multitudes of young converts flocking to Christ like morning dew drops.

**Verse 4**: Christ's priesthood "after the order of Melchizedek" is confirmed by God's oath, establishing an eternal priesthood distinct from the Levitical system.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to the New Testament, particularly **Matthew 22:41-46** (Jesus questioning the Pharisees), **Hebrews 7** (Melchizedek's priesthood), and **2 Corinthians 10:4** (gospel's mighty power). He also references **Revelation 19:14** (armies of heaven in white linen), **Isaiah 53:1**, **Romans 1:16**, and **1 Thessalonians 1:5** regarding the gospel's power.

The commentary demonstrates how this Old Testament psalm finds complete fulfillment in Christ's work, making it a foundational messianic text that bridges both testaments and reveals the eternal plan of redemption through the God-man who serves as humanity's ultimate Prophet, Priest, and King.

Chapter 111
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 111: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 111 as an alphabetically structured hymn of praise emphasizing God's magnificent works and character. The central theological theme is **God's eternal righteousness and faithfulness manifested through His works**. Henry identifies this as a liturgical psalm composed by David "for the service of the church in their solemn feasts," highlighting its corporate worship function.

The commentary emphasizes God's **immutable nature**: "His righteousness endures for ever. Whatever he does, he never did, nor ever will do, any wrong to any of his creatures." Henry sees all of God's works as unified—"it is his work, such is the beauty and harmony of Providence"—demonstrating divine coherence across creation and redemption.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living:

**Corporate and Private Worship**: "We must praise God both in private and in public, in less and greater assemblies, in our own families and in the courts of the Lord's house." He emphasizes maintaining "private meetings for devotion" alongside public assemblies.

**Diligent Study of God's Works**: Those who love God should not "take up with a superficial transient view" but "diligently search into" His works. Henry encourages studying "both natural and political history" to "discover the greatness and glory of God's works."

**Fear of the Lord as Wisdom**: The commentary concludes that "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom" and "A good understanding have all those that do his commandments," connecting reverence with practical wisdom.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry emphasizes wholehearted praise: "My heart, my whole heart, being devoted to his honour, shall be employed in this work."

**Verses 2-3**: God's works are "great like himself" with "nothing mean or trifling." The works display unified purpose—"all its dispensations centre in one design."

**Verse 4**: God's works are "admirable and memorable, fit to be registered and kept on record," with divine institutions like Passover preserving remembrance.

**Verse 5**: Henry interprets "meat" both literally (daily provision) and historically (manna, Egyptian spoils), showing God's covenant faithfulness.

**Verses 7-8**: Divine immutability: "What God has done shall never be undone... his works are firm and unchangeable."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout:

- **Ezekiel 10:13** (the wheels) - illustrating providential harmony
- **Psalm 25:14** - "the secret of the Lord is with those that fear him"
- **Ecclesiastes 3:14** - permanence of God's works
- **Exodus 34:6** - God's proclamation of grace and mercy to Moses
- **Genesis 15:14** - promise of coming out "with great substance"
- **Psalm 74:14** - giving Leviathan as meat to the people
- **Psalm 105:8** - God's eternal covenant remembrance
- **Proverbs 3:4** - finding favor and good understanding

Henry particularly connects this psalm to redemptive history, seeing Old Testament deliverances as "typical of the great redemption which in the fulness of time was to be wrought out by the Lord Jesus." The commentary masterfully demonstrates how praise of God's works should lead to practical wisdom and obedient living.

Chapter 112
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 112: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 112 as an alphabetical psalm that demonstrates "how much it is our wisdom to fear God and do his commandments." The central theological theme contrasts the blessedness of the righteous with the misery of the wicked, showing how "good and evil are set before us, the blessing and the curse."

Henry emphasizes that true righteousness stems from proper fear of God—not slavish terror, but reverential awe combined with delight in God's commandments. He describes the blessed person as one who "delights greatly in his commandments" and "thinks himself happy under God's government as well as in his favour."

## Key Practical Applications

**Character Development**: Henry identifies essential qualities of the blessed person: they are "well-principled with pious and devout affections," honest and sincere in their professions, and "both just and kind in all their dealings." 

**Financial Stewardship**: A significant emphasis falls on charitable giving. The righteous person "shows favour and lends" from "pure charity" and "disperses" his giving rather than concentrating it. Henry notes that "sometimes there is as much charity in lending as in giving."

**Emotional Stability**: Henry emphasizes cultivating a "settled spirit" through trusting God. The righteous "shall not be afraid of evil tidings" because their "heart is fixed, trusting in the LORD."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The fear of the Lord represents reverence "as a Father, with the disposition of a child, not of a slave."

**Verses 2-3**: Henry distinguishes between wealth "in his heart" versus "in his house," noting that righteousness "endures for ever" while material wealth must be left to others.

**Verse 4**: "Unto the upright there arises light in the darkness" promises comfort in affliction—not exemption from trouble, but divine support through it.

**Verse 5**: Discretion in affairs includes both business wisdom and charitable discernment, ensuring good stewardship.

**Verses 6-9**: The righteous achieve lasting remembrance and honor, particularly through their generosity to the poor.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Psalm 1:2** - regarding delight in God's law
- **Romans 11:28** - "beloved for the Father's sake" 
- **Micah 7:8** - "when they sit in darkness, the Lord shall be a light to them"
- **Isaiah 28:26** - God "instructs him to discretion"
- **James 1:5** - God gives wisdom "liberally"
- **2 Corinthians 9:9** - regarding charitable giving
- **1 Peter 1:7** - righteousness "found unto praise, and honour, and glory"

Henry concludes by noting that while the wicked "shall see it, and be grieved," the righteous enjoy both temporal blessings and eternal rewards, making the psalm both a practical guide for holy living and an encouragement for perseverance in righteousness.

Chapter 113
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 113: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 113 as a **hallelujah psalm** designed to promote the "great and good work of praising God." The commentary revolves around two central theological pillars: **God's transcendent glory** and **His condescending grace**. Henry emphasizes that these themes "very much illustrate one another, that we may be duly affected with both."

The psalm demonstrates God's **incomparable greatness** - He is "high above all nations" and His "glory above the heavens." Yet paradoxically, this same exalted God "humbles himself to behold the things that are in heaven, and in the earth." Henry notes this as divine condescension, stating it's "an act of wonderful condescension that God is pleased to take into the thoughts of his eternal counsel...both the armies of heaven and the inhabitants of the earth."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for believers:

**Praise as Essential Duty**: Praising God is presented as "a necessary and most excellent duty, greatly pleasing to God." Henry emphasizes believers should "much abound in" this practice and be "frequently employed and greatly enlarged" in it.

**Universal and Perpetual Praise**: The call to praise extends across all places "from the rising of the sun to the going down of the same" and through all time "from this time forth and for evermore." Henry sees this as anticipating the gospel's global spread when "in every place spiritual incense shall be offered to our God."

**Hope for the Lowly**: God's pattern of exalting the humble provides practical encouragement. Henry applies this both to temporal circumstances and spiritual redemption, noting how "poor fallen men are raised out of the dust...and set among princes, among angels."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry observes the repeated calls to praise indicate both the necessity of this duty and human tendency to neglect it, requiring "precept upon precept and line upon line."

**Verses 4-6**: The rhetorical question "Who is like unto the LORD our God?" emphasizes God's incomparability. Henry notes even angels cannot adequately praise Him, as "He is exalted above all blessing and praise, not only all ours, but all theirs."

**Verses 7-9**: Henry sees these verses as illustrating God's surprising providence that "delights in doing things we looked not for," showing "the world is governed, not by a course of nature...but by a God of nature."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry enriches his commentary with extensive scriptural connections:

- **1 Peter 2:9** - connecting all Christians as "a royal priesthood, to show forth the praises"
- **Malachi 1:11** - linking universal praise to gospel expansion
- **1 Samuel 2:6-8** - Hannah's song paralleling themes of divine reversal
- **Isaiah 54:1 and Galatians 4:27** - applying the barren woman motif to the Gentile church
- **1 Corinthians 1:27-28** - God choosing "weak and foolish ones" to confound the mighty

The commentary masterfully weaves together doctrinal truth with practical exhortation, presenting a God who is simultaneously "infinitely above" His creation yet intimately involved in transforming the lives of the humble and needy.

Chapter 114
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 114: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry centers his commentary on **God's redemptive power** displayed in Israel's exodus from Egypt. He views this psalm as celebrating the foundational miracle that "gave birth to their church and nation." The exodus serves as both a historical reality and a prophetic type pointing to "the much greater work of wonder, our redemption by Christ."

Henry emphasizes **God's sovereignty over nature**, noting how the Almighty commands seas, mountains, and rocks to serve His redemptive purposes. The commentary presents a **theocratic vision** where God establishes both sanctuary and dominion among His people, making them His living temples with "Holiness to the Lord" written upon them.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for believers:

1. **Remembrance and Worship**: "Time, as it does not wear out the guilt of sin, so it should not wear out the sense of mercy." Christians must continually remember God's mighty works.

2. **Trust in Difficulties**: Believers should encourage "ourselves and others to trust in God in the greatest straits," knowing that "there is no sea, no Jordan, so deep, so broad" that God cannot overcome for His people's redemption.

3. **Submission to God's Rule**: Henry emphasizes that "what is God's sanctuary must be his dominion. Those only have the privileges of his house that submit to the laws of it."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes Israel preserved their language among oppressors as "an earnest of their deliverance," and emphasizes God's purpose: "Let my people go, that they may serve me."

**Verses 3-6**: The poetical questioning "What ailed thee, O thou sea!" serves multiple purposes: demonstrating the miracle's reality ("it was at the presence of the Lord"), showing God's mercy toward Israel, and expressing "wonder and surprise" at nature's submission to God's will.

**Verses 7-8**: Henry marvels that "the same almighty power that turned waters into a rock to be a wall to Israel turned the rock into waters to be a well to Israel."

## Cross-References and Connections

Henry makes extensive scriptural connections:

- **Typological**: Links to **1 Corinthians 10:4** ("that rock was Christ"), seeing Christ as "a fountain of living waters to his Israel"
- **Gospel Application**: Connects to the early church's triumph over "Satan and the powers of darkness" and the silencing of "heathen oracles"
- **Personal Regeneration**: Applies the imagery to individual conversion, asking what causes "lusts and corruptions" to "fly back" - answering "it is at the presence of God's Spirit"

Henry references multiple passages including **Hosea 11:1**, **Habakkuk 3:8-13**, **Exodus 15:15**, **Mark 1:34**, **Revelation 6:15**, and **2 Corinthians 10:5** to demonstrate the psalm's broader biblical significance.

The commentary concludes that this historical deliverance prefigures Christ's greater redemptive work, encouraging believers to trust in God's power to overcome any obstacle for His people's salvation.

Chapter 115
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 115

### **Main Theological Themes**

Matthew Henry frames Psalm 115 around the central theme of **giving glory to God alone**, contrasting this with the futility of idolatry and self-glorification. The psalm teaches believers to ascribe all honor "Not unto us, O LORD, not unto us, but unto thy name" - a phrase Henry emphasizes as excluding all human boasting. He presents God as sovereign ("Our God is in the heavens: he hath done whatsoever he hath pleased") while systematically demolishing the credibility of idol worship through logical argument.

### **Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Humility in Success and Service**: When believers receive mercy or achieve success, they must "not assume the glory...but ascribe it wholly to God." He warns against thinking we accomplish anything "by our own strength" or deserve anything "by our own righteousness."

**Prayer Motivation**: In seeking God's help, believers should prioritize God's glory over personal benefit, asking "Lord, do so and so for us, not that we may have the credit and comfort of it, but that thy mercy and truth may have the glory of it."

**Trust Over Anxiety**: Henry encourages active faith, noting "it is wisdom to trust in the living God" who serves as "a help and a shield" to his people.

### **Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 4-8** receive extensive treatment as Henry mocks the "ridiculous" nature of idols - "silver and gold" creations that "seem to be something, but were really nothing, fitter for a toy shop than a temple." He notes the tragic irony that idol-makers "show their ingenuity" while idol-worshippers "show their stupidity" and become "like unto them, as senseless blockish things."

**Verses 9-11** emphasize universal trust in God across all social groups - Israel, Aaron's house, and God-fearers, with Henry noting that "wherever there is an awful fear of God, there may be a cheerful faith in him."

### **Cross-References**

Henry weaves numerous scriptural connections throughout, including Deuteronomy 8:17 and 9:4 (against self-righteousness), John 12:27-28 (prioritizing God's glory), Hosea 8:6 ("The workmen made it, therefore it is not God"), Romans 1:28 (divine judgment on idolaters), and Acts 10:34-35 (God's acceptance of all who fear Him). These references reinforce his arguments about humility, God's sovereignty, and the absurdity of idolatry.

The commentary ultimately presents this psalm as a "holy triumph" where the church places confidence in God rather than dwelling on complaints, making it suitable for times of distress when enemies threaten God's people.

Chapter 116
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 116: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry identifies this as a **thanksgiving psalm** expressing David's profound gratitude for divine deliverance. The central theological theme revolves around **God's faithfulness in answering prayer** and the believer's appropriate response of devotion. Henry emphasizes God's character as "gracious, righteous, and merciful," particularly highlighting His care for "the simple"—those who are sincere, upright, and dependent on God rather than their own sufficiency.

The commentary reveals a **covenant relationship** between God and believer, where divine mercy creates obligations of love, service, and public testimony. Henry notes the "wonderful condescension in God to hear prayer," describing how God "lays his ear, as it were, to the mouth of prayer."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Prayer Life**: Past answers to prayer should encourage continued prayer. "The experiences we have had of God's goodness to us, in answer to prayer, are great encouragements to us to continue praying." He advocates lifelong prayer: "As long as we continue living we must continue praying."

**Rest in God**: Believers should "return unto thy rest, O my soul"—finding ultimate peace and satisfaction in God alone rather than seeking rest in created things. Henry beautifully suggests this as appropriate words "to close our eyes with at night" and even at death.

**Walking Before God**: Christians must "walk before the Lord," conducting themselves consciously in His presence with holy living and cheerful confidence.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes David's abrupt beginning "I love the Lord" shows a heart overflowing with devotion, reflecting "compliance with the first and great commandment."

**Verse 6**: "The Lord preserveth the simple"—Henry interprets "simple" as both sincere/upright and weak/helpless, showing God protects those who trust Him rather than their own wisdom.

**Verse 15**: "Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of his saints"—Henry calls this the reason some term this "the Martyr's psalm."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Psalm 18:1** for similar expressions of love to God
- **Jeremiah 8:6** regarding God's attentive hearing
- **Matthew 11:28** connecting soul's rest to Christ's invitation to the weary
- **2 Corinthians 4:13** where Paul quotes verse 10 about believing and speaking
- **Genesis 17:1** on walking before God

Henry demonstrates how this psalm connects to broader biblical themes of prayer, divine faithfulness, and covenant relationship, making it relevant for all believers experiencing God's deliverance and seeking to respond with appropriate gratitude and devotion.

Chapter 117
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalms 117: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Matthew Henry emphasizes the **gospel significance** of this brief psalm, describing it as containing "a great deal of gospel." The primary theological theme centers on **God's universal call to all nations** through the gospel of Christ. Henry notes that while "for many ages in Judah only was God known," this psalm prophetically envisions the breaking down of the "partition-wall" between Jews and Gentiles. He identifies this as fulfillment of the mystery "that the Gentiles should be fellow-heirs" (Ephesians 3:3,6), representing the vast expansion from Old Testament particularism to New Testament universalism.

The psalm reveals **God's mercy and truth as foundational attributes** shining "most brightly" in the gospel. Henry explains that "God's mercy is the fountain of all our comforts and his truth the foundation of all our hopes."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry warns against superficial engagement with this psalm, noting people may "sing it so often...for the shortness of it" rather than "for the sweetness of it." He emphasizes maintaining "pious and devout affections" during worship, warning that "we are soon weary indeed of well-doing" without proper spiritual engagement.

The commentary calls believers to recognize their privileged position: "Those are highly favoured whom God invites by his word and inclines by his Spirit to praise him." This should motivate grateful worship and recognition of divine grace.

## Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The call to "all nations" represents the gospel's global scope. Henry notes these are "the same words used for the heathen that rage" (Psalm 2:1), showing how God's enemies become "willing subjects." The repeated call to praise indicates that gospel tidings "give them cause," gospel ordinances "give them leave," and gospel grace "give them hearts to praise him."

**Verse 2**: Henry highlights God's "merciful kindness" as "strong" and "mighty for the pardon of mighty sins." God's truth "endures forever," ensuring the promise's fulfillment even when "Jews were hardened and expelled" through "believing Gentiles, the spiritual seed of Abraham."

## Cross-References

Henry extensively uses **Romans 15:8-11**, where Paul quotes this psalm as proof that "gospel was to be preached to...Gentile nations." Other key references include **Ephesians 3:3,6** (Gentile inclusion), **Matthew 24:14** and **Mark 16:15** (gospel commission), **Revelation 7:9-10** (universal worship), and **Jeremiah 13:11** (God's people as "name and praise").

Henry concludes that this psalm prophetically celebrates the gospel age when all nations would join in praising God through Christ.

Chapter 118
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalms 118

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 118 as having dual authorship and application—written by David upon gaining "full possession of the kingdom" but ultimately pointing to Christ's messianic kingdom. The psalm demonstrates **typological fulfillment**, with David's experiences serving as a "figure of the exaltation of Christ." Henry emphasizes that "Christ himself applies it to himself (Matt. xxi. 42)," establishing clear messianic significance.

The central theological theme is **God's enduring mercy and goodness**. Henry notes how "David had his heart full of the goodness of God" and argues that "the more our hearts are impressed with a sense of God's goodness the more they will be enlarged in all manner of obedience."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Trust over human confidence**: From verses 8-9, he emphasizes it is "more wise, more comfortable, and more safe...to trust in the Lord, than to put confidence in man, yea, though it be in princes." This reflects the superiority of divine over human assistance.

**Prayer in distress**: David's experience shows that God "answered me with enlargements; he did more for me than I was able to ask." This encourages believers to call upon God during trials.

**Universal praise**: Henry notes that "Priests and people, Jews and proselytes, must all own God's goodness" - emphasizing that thanksgiving transcends social and ethnic boundaries.

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-4**: The refrain "his mercy endureth for ever" represents mercy flowing from "the everlasting fountain, God himself" through "never-failing streams" that "run parallel with the longest line of eternity."

**Verses 10-12**: Henry interprets the enemies compassing "like bees" as being "so numerous...so noisy, so vexatious" but ultimately self-destructive, "quenched as the fire of thorns" which "burns furiously for a while...but is presently out."

**Verse 14**: "The Lord is my strength and song" means making God both our support "for doing and suffering" and our source of joy to "express my joy and ease my grief."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm extensively to Christ's experience, noting how Jesus was hated "without a cause" and "Jews and Romans surrounded him." He references **Matthew 21:42** for Christ's self-application of the psalm.

Other key connections include:
- **Hebrews 13:6** - "The Lord is my helper"
- **2 Corinthians 6:9** - "As chastened, and not killed"
- **Isaiah 51:12** - "Who art thou, that thou shouldst be afraid of a man that shall die?"
- **1 Samuel 17:45** - David facing Goliath "in the name of the God of Israel"

Henry concludes that "in singing this psalm we must glorify God for his goodness...especially his goodness to us in Jesus Christ," connecting Old Testament experience to New Testament fulfillment.

Chapter 119
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 119: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 119 as the crown jewel of the Psalter, "excelling them all" and shining "brightest in this constellation." He interprets it as a **magnificent tribute to divine revelation**, designed "to magnify the law, and make it honourable." The psalm emphasizes **the excellency and usefulness of divine revelation** as both entertainment and governance for the believer's life.

Henry identifies **ten synonymous terms** for Scripture throughout the psalm: God's law, way, testimonies, commandments, precepts, word, judgments, righteousness, statutes, and truth/faithfulness. Each term expresses "the whole compass" of divine revelation and demonstrates the psalmist's comprehensive reverence for God's Word.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**1. Cultivating Pious Ejaculations**: He suggests the psalm represents David's "short and sudden breathings and elevations of his soul to God," teaching believers to maintain "constant communion with God" through brief, heartfelt prayers.

**2. Memorization and Meditation**: The acrostic structure serves as "a help to them both in committing it to memory and calling it to mind upon occasion." Henry recommends having it "as ready in our memories as the very letters of our alphabet."

**3. Universal Application**: The commentary emphasizes that "here is something or other to suit the case of every Christian. Is any afflicted? Is any merry? Each will find that here which is proper for him."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

In the opening **Aleph section (verses 1-8)**, Henry identifies five characteristics of blessed people:

- Those who make God's will "the rule of all their actions"
- Those who are "upright and honest in their religion—undefiled in the way"  
- Those who "keep his testimonies as a treasure of inestimable value"
- Those who "seek him with their whole heart" as their "chief good and highest end"
- Those who "carefully avoid all sin" and "do not allow themselves in any sin"

Henry notes that verse 6 teaches that those with "sincere respect to all God's commandments shall not be ashamed," providing both confidence before God and "clearness and courage in their own souls."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to broader biblical themes, referencing:
- **Job 23:10-11; 31:7** regarding walking in God's ways rather than worldly paths
- **James 2:10-11** on respecting all commandments with equal authority
- **1 John 3:21** concerning confidence toward God through obedience

He emphasizes that true believers have "respect to the commands of both testaments and both tables," demonstrating comprehensive biblical obedience.

## Conclusion

Henry concludes that this psalm serves as "the saints' alphabet," providing "work for all the devout affections of a sanctified soul." His memorable assessment captures its transformative power: **"He that shall read it considerately, it will either warm him or shame him."** The commentary presents Psalm 119 as both a practical guide for devotional life and a theological masterpiece celebrating the supremacy of God's Word.

Chapter 120
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 120: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 120 as the first of the fifteen "Songs of Degrees," likely composed by David during his persecution by Saul (possibly relating to Doeg's accusations). The psalm presents three central theological themes: **divine deliverance through prayer**, **God's judgment against false accusers**, and the **believer's struggle in hostile environments**.

Henry emphasizes that "the God of truth is, and will be, the protector of his people from lying lips," establishing God's role as defender of the innocent against slander and deception. The commentary presents David as a type of Christ, noting that "David was herein a type of Christ, who was distressed by lying lips and deceitful tongues."

## Key Practical Applications

The commentary draws several practical lessons for believers facing similar trials:

**Prayer as the primary recourse**: Henry notes that when facing false accusations, David "had recourse to God by faithful and fervent prayer," appealing to "him who has all men's hearts in his hand" and "can bridle their tongues."

**Maintaining a peaceful disposition**: Henry praises David's character, stating "I am for peace" demonstrates that true wisdom "is first pure, then peaceable." Even when facing hostile opposition, believers should not "love peace the less for our seeking it in vain."

**Enduring hostile environments**: For those forced to live among ungodly people, Henry acknowledges this as "a very grievous burden to a good man" while encouraging perseverance and maintaining godly character despite opposition.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry explains that David faced distress through two types of lies: lies told *to* him (flattery and false friendship used to betray him) and lies told *about* him (false accusations). The "most dangerous enemies" are "such as carry on their malicious designs under the colour of friendship."

**Verses 3-4**: The divine judgment involves "sharp arrows of the Almighty, with coals of juniper." Henry explains these arrows "will pierce through the strongest armour and strike deep into the hardest heart," while the juniper coals represent God's wrath that burns intensely and persistently, "even when they seem to be gone out."

**Verses 5-7**: David's complaint about dwelling in "Mesech" and "Kedar" represents living among "rude and barbarous people." Henry interprets this as describing both separation from God's ordinances and forced cohabitation with hostile individuals who "hated peace."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to several other biblical passages. He references **Psalm 119:29, 163** regarding David's hatred of lying, **Psalm 37:6** about God protecting from false accusations, and **Psalm 12:3, 7** concerning God's judgment on enemies.

The commentary links the divine judgment imagery to **Job 6:4** (God's arrows as terrors) and **Psalm 64:7** (God shooting arrows at the wicked). For the ultimate fate of liars, Henry cites **Revelation 22:15**, noting that "all that love and make a lie shall have their portion in the lake that burns eternally."

The comparison of David's peaceful nature versus his enemies' hostility connects to **2 Peter 2:8** (Lot's torment in Sodom) and the principle from Romans: "Be not overcome of evil...but overcome evil with good."

Chapter 121
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 121: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents this psalm as a universal declaration of **divine protection and providence**. He rejects limiting it to military ("soldier's psalm") or travel contexts, emphasizing that "wherever we are, at home or abroad, we are exposed to danger more than we are aware of." The central theological theme is **complete dependence on God rather than human instruments**. Henry argues we must "not rely upon creatures, upon men and means, instruments and second causes, nor make flesh our arm," but instead recognize that God as Creator of "heaven and earth" possesses unlimited power for our protection.

The commentary emphasizes God's **covenant faithfulness**, noting that "the same that is the protector of the church in general is engaged for the preservation of every particular believer." This connects individual security to corporate divine promises.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for believers:

1. **Proper focus in worship**: Look to God's sanctuary and "special presence of God in his church" rather than earthly powers
2. **Confident daily living**: Trust God's protection in all circumstances, knowing "no real evil, no mere evil, shall happen to us"
3. **Perspective on affliction**: Understand that permitted trials are "what God sees good for us and will do us good by"
4. **Assurance in spiritual warfare**: Protection extends to both "open assaults of thy enemies" and "secret treacherous attempts"

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry offers dual interpretations of "lifting eyes to the hills" - either rejecting earthly help or looking toward Jerusalem's temple mount where God's presence dwells. He notes our help comes "from before the Lord," possibly referencing Christ's incarnation and intercession.

**Verses 3-4**: Emphasizes God as "a wakeful watchful keeper" who "never did, nor ever will" slumber, highlighting divine reliability versus human limitations.

**Verses 5-6**: God as "shade" represents both protection and refreshment, keeping believers safe from "malignant influences" both literal and figurative.

**Verses 7-8**: "Preserve thy going out and thy coming in" encompasses all life activities, from daily routines to "life and death" itself.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to broader biblical themes:

- **Jeremiah 3:23**: "In vain is salvation hoped for from hills and mountains"
- **Psalms 28:2; 42:6**: Looking toward the sanctuary for help
- **Isaiah 63:9**: Christ as "angel of his presence"
- **Isaiah 32:2**: God as "shadow of a great rock in a weary land"
- **1 Samuel 2:9**: God keeping "the feet of his saints"
- **Isaiah 27:3**: God's continual care "night and day"

The commentary concludes with eternal perspective: God's protection extends "from this time forth and even for evermore," with the Spirit as "preserver and comforter" who "shall abide with them for ever." Henry masterfully weaves together themes of divine omnipotence, covenant faithfulness, and practical Christian living under God's unchanging care.

Chapter 122
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 122

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 122 as David's composition designed to cultivate love for Jerusalem as the newly established holy city. He emphasizes three primary theological themes: **corporate worship**, **unity of God's people**, and **intercession for the church**. Henry notably connects the psalm to the gospel church, stating that "in singing this psalm we must have an eye to the gospel church, which is called the 'Jerusalem that is from above.'" This typological interpretation sees earthly Jerusalem as prefiguring the Christian church.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications centered on **public worship and community**. He emphasizes that corporate worship is God's will: "It is the will of God that we should worship him in concert." He advocates for mutual encouragement in worship, noting that "we should not only agree with one another, but excite and stir up one another, to go to worship God in public," using the metaphor that "iron sharpens iron."

Regarding **prayer for the church**, Henry instructs believers to earnestly intercede for the gospel church's peace and welfare. He emphasizes that such prayers must be accompanied by action: "Whatever lies within the sphere of our activity to do for the public good we must do it, else we are not sincere in praying for it."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry highlights the **joy in worship invitations**, noting that David "was glad" when called to worship, demonstrating that "those that rejoice in God will rejoice in calls and opportunities to wait upon him."

**Verses 3-5**: Jerusalem is praised as the **beautiful, holy, and royal city**. Henry describes it as "compact together in holy love and Christian communion, so that it is all as one city"—a type of gospel unity. He notes the harmony between religious and civil authority: "Let the testimony of Israel direct the thrones of judgment, and the thrones of judgment protect the testimony of Israel."

**Verses 6-9**: The call to "pray for the peace of Jerusalem" receives extensive treatment. Henry provides the encouragement that "those shall prosper that love thee" and emphasizes that true concern for public welfare stems from "sincere love to God's institutions and his faithful worshippers."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry incorporates several scriptural cross-references: **Zechariah 8:21** supports mutual encouragement in worship; **Zechariah 3:7** and **Psalm 84:10** illustrate the privilege of having any place in Jerusalem; **1 Chronicles 28:2** and **29:3** demonstrate David's affection for God's house and his people. These references strengthen his arguments about corporate worship and devotion to God's dwelling place.

Henry's commentary masterfully balances historical context with timeless application, making this ancient psalm relevant for Christian worship and community life.

Chapter 123
----------------------------------------
# Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 123

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 123 as a prayer of the church during a time of severe persecution and humiliation, possibly during the Babylonian captivity. The psalm demonstrates the **proper Christian response to worldly contempt**: looking upward to God's mercy rather than retaliating against human oppression. 

Henry emphasizes God's heavenly dwelling not as spatial limitation but as a place of **divine perspective and power**—"a place of prospect and a place of power; he that dwells there beholds thence all the calamities of his people and thence can send to save them." The commentary stresses that when earthly circumstances seem hopeless, believers can find comfort knowing "Our God is in the heavens."

## Key Practical Applications

The central application revolves around the **servant-master relationship** metaphor. Henry draws six practical ways Christians should look to God as servants look to their masters:
1. For **direction** in their work and calling
2. For **daily provision** ("portion of meat in due season")
3. For **strength and assistance** in difficult tasks
4. For **protection** when persecuted ("We are thine; save us")
5. For **correction** when disciplined, submitting rather than fleeing
6. For **eternal reward** as their true recompense

Henry emphasizes that believers should respond to persecution by appealing to their heavenly Master rather than seeking worldly vindication.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry notes the transition from individual ("I") to corporate prayer, showing how personal faith leads to communal intercession. The phrase "dwellest in the heavens" points to God as judge who sits in ultimate authority over earthly oppressors.

**Verse 2**: The servant metaphor receives extensive treatment. Henry particularly emphasizes that true Christians "have their eye to God as their rewarder," contrasting with hypocrites who seek worldly recognition.

**Verses 3-4**: The repeated plea "have mercy upon us" shows proper prayer—"not prescribing to God what he shall do...nor pleading any merit of their own." Henry notes they were "exceedingly filled with contempt," emphasizing how deeply persecution affected the faithful.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Psalm 25:15**: "The eyes of a good man are ever towards the Lord"
- **Zechariah 9:1**: Mercy comes when "the eyes of man, as of all the tribes of Israel, are towards the Lord"
- **Matthew 6:2**: Contrasting hypocrites who "have their reward" from worldly recognition
- **Galatians 4:29**: Connecting Old Testament persecution (Ishmael mocking Isaac) to ongoing Christian experience
- **Psalm 42:10**: Describing contempt as "a sword in their bones"

## Concluding Application

Henry concludes that **"scorning and contempt have been, and are, and are likely to be, the lot of God's people in this world,"** but emphasizes the comfort available: **"there is mercy with God, mercy to our good names when they are barbarously used."** The proper response remains: "Hear, O our God! for we are despised."

Chapter 124
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry on Psalm 124: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 124 as David's thanksgiving for divine deliverance from overwhelming danger, emphasizing **God's sovereignty in salvation**. The central theme is that without the LORD's intervention, His people would have been completely destroyed. Henry notes the emphasis on "Jehovah" specifically, stating: "If it had not been Jehovah himself, a God of infinite power and perfection, that had undertaken our deliverance, our enemies would have overpowered us."

A key theological insight is **God's timing in deliverance** - He often allows enemies to "prevail very far against them, that his own power may appear the more illustrious in their deliverance." Henry also applies this typologically to **Christian redemption**: "we may have in our thoughts the great work of our redemption by Jesus Christ, by which we were rescued from the powers of darkness."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

1. **Proper attribution of deliverance**: "God is the author of all our deliverances, and therefore he must have the glory of them. We rob him of his due if we do not return thanks to him."

2. **Corporate worship and testimony**: Israel should collectively acknowledge God's goodness, with Henry emphasizing "Let Israel say this, to his honour, and resolve never to forsake him."

3. **Encouragement for future trials**: The psalm serves to encourage "hearts, and the hearts of others...to trust in God in the like dangers," building confidence for future challenges.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry structures the psalm around escalating imagery of destruction. Verses 1-5 use two powerful metaphors: being "swallowed up alive" like prey and being overwhelmed by "proud waters." He notes the progression: "The waters had overwhelmed us...the stream had gone over our souls...the proud waters."

For verses 6-7, Henry highlights the deliverance metaphors: rescue from "the very jaws of a beast of prey" and escape "like a bird, a little bird (the word signifies a sparrow), out of the snare of the fowler." He emphasizes how God's people become "as unable to help themselves out as any weak and silly bird is; and then is God's time to appear."

Verse 8's conclusion points to God as Creator: "Israel's God is the same that made the world, and therefore will have a church in the world, and can secure that church."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry references **Psalm 121:2** for comparison between personal help ("My help is in the name of the Lord") and corporate help ("Our help is so"). He also alludes to **Isaac's near-sacrifice**, noting "Isaac was saved when he lay ready to be sacrificed. Jehovah-jireh--in the mount of the Lord it shall be seen."

The commentary connects human malice to broader biblical themes, noting "Homo homini lupus--Man is a wolf to man" and referencing Revelation's imagery of persecution by those "drunk with the blood of saints."

Henry concludes that those who "lay the interests of God's Israel near their hearts" can find comfort in knowing "Israel's God is the same that made the world" and can secure His church through any crisis.

Chapter 125
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 125: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry frames Psalm 125 around the fundamental biblical contrast between the righteous and wicked, summarizing it with Isaiah 3:10-11: "Say you to the righteous, It shall be well with him. Woe to the wicked, it shall be [ill] with him." He identifies two primary theological movements: **certainty of blessing for God's people** and **certainty of judgment for the wicked and apostates**. 

Henry notes that while some Jewish rabbis see messianic references, gospel-church members can confidently claim these promises. The psalm demonstrates God's covenant faithfulness, showing how divine protection operates through both providence and grace.

## Key Practical Applications

**For Believers:** Henry emphasizes that God's people must be characterized by two qualities: righteousness (justified and sanctified before God and righteous toward others) and trust in the Lord. He notes that "all that deal with God must deal upon trust" and that "the closer our expectations are confined to God the higher our expectations may be raised from him."

**Regarding Trials:** Henry provides pastoral comfort, explaining that while "the rod of the wicked may come upon the lot of the righteous," God proportions trials to strength and strength to trials. He observes that "God considers the frame of his people" and warns that prolonged oppression could tempt even the righteous toward compromise.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2:** Henry draws a powerful analogy between Mount Zion's physical stability and spiritual security. Believers "shall be as Mount Zion, which cannot be removed," established by faith rather than circumstances. God's protection surrounds His people like Jerusalem's mountains, with "no gap in the hedge of protection."

**Verse 3:** The promise that the wicked's rod "shall not rest" upon the righteous demonstrates divine timing—troubles last only as long as believers can bear them without spiritual compromise.

**Verses 4-5:** Henry distinguishes between praying for the good (which believers should do) and predicting judgment for apostates. He notes that "crooked ways" represent sin as "the perverting of that which is right."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry enriches his commentary with numerous biblical connections: **Job 1:10** (God's protective hedge), **Hebrews 12:22** (church as Mount Zion), **Psalm 112:7** (establishment through faith), **Isaiah 7:9** (faith as fixation), **Job 14:18** (mountains' temporality vs. God's eternal covenant), **Revelation 3:12** (eternal security in heaven), and **Acts 12:23-24** (how judgment on the wicked advances God's kingdom).

## Memorable Phrases

Henry's commentary includes several striking observations: "Oppression makes a wise man mad, especially if it continue long," and his definition of apostates as those who "basely turn aside" from righteousness "for fear of the rod of the wicked." He concludes with the comforting truth that "Peace shall be upon Israel," showing how the destruction of the wicked ultimately "contributes to the peace and safety of the church."

The commentary balances theological depth with pastoral sensitivity, offering both doctrinal foundation and practical encouragement for believers facing trials.

Chapter 126
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 126 Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 126 as celebrating Israel's return from Babylonian captivity, most likely during Ezra's time, while viewing it as **typological of redemption through Christ**.
