The commentary emphasizes **God's sovereignty in deliverance** - though Cyrus politically proclaimed liberty, "it was the Lord's doing, according to his word many years before." Henry presents captivity not as destructive judgment but redemptive discipline: "God sent them into captivity, not as dross is put into the fire to be consumed, but as gold to be refined."

The central theological principle is that **God's people must accommodate themselves to all dispensations of Providence**, moving from mourning in captivity to celebration in deliverance. Henry notes the restoration of "Zion, the holy hill" emphasizes that "the restoring of their sacred interests, and the reviving of the public exercise of their religion, were the most valuable advantages."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

**Gratitude and Testimony**: Believers should give public expression to God's mercies, as Israel's "mouth was filled with laughter and our tongue with singing." This testimony impacts unbelievers, causing even "the heathen" to acknowledge God's greatness.

**Perseverance in Suffering**: The famous principle that **"weeping must not hinder sowing"** - believers must continue faithful duty during affliction. "When we suffer ill we must be doing well." Times of affliction should dispose us "to repentance, and prayer, and humiliation."

**Intercession for Others**: Even when blessed, we "must not be unmindful of our brethren that are in trouble and under restraint."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: The phrase "like them that dream" captures the **overwhelming surprise of divine deliverance** - "we thought it too good news to be true." Henry compares this to Peter's prison escape, where reality seemed like vision.

**Verses 5-6**: Henry's exposition of sowing and reaping is particularly rich. "Tears of sorrow for sin" and "tears of sympathy with the afflicted church" are themselves "precious seed" that believers sow. The metaphor extends to a farmer who "weeps to part with" scarce grain yet "buries it under ground, in expectation of receiving it again with advantage."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to multiple scriptures: **Psalm 137** (Babylon captivity), **Acts 12:9** (Peter's deliverance surprise), **Jeremiah 22:8-9** (heathen observation of Israel's calamity), **Nehemiah 1:3** (continued distress after return), **Proverbs 25:25** (good news as cold water), and **Hosea 9:1** (joy forbidden during unfaithfulness).

The commentary concludes with the eschatological promise that **"those that sow to the spirit, in this vale of tears, shall of the spirit reap life everlasting"** - connecting temporal deliverance to eternal salvation and referencing Christ's beatitude: "Blessed are those that mourn, for they shall be for ever comforted."

Chapter 127
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 127: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 127 as a "family-psalm" that emphasizes **absolute dependence on divine Providence** in all earthly endeavors. The central theological theme is that human effort without God's blessing is fundamentally futile. Henry interprets this psalm as David's instruction to Solomon (or possibly Solomon's own composition), teaching that even the wisest must "look higher" than their own understanding and "take God along" in their undertakings.

The commentary stresses that **God's sovereignty extends over both temporal and eternal matters**—from building houses to raising families. Henry emphasizes that "every creature is that to us which he makes it to be and no more," underscoring God's complete control over human circumstances.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Against Excessive Worldly Care**: He warns that "inordinate excessive care about the things of this world is a vain and fruitless thing." Those who "rise up early and sit up late" while eating "the bread of sorrows" labor in vain without God's blessing.

**Contentment in God's Love**: Christians should focus on keeping themselves "in the love of God," after which they "may be easy whether we have little or much of this world." God gives His beloved both literal sleep and "quietness and contentment of mind."

**Parental Instruction**: Parents should "suit their exhortations to their condition and occasions," teaching children to depend on God in their specific callings and circumstances.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets "building the house" as both literal construction and establishing a family's reputation and security. He notes that even city guards "wake but in vain" without divine protection, as "a thousand accidents" could overcome the most watchful preparation.

**Verses 3-5**: Children are presented as "God's gift" and "a heritage from the Lord." Henry emphasizes they should be received through "lawful marriage to one wife" and viewed as "blessings and not burdens." He offers the memorable insight that children "of the youth are arrows in the hand" that can be directed properly, but later become "arrows out of the hand" when grown—though sometimes these arrows "prove arrows in the heart" for godly parents.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical references throughout his commentary, including **Ecclesiastes 5:17** and **9:11** on the vanity of worldly pursuits, **Genesis 30:2** and **33:5** on God's control over childbearing, **Malachi 2:15** on seeking godly offspring through proper marriage, and **1 Chronicles 26:5** on Obed-edom's blessing of many sons for faithfully serving God.

The commentary connects this psalm's themes to the broader biblical narrative of trusting divine providence over human wisdom and effort.

Chapter 128
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 128: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 128 as a "psalm for families" that establishes the foundational principle that **"the only way to obtain that blessing which will make our families comfortable is to live in the fear of God and in obedience to him."** The core theological theme centers on the connection between godliness and divine blessing, demonstrating that "godliness has the promise of the life that now is and of that which is to come."

Henry emphasizes that those who are "truly holy are truly happy," defining the blessed as "such as fear the Lord and walk in his ways, such as have a deep reverence of God upon their spirits and evidence it by a regular and constant conformity to his will." He stresses that genuine fear of God must manifest in consistent obedience: "in vain do we pretend to be of those that fear God if we do not make conscience both of keeping to his ways."

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for godly living:

**Economic Prosperity**: God's people will "get an honest livelihood and live comfortably upon it." This includes both having meaningful work ("they shall have something to do") and success in their endeavors, with the assurance that "others shall not come and eat the bread out of their mouths."

**Family Harmony**: The commentary provides detailed guidance for family relationships, emphasizing that godly families experience particular blessings in their domestic life as God's reward for faithful living.

**Longevity and Legacy**: The faithful will "live long, to enjoy the comforts of the rising generations" and witness the continuation of their family lines.

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes the universal scope ("Blessed is everyone that fears the Lord") and personal application ("Happy shalt thou be"), encouraging readers to "take the comfort of the promise" personally.

**Verse 3**: The wife is compared to "a fruitful vine by the sides of the house" - Henry elaborates that she should be "not under-foot to be trampled on, nor yet upon the house-top to domineer, but on the side of the house, being a rib out of the side of the man." She should be "loving...obedient...and fruitful."

**Verse 6**: Children are described as "olive plants" - "straight and green, sucking in the sap of their good education, and likely in due time to be serviceable."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry incorporates numerous biblical references to support his interpretations:
- **2 Thessalonians 3:12** regarding working with quietness
- **Judges 9:13** concerning the vine's honor and profit
- **Genesis 49:22** and **Psalm 80:11** about fruitful branches
- **Romans 11:17** regarding being grafted into the good olive
- **Job 29:5** about children being present as a sign of prosperity
- **Proverbs 17:6** describing grandchildren as "the crown of old men"
- **Psalm 20:2** about blessings from Zion

Henry concludes that godly individuals find their private family blessings interconnected with the welfare of God's church, noting that "a good man can have little comfort in seeing his children's children, unless withal he see peace upon Israel."

Chapter 129
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 129: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 129 as addressing the "public concerns of God's Israel," likely written during or after the Babylonian captivity. The psalm presents the church as "one single person, now old and gray-headed," reflecting on God's faithfulness through generations of persecution. 

The central theological theme is God's covenant faithfulness despite persistent opposition. Henry emphasizes that while the church has faced constant affliction "from its youth," God's righteousness ensures both just discipline for Israel's sins and ultimate preservation of His people. He applies this both to Old Testament Israel and the "Gospel-Israel," noting that "the gospel-church, ever since it had a being, has been at times afflicted."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons:

1. **Expectation of persecution**: "God's people have always had many enemies, and the state of the church, from its infancy, has frequently been an afflicted state."

2. **Prayer against church enemies**: Believers should pray that opponents' "attempts against the church may be frustrated" and that they be "confounded and turned back with shame."

3. **Careful association**: "It is a dangerous thing to let the church's enemies have our good wishes in their designs against the church."

4. **Reverent speech**: Religious expressions should never be used "in light and ludicrous actions" since "holy things must not be jested with."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes Israel could say "I am the people that has been oppressed more than any people," comparing them to "a speckled bird, pecked at by all the birds round about" (Jer. 12:9).

**Verse 3**: The metaphor "The ploughers plowed upon my back" represents barbarous treatment, where enemies "tore them, as the husbandman tears the ground." Henry suggests this could refer to literal scourging or city destruction, connecting it prophetically to Christ who "gave his back to the smiters" (Isa. 50:6).

**Verse 4**: "The LORD is righteous" applies both ways - God was just in allowing affliction due to Israel's sins, and righteous in preserving them according to His promises.

**Verses 5-8**: Enemies are compared to "grass upon the housetops" - superficial, quickly withering, and "good for nothing." Unlike blessed harvest workers, no one blesses those attacking God's people.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical passages: Jeremiah 12:9 (speckled bird), Psalm 125:3 (rod of the wicked), Isaiah 10:7 (enemies' intentions), Hebrews 11:36 (cruel scourgings), Micah 3:12 (Zion plowed as a field), Matthew 16:18 (gates of hell), 2 Thessalonians 1:6 (divine recompense), Judges 5:31 (Deborah's song), Isaiah 51:12 (enemies as grass), Ruth 2:4 (harvest blessings), and 2 John 11 (not blessing evildoers).

The commentary emphasizes God's unbreakable covenant with His church, promising that despite facing "many a storm" and "many a shock," the church endures because "Christ has built his church upon a rock, and the gates of hell have not prevailed against it, nor ever shall."

Chapter 130
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 130: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies Psalm 130 as one of the seven penitential psalms, focused entirely on "the affairs of the soul" rather than temporal concerns. The psalm presents a progression through four stages: desire toward God, repentance before God, attendance upon God, and expectations from God. Henry emphasizes the universal application, noting that "as in water face answers to face, so does the heart of one humble penitent to another."

The central theological tension Henry explores is the balance between divine justice and mercy. He stresses that if God "were extreme to mark what we do amiss, there would be no hopes of coming off," yet God's pardoning mercy makes relationship possible.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Persistent Prayer in Distress**: Even "in the greatest depths," believers maintain the privilege to "cry unto God and be heard." Prayer can reach heaven's heights from the depths of earthly trouble.

**Humble Self-Assessment**: Christians must "acknowledge our iniquities" and recognize they "cannot justify ourselves before God." Henry emphasizes owning "the power and justice of God" while marveling at "God's patience and forbearance."

**Hopeful Waiting**: Believers should wait for God "more than those that watch for the morning"—with both assurance that relief will come and earnest desire for God's favor.

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry sees the "depths" as representing both external troubles and internal spiritual darkness, yet maintaining that prayer remains effective from any depth except hell itself.

**Verse 3**: The double use of "Lord" (Jah and Adonai) indicates "a very awful sense of God's glorious majesty and a dread of his wrath."

**Verse 4**: Henry interprets "forgiveness" as both God's declared mercy and the propitiation found in Jesus Christ. Crucially, forgiveness leads not to presumption but to fear: "that thou mayest be feared."

**Verses 5-6**: The intensity of waiting is compared to sentinels, caregivers with the sick, and travelers—all longing for dawn, but the believer's longing for God's favor exceeds even these.

**Verses 7-8**: Henry sees "plenteous redemption" as ultimately fulfilled in Christ's "eternal redemption," offering "redemption from all iniquity" with "all-sufficient fulness of merit and grace."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout: Jeremiah's dungeon prayers, Daniel in the den, and Jonah in the fish (verse 1); Exodus 34:6-7 on God's mercy (verse 4); Joel 2:13 and Matthew 3:2 on repentance (verse 4); Isaiah 44:5 on taking God's name (verse 7); Matthew 1:21, Titus 2:14, and Romans 11:26 on Christ's redemptive work (verse 8).

Henry concludes that this redemption extends to "Israel according to the spirit"—all covenant people who are "Israelites indeed, in whom is no guile," universalizing the psalm's comfort to all genuine believers.

Chapter 131
----------------------------------------
## Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 131: Summary

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry frames Psalm 131 as **David's profession of humility** in response to accusations of ambition from Saul and his courtiers. The central theological theme is **true humility before God** contrasted with worldly pride and ambition. Henry emphasizes that genuine humility involves both **consciousness of our integrity** and **confidence in God**. He presents humility not as weakness, but as spiritual maturity—comparing the believer to "a weaned child" who has moved beyond dependence on worldly things to find contentment in God alone.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications:

**Staying Within Our Sphere**: He warns against employing ourselves "in things above our station, in things too great or too high for us." This applies to both scholars avoiding speculation "above what is written" and common people not pretending to things "out of their reach."

**Contentment in Providence**: The commentary emphasizes accepting God's timing and placement. As Henry notes, "When our condition is not to our mind we must bring our mind to our condition."

**Weaning from Worldly Things**: Henry extensively develops the weaning metaphor, explaining that "Providence puts wormwood upon the breast" to help believers become "indifferent to the wealth and honour of this world."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1**: Henry breaks down humility into three components: no "conceited opinion of himself" (heart not haughty), no "scornful nor aspiring look" (eyes not lofty), and not meddling with affairs beyond one's station.

**Verse 2**: The weaning imagery receives extensive treatment. Henry explains that just as "the child is perhaps cross and fretful while it is in the weaning," believers may struggle initially but eventually find that "a gracious soul quiet itself under the loss of that which it loved."

**Verse 3**: David's call for Israel to hope represents his concern that supporters might "attempt something in favour of him before the time."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to several biblical passages:
- **1 Samuel 17:28** (brother's false charge of pride)
- **Proverbs 6:17** (proud look accompanying proud heart)
- **Matthew 18:3** (becoming like little children)

He also references the "humble publican" and emphasizes making "God's word his meditation" rather than pursuing worldly wisdom.

### Memorable Phrases

Henry concludes with the powerful statement: "Thus it is good to hope and quietly to wait for the salvation of the Lord," encapsulating the psalm's message of humble confidence in God's timing and providence.

Chapter 132
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 132: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry interprets Psalm 132 as **Solomon's prayer at the temple dedication**, structured around God's covenant faithfulness and the establishment of both throne and sanctuary. The psalm operates on **dual levels**: historically referring to David's dynasty and the Jerusalem temple, but **typologically pointing to Christ** as the ultimate Son of David and the church as God's eternal dwelling place.

Henry emphasizes the **covenant relationship** between God and David, confirmed by divine oath, which finds its ultimate fulfillment in Christ who "kept God's covenant and his testimony, did his Father's will, and in all things pleased him." The commentary treats Zion as "a type of the gospel-church" where God's promises receive their fullest expression.

## Key Practical Applications

**For Ministers**: Henry stresses that "righteousness is the best ornament of a minister," calling for priests to "appear righteous both in their administrations and in their conversations." He emphasizes that faithful ministry "is the joy of the saints."

**For Believers**: The commentary encourages Christians to plead Christ's merits rather than their own: "We have no merit of our own to plead, but for his sake, in whom there is a fulness of merit, let us find favour." Henry advocates for **urgent dedication** to God's work, noting David's vow shows "when needful work is to be done for God it is good for us to task ourselves."

**For the Church**: Believers should maintain "concern for the gospel church as the temple of God, and a dependence upon Christ as David our King."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 3-4** (David's sleepless vow): Henry sees this as David's commitment to prioritize God's house before his own comfort, noting "we must not enjoy the comforts of this life till we have laid a foundation for hopes of a better."

**Verse 8** ("Arise, O LORD, into thy rest"): This represents the invitation for God's presence to dwell permanently in the temple, with Henry noting the transition from temporary residence to eternal dwelling.

**Verses 11-12** (God's oath to David): Henry applies this both to Solomon's succession and Christ's eternal kingship, emphasizing the conditional nature for earthly kings but unconditional fulfillment in Christ.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout:
- **1 Chronicles 28:2** and **2 Chronicles 6:41-42** for the historical context
- **Acts 2:30** and **Luke 1:32** for Christ as David's ultimate heir
- **Hebrews 12:22** identifying the church as "Mount Zion"
- **Revelation 3:21** for believers sharing Christ's throne
- **2 Samuel 7:2** regarding David's concern for the ark's temporary housing

The commentary consistently moves from **Old Testament shadows to New Testament realities**, showing how David's dynasty, the Jerusalem temple, and Zion's election all point forward to Christ's eternal kingdom and the church as God's dwelling place. Henry concludes that these "precious promises, confirmed by an oath" provide "strong consolation" for believers who can claim them through Christ's merit.

Chapter 133
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 133: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents **brotherly unity** as both a divine mandate and earthly blessing that reflects heavenly harmony. He describes unity as "agreeable to the will of God, the conformity of earth to heaven," emphasizing that genuine Christian fellowship mirrors God's nature. The commentary treats unity not merely as conflict avoidance, but as active mutual love where believers "delight in each other with mutual endearments, and promote each other's welfare with mutual services."

Henry suggests David may have written this psalm when the tribes united to make him king, demonstrating how unity serves both civil and sacred purposes. The theological foundation rests on love originating from God: "believers are taught of God to love one another; it is a grace of his working in us."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian communities. He notes that while separation sometimes prevents conflict (referencing Abraham and Lot), true goodness comes when "brethren dwell together and so dwell in unity...as having one heart, one soul, one interest." 

The commentary emphasizes that **love should be proactive**: "Nor should our love to our brethren stay for theirs to us (that is publican's love), but should go before it—that is divine love." Henry also warns that without holy love, "our services are not acceptable to God...if we have it not we are nothing."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verse 1:** Henry emphasizes the exclamatory "Behold!"—calling unity "a rare thing, and therefore admirable...an amiable thing, which will attract our hearts." He notes we "cannot conceive or express the goodness and pleasantness of it."

**Verse 2:** The holy oil comparison yields five key points: (1) love must be holy and devoted to God, (2) it's divinely composed, (3) it's precious in God's sight, (4) it brings joy to all parties, and (5) it's essential for acceptable service to God.

**Verse 3:** The dew metaphor shows unity as both **refreshing and fructifying**—it "cools the scorching heat of men's passions" and "makes the heart tender and fit to receive the good seed of the word."

## Cross-References and Supporting Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to several biblical passages: Genesis 13:9 (Abraham and Lot's separation), 1 John 5:1 (loving the begotten for the Father's sake), 1 Corinthians 13:1-2 (love's necessity for acceptable service), 1 Peter 2:1 (bitterness hindering spiritual growth), and Micah 5:7 (dew's independent nature).

## Divine Blessing and Eternal Perspective

Henry concludes that **God "commands the blessing" where unity exists**—a comprehensive blessing culminating in "life for evermore." He beautifully states: "Those that dwell in love not only dwell in God, but do already dwell in heaven. As the perfection of love is the blessedness of heaven, so the sincerity of love is the earnest of that blessedness."

The commentary presents unity as both a present foretaste and future guarantee of eternal fellowship with "the God of love and peace."

Chapter 134
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 134: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 134 as the final "song of degrees" and interprets it as a dialogue between temple servants. The psalm centers on **two-fold blessing**: humanity blessing God (verses 1-2) and God blessing humanity (verse 3). Henry emphasizes the **reciprocal nature of worship**, where those who faithfully serve God receive divine blessing in return. He views this as particularly relevant for night watchmen in the temple who maintained sacred duties when public worship had ended.

The commentary highlights the **priesthood of all believers**, applying Old Testament Levitical service to Christian life: "We are the servants of the Lord; we have a place and a name in his house, in his sanctuary."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

**Time Management and Devotion**: He advocates using idle moments for worship, calling it "an excellent piece of husbandry to fill up the vacancies of time with pious meditations and ejaculations." He suggests that when believers "have nothing else to do," conversation with God is "very modest and reasonable."

**Workplace Spirituality**: Those serving in God's house "must remember where they were, and that holiness and holy work became that house." This applies to Christians maintaining spiritual awareness in their daily responsibilities.

**Mutual Encouragement**: Henry emphasizes that believers should "mutually exhort one another and pray for one another," demonstrating community support in spiritual service.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry sees the call to "bless ye the LORD" as directed both to Levites maintaining temple watch and to contemporary Christians as "priests to our God." The phrase "lift up your hands" represents both physical gesture and spiritual elevation - "the lifting up of their hearts."

**Verse 3**: The blessing from "him that made heaven and earth" emphasizes God's comprehensive authority over "all the blessings of both [heaven and earth] at his disposal, the upper and nether springs."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry incorporates several scriptural connections:
- **Numbers 3:6** - Levitical temple service and responsibilities
- **Luke 2:37** - Anna's continuous temple devotion as example of dedicated service
- **Isaiah 66:21** - Christians as spiritual priests and Levites
- **Hebrews 7:7** - The principle that "the less is blessed of the greater"

These references support his interpretation that ancient temple practices provide models for contemporary Christian devotion and service.

Chapter 135
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 135: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**God's Supremacy and Sovereignty**: Henry emphasizes God's absolute dominion, describing Him as having "absolute power" who "may do what he will" and "can do what he will." The psalm demonstrates God's superiority over all false gods through His unlimited authority in "heaven, in earth, in the seas, and all deep places."

**Election and Covenant Relationship**: A central theme is God's special relationship with Israel. Henry notes that "The Lord hath chosen Jacob to himself" and calls Israel God's "peculiar treasure" - His "Segullah, a people appropriated to him." This election creates both privilege and responsibility for praise.

**God's Eternal Nature vs. Idol Vanity**: Henry contrasts the living God whose "name endures for ever" with lifeless idols that "have mouths, but they speak not." He argues that worshipping "senseless" gods makes worshippers "sensual and senseless," while worshipping the spiritual God makes believers "spiritual and wise."

## Key Practical Applications

**Universal Call to Praise**: Henry emphasizes that praise should come from "servants of the Lord" - particularly those who "stand in the house of the LORD" but extending to "all others that feared the Lord." He notes that "the work is its own wages" because praising God creates "a heaven upon earth."

**Grateful Recognition of God's Works**: Believers should praise God not only for personal benefits but "for what he is in himself and has done for others." Henry encourages taking "all occasions to speak well of God and to give his truths and ways a good word."

**Spiritual Discernment**: The commentary warns against idolatry by showing the absurdity of trusting in human-made gods, encouraging believers to exercise reason in their faith commitments.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-4**: Henry identifies three reasons for praise: God's inherent goodness, the pleasure found in worship itself, and the "peculiar privileges of God's people" through divine election.

**Verses 5-7**: The assertion "I know that the LORD is great" represents both observational proof and revelational knowledge. Henry explains God's control over natural phenomena - vapors, lightning, rain, and wind - as evidence of His "wonderful power" over the "chain of natural causes."

**Verses 8-14**: Historical demonstrations of God's power through the Egyptian plagues and Conquest of Canaan serve as perpetual reasons for praise, showing God's faithfulness to His covenant promises across generations.

**Verses 15-18**: The idol description emphasizes their complete powerlessness - they cannot perform "the least act, no, not of the animal life" despite having the form of living creatures.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to multiple biblical passages:
- **Jeremiah 13:11** - God chose a people "that they might be unto him for a name and a praise"
- **Exodus 19:5** - Israel as God's "peculiar treasure above all people"
- **Psalm 115:4** - Parallel description of lifeless idols
- **Deuteronomy 32:36** - Source of verse 14's language about God judging and having compassion on His people
- **Exodus 3:15** - God's eternal name and memorial
- **1 Kings 18:38** - Example of God's power over fire and water

Henry frames this as a "Hallelujah-psalm" that serves as both "Alpha and Omega" - beginning and ending with praise, designed to fill both "hearts and mouths with the high praises of God."

Chapter 136
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 136: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

**The Eternal Nature of God's Mercy**: Henry emphasizes that the psalm's repetitive refrain "for his mercy endureth for ever" (appearing 26 times) is "no vain repetition." This repetition demonstrates that "God's mercies to his people are thus repeated and drawn, as it were, with a continuando from the beginning to the end" and that "the everlasting continuance of the mercy of God is very much his honour and that which he glories in."

**God's Comprehensive Sovereignty**: The commentary presents God as supreme over all creation and history - as "the God of gods," "Lord of lords," and one "who alone doeth great wonders." Henry structures God's praise around five key roles: His inherent greatness, Creator of the world, Israel's Savior, universal Redeemer, and benefactor of all creation.

**Typological Interpretation**: Henry sees Israel's redemption from Egypt as a type of Christ's eternal redemption, noting that "our redemption by Christ, which was typified by that, does indeed endure for ever, for it is an eternal redemption."

## Key Practical Applications

**Thanksgiving as Primary Worship**: Henry observes, "We are never so earnestly called upon to pray and repent as to give thanks; for it is the will of God that we should abound most in the most pleasant exercises of religion, in that which is the work of heaven."

**Detailed Recognition of God's Favors**: "It is good to enter into the detail of God's favours and not to view them in the gross, and in each instance to observe, and own, that God's mercy endureth for ever."

**Proper Focus in Thanksgiving**: Christians must avoid the Pharisee's error who "made all his thanksgivings terminate in his own praise," instead "directing them all to God's glory."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: These establish proper worship directed to God's transcendent excellence and "incontestably and uncontrollably supreme" dominion.

**Verses 7-9**: Henry notes the celestial bodies are called "great lights because they appear so to us," and while they "rule," they remain "the world's servants and God's subjects" - a rebuke to Gentile idolatry.

**Verses 21-22**: The promised land prefigures "the heavenly Canaan, the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ unto eternal life."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to multiple scriptures: Hebrews 13:15 (sacrifice of praise), 2 Chronicles 5:13 and 20:21-22 (divine acceptance of this refrain), Exodus 20:2 (Mosaic covenant foundation), Jeremiah 34:18 (covenant imagery), and Romans 9:23 (vessels of mercy). He particularly emphasizes how this psalm echoes Psalm 135:8 in recounting God's mighty acts for Israel.

The commentary presents this psalm as a comprehensive celebration of God's unchanging mercy manifest throughout creation, redemption history, and personal salvation, calling believers to detailed, God-centered thanksgiving that mirrors heaven's eternal worship.

Chapter 137
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 137: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies this as a "mournful psalm" written during the Babylonian captivity, likely near its end when "the destruction of Babylon [was] hastening on apace." He emphasizes four key spiritual dynamics: **the captives' inability to enjoy themselves, humor their oppressors, forget Jerusalem, or forgive their enemies**. 

Henry interprets the psalm through the lens of God's sovereignty over nations, viewing Babylon's eventual destruction as divine justice. He sees the captives' loyalty to Jerusalem as representing proper Christian priorities—**"A godly man will prefer a public good before any private satisfaction or gratification whatsoever."** The psalm demonstrates how God's people should respond to persecution: with patient endurance rather than revenge.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers. He warns against **"mixing the leaven of malice with our sacrifices"** while still acknowledging that **"those that are glad at calamities, especially the calamities of Jerusalem, shall not go unpunished."** 

He emphasizes maintaining spiritual priorities during hardship, noting how the captives' **"affection to God's house swallowed up their concern for their own houses."** Henry also advocates for spiritual discernment in refusing to accommodate ungodly demands, as the captives demonstrated by asking **"How shall we sing the Lord's song in a strange land?"**

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry sees the hanging of harps as both appropriate mourning and possibly excessive—**"praising God is never out of season."** The choice to sit by rivers reflected their desire for solitude to **"mingle their tears with the streams."**

**Verses 3-4**: The Babylonians' demand for entertainment was **"very barbarous and inhuman"** and **"very profane and impious."** The captives' refusal demonstrated that **"profane scoffers are not to be humoured, nor pearls cast before swine."**

**Verses 5-6**: Henry highlights their unwavering devotion: **"Their heads were full of it...Their hearts were full of it."** Their loyalty persisted even though **"many of them had never seen it, nor knew any thing of it but by report."**

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to other biblical texts. He references **Jeremiah 29:5** regarding building houses in exile, **Ezekiel 1:3** and **Daniel 8:2** for other riverside locations, and **Lamentations 3:28-29** for proper behavior under oppression.

For the final verses, he cites **Zechariah 1:15** about those who "helped forward the affliction," **Revelation 18:6** regarding Babylon receiving just recompense, and **Isaiah 45:1** where Cyrus is called God's "anointed" for destroying Babylon. He connects New Testament Babylon to **2 Thessalonians 2:3** and **Revelation 19:1**, showing how this psalm prefigures ultimate spiritual victory.

Henry concludes that believers should **"be much affected with the concernments of the church"** while **"carefully avoiding all personal animosities."**

Chapter 138
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 138: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 138 as a psalm of **grateful praise and confident trust**. He divides it into two main movements: David's **retrospective thanksgiving** for God's past goodness (verses 1-3) and his **prospective hope** for future blessings, both for others and himself (verses 4-8).

A central theological theme is **God's faithfulness to His word**. Henry emphasizes verse 2's declaration that God has "magnified thy word above all thy name," interpreting this as God's promises and revelations through Scripture surpassing even His works in creation. He notes that "the wonders of grace exceed the wonders of nature; and what is discovered of God by revelation is much greater than what is discovered by reason."

Henry also highlights **God's care for the humble versus the proud** (verse 6), calling this "the sum of that gospel which the kings of the earth shall hear"—that God accepts penitent sinners while rejecting the impenitent.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Wholehearted worship** should be sincere and bold. David's commitment to praise "with my whole heart" and "before the gods" demonstrates that "praising God is work which the greatest of men need not be ashamed of; it is the work of angels, the work of heaven."

**Prayer and divine communion** are emphasized through David's experience: "The intercourse between God and his saints is carried on by his promises and their prayers." Henry notes that spiritual strength in affliction is a real answer to prayer, even when circumstances don't change.

**Perseverance in faith** is anchored not in human strength but in God's enduring mercy. Henry counsels: "Our hopes that we shall persevere must be founded, not upon our own strength, for that will fail us, but upon the mercy of God, for that will not fail."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: Henry emphasizes the progression from heart worship to bold public praise to proper temple worship, with Christ as "our temple, and towards him we must look with an eye of faith."

**Verse 3**: The "speedy answer" and "spiritual answer" demonstrate that "those that trade with heaven by prayer grow rich by quick returns."

**Verses 4-5**: Henry sees both immediate application to David's neighboring kings and prophetic fulfillment in the gospel's spread to all nations.

**Verses 7-8**: The promise that "The LORD will perfect that which concerneth me" becomes a foundation for confident prayer: "Whatever good there is in us it is the work of God's own hands."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:
- **Psalm 111:1** for wholehearted praise
- **1 Corinthians 11:10** regarding angels' presence in worship
- **Isaiah 65:24** on God's quick response to prayer
- **Philippians 1:6** on God's completion of His good work
- **Luke 18** (Pharisee and publican) illustrating God's favor toward the humble
- **Psalm 72:11** regarding kings bowing before Christ

Henry concludes that this psalm calls believers to "devote ourselves to God's praise and glory and repose ourselves in his power and goodness," trusting that "whom he loves he loves to the end; and, as for God, his work is perfect."

Chapter 139
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 139: Summary

## Main Theological Themes

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 139 as a profound meditation on **God's omniscience**, calling it potentially "the most excellent of all the psalms of David." The central doctrine is that "God knows our hearts, and the hearts of all the children of men," with Henry emphasizing that if believers "mix faith with it and seriously consider it," this truth would greatly influence both "our holiness and our comfort."

The commentary develops this through three major theological pillars: God's perfect knowledge of humanity, His omnipresence, and His role as Creator. Henry notes that "all the motions and actions both of our inward and of our outward man are naked and open before him."

## Practical Applications

Henry draws several key applications from God's omniscience:

1. **Personal devotion**: Believers should approach this doctrine through prayer rather than mere intellectual study, as "Divine truths look fully as well when they are prayed over as when they are preached over."

2. **Self-examination**: The knowledge of God's omniscience should produce "pleasing admiration of God," "holy dread and detestation of sin and sinners," and "holy satisfaction in our own integrity."

3. **Humble acknowledgment**: Henry emphasizes personalizing this truth—not merely acknowledging "Thou hast known all," but "Thou hast known me."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-6**: Henry details God's comprehensive knowledge, including our "down-sitting and up-rising," our thoughts "afar off," and our words before they're spoken. He notes that God "compassest every particular path" and "sifts" our actions "so as thoroughly to distinguish between the good and evil."

**Verses 7-12**: On omnipresence, Henry observes that "No flight can remove us out of God's presence" and "No veil can hide us from God's eye." He notes that to God "the darkness and the light are both alike."

**Verses 13-16**: Regarding divine creation, Henry explains that God's knowledge stems from His creative work: "He that framed the engine knows all the motions of it."

## Cross-References

Henry incorporates several scriptural connections:
- Job 5:27 on knowing truths "for ourselves"
- Proverbs 25:3 on the unsearchable hearts of kings
- Psalm 33:14 on God understanding thoughts "from afar"
- Jeremiah 23:24 on God filling heaven and earth
- Malachi 4:2 referencing "wings of the morning"

Henry concludes that this "great and self-evident truth" should profoundly impact Christian living when properly applied through faith.

Chapter 140
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 140: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 140 as a **typological psalm** where David's persecution prefigures Christ's suffering, emphasizing that "David was a type of Christ, that he suffered before he reigned, was humbled before he was exalted." The central theological theme revolves around **divine protection against evil**, with enemies characterized as "the genuine offspring and seed of the serpent," connecting their malice directly to Satan's work.

Henry identifies the psalm's **covenant framework**, where believers can claim God's protection based on their relationship with Him. The commentary emphasizes God's dual nature as both "Jehovah" (self-existent) and "Adonai" (personal ruler), making Him "the strength of my salvation."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing persecution:

**Prayer as Primary Defense**: "The more malice appears in our enemies against us the more earnest we should be in prayer to God to take us under his protection." He emphasizes turning to God rather than responding with equal malice.

**Claiming Covenant Relationship**: "In troublous dangerous times it is good to claim relation to God, and by faith to keep hold of him." Believers should actively declare "Thou art my God" in times of crisis.

**Drawing from Past Experience**: Henry notes that "the experience we have had of his power and care, in dangers of one kind, may encourage us to trust in him and depend upon him in dangers of another nature."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-5**: Henry identifies enemies as characterized by **subtlety** ("crafty to do mischief"), **spite** ("adders' poison is under their lips"), **confederation** ("gathered together for war"), and **pride**. He notes that "those who can agree in nothing else can agree to persecute a good man."

**Verses 6-7**: The response to persecution should be claiming relationship with God rather than retaliation: "When his enemies sharpened their tongues against him, did he sharpen his against them? No; adders' poison was under their lips, but grace was poured into his lips."

**Verses 8-11**: Henry interprets these either as prayers or prophecies, noting that "mischief of their own lips shall cover their heads"—evil returns upon its perpetrators.

**Verses 12-13**: The conclusion emphasizes both God's justice for the oppressed and the believers' responsive gratitude: "This is true thanksgiving, even thanksliving."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects the psalm to numerous biblical passages: **Psalm 7:15-16** (evil returning on perpetrators), **Job 20:23, 27:22** (burning coals of judgment), **Isaiah 30:33** (Tophet's depth), and **Psalm 2:5** (destruction of the wicked). He references the **Sodom narrative** for divine judgment imagery and connects the serpent imagery to **Genesis 3** and Satan's character.

The commentary also draws parallels to **Herod and Pilate's alliance** against Christ, demonstrating how enemies unite against the righteous, and references **David's prayer against Ahithophel** as a model for praying against God's enemies.

Henry concludes that this psalm teaches believers to find security in God's covenant protection while maintaining faithfulness through persecution, ultimately pointing to the greater fulfillment in Christ's experience and victory.

Chapter 141
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 141: Summary

## Context and Main Theological Themes

Matthew Henry identifies this as a psalm of David written during distress, likely while fleeing from Saul. The commentary emphasizes **prayer as spiritual sacrifice** and the necessity of divine grace for holy living. Henry presents two central theological themes: "Mercy to accept what we do well, and grace to keep us from doing ill" as the fundamental requests Christians should make.

The psalm demonstrates **prayer as intimate communion with God**, where Henry notes that "Prayer is a spiritual sacrifice; it is the offering up of the soul, and its best affections, to God." He emphasizes that prayer has "sweet-smelling savour to God, as incense, which yet has no savour without fire; nor has prayer without the fire of holy love and fervour."

## Key Practical Applications

**1. Earnest Prayer Life (vv. 1-2)**
Henry advocates for fervent, faith-filled prayer. He distinguishes that God hears prayers "not for their loudness, but their liveliness." When separated from formal worship, believers should offer prayer as substitute for temple sacrifices.

**2. Guarding Against Sin (vv. 3-4)**
The commentary emphasizes proactive spiritual vigilance. Henry writes: "We must keep our mouths as with a bridle; but that will not serve: we must pray to God to keep them." He stresses dependence on divine grace rather than human willpower for avoiding both sinful speech and actions.

**3. Receiving Righteous Reproof (v. 5)**
Henry teaches that believers should welcome correction from godly people: "let the righteous smite me; it shall be a kindness." He presents four practical steps: desire reproof for our faults, account it friendship, receive it as healing medicine, and requite kindness through prayer for our reprovers.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Prayer replaces temple worship when circumstances prevent formal religious observance. The "evening sacrifice" reference points to Christ's eventual sacrifice.

**Verse 3**: Tongue-control requires divine assistance beyond human resolve. Even Moses, "the meekest of men," spoke unadvisedly under provocation.

**Verse 4**: Avoiding sinful companionship and pleasures requires constant vigilance. Henry warns that "Better is a dinner of herbs, out of the way of temptation, than a stalled ox in it."

**Verse 7**: The metaphor of scattered bones represents extreme desolation, yet hope remains possible through God's power.

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry connects this psalm to broader scriptural themes:
- **Proverbs 6:23** and **27:6** on receiving correction
- **1 Samuel 25:32** referencing David's gratitude for Abigail's reproof
- **Psalm 51:8** on sin's devastating effects
- **Ecclesiastes 7:5** comparing righteous reproof to foolish flattery

The commentary also anticipates Christ's sacrifice, seeing the evening sacrifice as prophetic of Jesus offering himself "in the evening of the world and in the evening of the day."

Henry concludes by emphasizing God's justice in allowing the wicked to fall into their own traps while delivering the righteous, demonstrating that "No law can be more just than that the architects of destruction should perish by their own contrivances."

Chapter 142
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 142: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 142 as David's prayer from a cave while fleeing Saul, emphasizing several key theological themes. **God's omniscience and faithfulness** stands central—when David's "spirit was overwhelmed," he found comfort knowing "thou knewest my path." Henry interprets this as God knowing both David's sincerity and his complete situation, providing protection even when circumstances seemed dire.

The commentary develops a **typological interpretation**, seeing David as prefiguring Christ, "who, in his sufferings for us, was forsaken of all men, even of his own disciples, and trod the wine-press alone." This connects David's abandonment to Christ's ultimate abandonment on the cross.

Henry emphasizes **God as both refuge and portion**, noting that "there is enough in God to answer all the necessities of this present time." He presents God as all-sufficient, meeting both protective needs (refuge) and provision needs (portion).

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing trials. He emphasizes that **prayer should be detailed and earnest**—David "poured out his complaint," demonstrating "free and full complaint" with great fervency. Believers should "unbosom" themselves to God, showing their troubles to Him rather than dwelling on them alone.

The commentary advocates **finding satisfaction in God when human support fails**. When "no man cared for my soul," David turned to God, illustrating that divine constancy surpasses human friendship. Henry notes how "swallow-friends...are gone when winter comes," but God remains faithful.

Henry emphasizes **praying with praise in view**—David sought deliverance "that I may praise thy name." This demonstrates proper motivation in prayer, seeking God's glory rather than mere personal relief.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry notes David wasn't "ashamed to own...the fright he had been in," encouraging even "men of the first rank" to "cry to God" like "children to their parents." The phrase "mental prayer is vocal to God" highlights how God hears unspoken prayers.

**Verse 3**: The phrase "thou knewest my path" receives extended treatment—God knew David's sincerity, his condition, and provided protection. Henry connects this to Job 23:10, emphasizing divine knowledge as comfort.

**Verse 4**: "No man would know me" illustrates complete social abandonment, yet becomes opportunity for deeper dependence on God.

**Verse 5**: "Thou art my refuge and my portion in the land of the living" receives theological development—God as both protection from evil and source of all good.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout. Romans 8:26 supports prayer "with the voice of his mind," while Job 23:10 parallels God's knowledge of our ways. Psalm 31:7 and Deuteronomy 2:7 reinforce God's protective knowledge.

The commentary references 1 Samuel 18:21 regarding Michal as a snare, and Isaiah 63:5 concerning Christ's abandonment. Psalm 109:31 explains looking for an advocate "on the right hand."

2 Corinthians 1:11 supports the concept of communal thanksgiving, while various passages illuminate the "refuge" theme central to the psalm.

Henry concludes that this psalm serves as "instruction" (Maschil), teaching believers to "warrant his complaints and fetch in his comforts" when facing similar trials, making David's experience a template for faithful prayer in distress.

Chapter 143
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 143 - Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry identifies this as a psalm of David likely written during Saul's persecution, emphasizing **dependence on divine mercy over human righteousness**. The central theological theme is the contrast between God's faithfulness and human sinfulness. Henry notes that David appeals to "God's righteousness, the word of promise" rather than his own merit, stating that "we have no righteousness of our own to plead." 

The commentary emphasizes **justification by faith alone**, with Henry explaining David's plea in verse 2: "Enter not into judgment with thy servant, for thou hast already entered into judgment with thy Son." This reflects the substitutionary atonement where Christ bears our iniquity. Henry also highlights the **necessity of divine grace** for both knowing and doing God's will, noting that believers need God not only to show them the way but to "take us by the hand and lead us."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers facing adversity:

**Proper response to overwhelming circumstances**: When troubles seem insurmountable, believers should look backward to "remember the days of old," look around at God's works in creation, and look upward with "earnest desires towards God." Henry notes this is "the best course we can take when our spirits are overwhelmed."

**Daily spiritual practices**: He emphasizes seeking God's favor "in the morning, every morning; let my waking thoughts be of God's lovingkindness." This establishes a pattern of beginning each day with divine perspective.

**Honest prayer in distress**: Henry validates emotional honesty before God, noting that even great saints like David can have "spirits for a time almost overwhelmed" and that "it is sometimes the lot of the best men."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry explains the paradox of appealing to God's righteousness while pleading for mercy, clarifying that David means "the righteous promises of the gospel, not the righteous threatenings of the law."

**Verse 6**: The image of thirsting souls is particularly vivid: "My soul thirsteth after thee; it is as a thirsty land, which, being parched with excessive heat, gapes for rain." Henry emphasizes this represents complete dependence on divine consolation.

**Verse 10**: Henry notes the progression from knowing God's will to doing it: "not only show me what thy will is, but teach me how to do it, how to turn my hand dexterously to my duty."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to numerous biblical passages: **Habakkuk 2:1** (looking up after prayer), **Job 9:3** (inability to answer God), **Psalm 77:5,11** (remembering God's former works), **Isaiah 51:12-13** (considering God's power reduces fear of man), **Psalm 4:6** (preferring God's favor above all good), and **Psalm 31:8** (God's goodness to those who trust Him).

The commentary consistently demonstrates how this individual lament connects to broader biblical themes of grace, redemption, and spiritual growth, making it "more easily accommodate[d]...to ourselves" since "most of the petitions in it are for spiritual blessings which we all need at all times."

Chapter 144
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry Commentary on Psalm 144: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 144 as David's post-coronation composition, written when facing ongoing threats from neighboring nations, particularly the Philistines. The psalm demonstrates several key theological themes: **God's sovereignty over human affairs**, **divine condescension to mankind**, and **the necessity of dependence on God rather than earthly power**. Henry notes that some Jewish writers interpret this psalm messianically, referring to Christ's kingdom.

The commentary emphasizes God's role as the ultimate source of all human authority and ability. As Henry states, David acknowledges that God "taught his hands to war and his fingers to fight," demonstrating that **all human skills and positions derive from divine instruction and providence**.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Gratitude must precede petition**: "A prayer for further mercy is fitly begun with a thanksgiving for former mercy." This establishes the proper posture for approaching God.

**Recognition of human frailty**: David's reflection that "Man is like to vanity; his days are as a shadow that passeth away" should cultivate humility and dependence on God rather than human strength.

**Trust in God's protection**: Henry emphasizes that believers should view "the divine attributes and promises as fortifications... far exceeding those either of nature or art."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry highlights David's multiplication of descriptive terms for God (strength, fortress, deliverer, shield), showing "the satisfaction he had in God and his interest in him." The phrase "my goodness" means not only that God is good to David, but represents "my chief good, in whose favour I place my felicity."

**Verses 3-4**: The rhetorical question about man's insignificance leads Henry to marvel at God's condescension, noting that man "is as like as may be to vanity itself" yet receives divine attention and care.

**Verses 5-8**: David's prayer for divine intervention against "strange children" (Philistines) who speak vanity and whose "right hand is a right hand of falsehood" demonstrates the need for divine justice against deceptive enemies.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to multiple biblical passages: He references **Psalm 8:4-5** regarding human dignity, **Hebrews 2:6** applying these words to Christ, and **2 Chronicles 20:20-21** regarding praising God for promised mercies. The commentary also draws connections to David's earlier experiences at En-gedi (1 Samuel 23:29) and his conquest of Zion (2 Samuel 5:7,9), while noting parallels to God's appearance at Mount Sinai and His promise to Abraham ("Fear not, Abram, I am thy shield").

The final verses about national prosperity are interpreted as both literal blessings for Israel and spiritual blessings for believers, with the ultimate happiness found in having "the LORD" as one's God rather than in material prosperity alone.

Chapter 145
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 145

### Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 145 as a pinnacle of praise, noting its strategic placement as the final "David's psalm" that transitions from five "begging psalms" to six concluding psalms of pure praise. He emphasizes that **"praise is the conclusion of the whole matter"** and suggests believers should "abound much in praise" near life's end as preparation for "the world of everlasting praise."

Henry identifies two major theological focuses: God's **greatness and goodness**, demonstrated through His kingdom's administration. He divides God's reign into the "kingdom of providence" (verses 14-16) and the "kingdom of grace" (verses 17-20), showing how divine attributes manifest in both natural and spiritual realms.

### Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for Christian living:

1. **Daily, Constant Praise**: "Every day will I bless thee" means "no day must pass, though ever so busy a day, though ever so sorrowful a day, without praising God."

2. **Conversational Worship**: Believers should give glory to God "not only in solemn devotions, but in common conversation," making God's glory a regular topic of discourse.

3. **Generational Responsibility**: "One generation shall praise thy works to another"—each generation must transmit God's mighty acts to the next, ensuring uninterrupted succession of worship.

4. **Active vs. Passive Praise**: While all creation praises God objectively (like "the beautiful building praises the builder"), saints must praise Him actively, serving as collectors of "the rent or tribute of praise from the inferior creatures."

### Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

Henry highlights the psalm's **alphabetic structure** for memorization purposes. On verse 8, he emphasizes God being "ready to give, and ready to forgive, more ready than we are to ask." 

Regarding verse 9 ("The Lord is good to all"), he notes God's goodness extends "to all his creatures, from the highest angel to the meanest worm, to all but devils and damned sinners, that have shut themselves out from his goodness."

On verses 15-16 about God's provision, Henry observes that **"His tender mercies are over all his works"** and that divine mercy "out-shine all his other works."

### Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to several biblical passages: Psalm 78:3 regarding generational testimony, Romans 11:22 on God's goodness and severity, Romans 11:33 on God's unsearchable greatness, and Exodus 33:19 and 34:6-7 concerning God's glory in goodness. He also references 2 Chronicles 29:30 regarding the ongoing use of Davidic psalms in worship.

Henry concludes that the "everlasting hallelujahs of all the saints" will forever celebrate divine goodness, making this psalm both a model for present worship and preparation for eternal praise.

Chapter 146
----------------------------------------
## Summary of Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 146

### **Main Theological Themes and Interpretations**

Henry presents Psalm 146 as fundamentally about proper worship and trust, contrasting human frailty with divine reliability. A central theme is the **Messianic interpretation** - Henry notes that "many of the Jewish writers refer this psalm to the kingdom of the Messiah," connecting verses 7-8 with Christ's ministry described in Matthew 11:5-6. The psalm emphasizes **God's threefold kingdom**: nature (His creative power), providence (His earthly dominion), and the Messiah (His gracious reign).

The commentary highlights **divine fidelity versus human mortality**. Henry emphasizes that even good princes are "sons of Adam, weak and mortal," while God "keepeth truth for ever." This creates a theological framework where ultimate trust belongs only to the eternal God.

### **Key Practical Applications**

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Personal Worship**: Even kings like David shouldn't consider themselves exempt from praising God. Henry notes David "considered his dignity as so far from excusing him from it that it rather obliged him to lead in it." Praise should be our "great employment and delight while we have any being."

**Proper Trust Placement**: The commentary warns against excessive reliance on human leaders, even good ones. "Put not your trust in princes," Henry explains, because we cannot be sure of their ability or continuance - "his breath goeth forth...in that very day his thoughts perish."

**Hope in Divine Provision**: Believers should trust God for both temporal needs (providence) and eternal salvation (grace in Christ), living "a life of dependence upon him."

### **Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights**

**Verses 1-2**: Henry emphasizes lifelong praise as essential, noting David's resolve to praise "while I have any being" - extending beyond earthly life to heavenly existence.

**Verses 3-4**: The commentary provides a striking meditation on human mortality: "all his purposes are cut off and buried with him." Even David, despite his greatness, warns against trusting in princes because they return to "his earth" - their graves being ultimately "all that will remain to him."

**Verses 5-10**: Henry sees explicit Messianic fulfillment here, particularly noting that "since the world began was it not heard that any man opened the eyes of one that was born blind till Christ did it."

### **Cross-References to Other Scripture**

Henry weaves extensive scriptural connections throughout:

- **Genesis 3:19** (dust to dust) and **Job 17:11** (purposes cut off) illustrate human mortality
- **Matthew 11:5-6** provides the key Messianic connection to Christ's healing ministry
- **John 9:32** specifically links the blind receiving sight to Christ's unique divine power
- **Isaiah 61:1-2** connects to spiritual liberty and the Messiah's proclamation
- **Revelation 3:14** describes Christ as "the Amen, the faithful witness"
- **Hosea 14:3** relates to God's care for the fatherless

Henry particularly emphasizes that **Jehovah appears five times in five lines** (verses 7-9), highlighting that "almighty power...is engaged and exerted for the relief of the oppressed."

The commentary masterfully balances practical Christian living with deep theological reflection, presenting this psalm as both a call to worship and a prophetic glimpse of Christ's redemptive work.

Chapter 147
----------------------------------------
**Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 147: Summary**

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry presents Psalm 147 as a comprehensive praise psalm emphasizing God's threefold nature: **God of nature** (displaying His greatness through creation), **God of grace** (comforting His people), and **God of Israel** (establishing both civil order and religious worship). The psalm demonstrates God's sovereignty over all creation while highlighting His intimate care for the humble and broken-hearted.

Henry argues this psalm was likely penned by David rather than during post-exilic times, applying verses about building Jerusalem to David's era rather than the return from Babylon. The commentary emphasizes the balance between God's cosmic power and personal tenderness.

## Key Practical Applications

**The Nature of Praise**: Henry emphasizes that praise is both duty and delight, stating "it is good, for it is pleasant" and "praising God is work that is its own wages; it is heaven upon earth." He stresses that proper praise requires "holy joy" as its principle.

**Divine Priorities**: A central application concerns what pleases God. Henry notes that God "delighteth not in the strength of the horse" nor "taketh pleasure in the legs of a man," but rather "takes pleasure in those that fear him, in those that hope in his mercy." This teaches that God values spiritual qualities over military might or human strength.

**Balance of Fear and Hope**: Henry provides practical guidance on Christian living: "Our fear must save our hope from swelling into presumption, and our hope must save our fear from sinking into despair."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 2-3**: Henry beautifully connects God's building of Jerusalem with the gospel church, describing how God "gathers the outcasts" both through repentance and by opening doors for return from persecution.

**Verses 4-5**: On God naming the stars, Henry writes that this "shows his dominion over them and the command he has them at," while noting that "God's knowledge is a depth that can never be fathomed."

**Verses 8-9**: Henry draws a striking parallel between clouds and afflictions: "Clouds look melancholy, and yet without them we could have no rain...Thus afflictions, for the present, look black, and dark, and unpleasant...but they are necessary."

## Cross-References and Biblical Connections

Henry references **Psalm 40:2-3** regarding God putting "a new song" in the mouths of those delivered from the "horrible pit." He connects to **Job 39:19** concerning the war-horse and **Job 40:12** about God abasing the proud.

He references **Hebrews 12:11** regarding afflictions producing "peaceable fruits of righteousness," and **Psalm 20:7** about trusting in horses versus trusting God. The commentary also alludes to **Zechariah 2:5** about God as a "wall of fire" for protection.

Henry concludes by noting God's unique revelation to Israel: "He hath not dealt so with any nation," emphasizing Israel's privileged position in receiving God's statutes and judgments while other nations remained ignorant of them.

Chapter 148
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 148: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 148 as "a most solemn and earnest call to all the creatures, according to their capacity, to praise their Creator." He structures this as a comprehensive praise symphony with two main divisions: the "higher house" (celestial beings and heavenly bodies, verses 1-6) and the "lower house" (earthly creatures and humanity, verses 7-14).

The central theological theme emphasizes God's absolute sovereignty over creation, with Henry noting that "his name alone is excellent and worthy to be praised; it is a name above every name." He highlights God's eternal power demonstrated through both creation ("He commanded, and they were created") and preservation ("He hath established them for ever and ever").

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical lessons for believers:

**Spiritual Communion with Angels**: When Christians sing this psalm calling upon angels to praise God, "we have a spiritual communion with those that dwell in his house above and are still praising him." This connects earthly worship with heavenly worship.

**Reconciliation to Our Earthly Place**: Henry addresses the challenge of living in "this earth, and the atmosphere that surrounds it, [which] are the very sediment of the universe." He finds comfort that "even in this world, dark and as bad as it is, God is praised."

**Universal Participation in Praise**: All social ranks, ages, and conditions must praise God. "Old men must still bring forth this fruit in old age," while "children too must begin betimes to praise God; even out of the mouth of babes and sucklings this good work is perfected."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2 (Angels)**: Henry acknowledges the mystery of calling upon angels who "do not need it, for they are continually praising God and there is no deficiency at all in their performances."

**Verses 3-6 (Celestial Bodies)**: He contrasts pagan worship with Christian perspective: "Idolaters made the sun, moon, and stars, their gods, and praised them...but we...make them our fellow-worshippers."

**Verses 7-10 (Natural Elements)**: Henry finds particular significance in stormy winds that "fulfil God's word," noting that rebellious humans "show themselves to be more violent and headstrong than even the stormy winds."

**Verse 14 (God's People)**: He emphasizes Israel's special relationship as "a people near unto him," suggesting particular obligations and privileges in praise.

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry weaves numerous biblical connections throughout:

- **Ephesians 3:10** - regarding angels learning God's wisdom through the church
- **Psalm 103:20** - parallel call for angelic praise
- **Hebrews 12:22** - believers joining "the innumerable company of angels"
- **Job 38:23** - waters reserved for God's purposes
- **1 Kings 8:27** - the heavens cannot contain God
- **Psalm 104:16, 26** - God's provision in creation

Henry's commentary reveals Psalm 148 as both a theological statement about God's sovereignty over all creation and a practical guide for comprehensive worship that transcends earthly limitations through spiritual communion with the heavenly realm.

Chapter 149
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 149: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Henry interprets Psalm 149 as "a hymn of praise to the Redeemer" that looks beyond immediate historical victories to "the kingdom of the Messiah, who, in the chariot of the everlasting gospel, goes forth conquering and to conquer." The psalm proclaims dual themes: abundant joy for God's people (verses 1-5) and terror for their enemies (verses 6-9).

The commentary emphasizes the Trinity, noting that "Israel rejoice in his Makers" (plural) suggests "the mystery of the Trinity." Henry also stresses God's sovereignty—since He made His people, He rightfully rules as their King, creating both obligation and joy in worship.

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living:

**Corporate and Private Worship**: Believers must praise God both "in the solemn assembly" and privately, even "sing aloud upon their beds" during sleepless nights or illness. He emphasizes that public worship should be conducted with enthusiasm—"we should sing psalms with all our heart."

**Spiritual Warfare**: The "two-edged sword" represents God's Word, which believers must actively wield. Henry applies this to three levels: historical victories over literal enemies, present battles against personal sin ("believers fight against their own corruptions"), and the future final judgment.

**Balanced Approach to Conflict**: Henry warns against misusing this psalm to justify violence, stating that "Christ never intended that his gospel should be propagated by fire and sword." Instead, "when the high praises of God are in our mouth with them we should have an olive-branch of peace in our hands."

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-3**: The call for "new song" means both newly composed pieces and "new affections, which make the song new, though the words have been used before." Henry addresses instrumental music and dancing, noting they were Old Testament practices not explicitly continued in New Testament worship.

**Verse 4**: God's pleasure in His people demonstrates divine condescension—"He that is infinitely happy...yet graciously condescends to take pleasure in his people."

**Verse 5**: "Beautify the meek with salvation" refers both to temporal deliverances and eternal salvation, when "the righteous shall be beautified...shine forth as the sun."

**Verses 6-9**: The "judgment written" justifies action only when done "by commission from God, according to his direction, and in obedience to his command," not from "personal malice and revenge."

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry extensively connects this psalm to broader biblical themes:

- **Hebrews 12:22** - Christians as "children of Zion" who have "come unto Mount Zion"
- **Zechariah 9:9** - The coming Messiah King
- **Hebrews 4:12 and Ephesians 6:17** - God's Word as the "two-edged sword"
- **1 Corinthians 6:2** - Saints judging the world
- **Jude 14-15** - Final judgment with the saints

The commentary ultimately presents this psalm as both historical celebration and prophetic vision, calling believers to joyful worship while engaging in spiritual warfare through God's Word rather than physical violence.

Chapter 150
----------------------------------------
# Matthew Henry's Commentary on Psalm 150: Summary

## Main Theological Themes and Interpretations

Matthew Henry presents Psalm 150 as the climactic conclusion to the entire Psalter, designed to show that the ultimate purpose of all sacred songs is "to assist us in praising God." He emphasizes the deliberate structure, noting that while Psalm 1 provides "elaborate instruction in our duty," Psalm 150 is "all rapture and transport." The psalm represents a call to universal praise, with "Praise the Lord" appearing thirteen times in six verses, demonstrating the psalmist's desire to "fill all the world" with God's praises.

Henry suggests this psalm may represent "the work of glorified saints in heaven," where praise is continuous without weariness—"hallelujahs for ever repeated, and yet still new songs." He notes the progression from prayers to pure praise, stating that "prayers will there be swallowed up in everlasting praises."

## Key Practical Applications

Henry draws several practical applications for Christian living. He emphasizes that "the best music in God's ears is devout and pious affections," explaining that true praise involves "a strong faith," "holy love and delight," "entire confidence in Christ," and "cheerful submission to all his disposals." 

He stresses that in serving God "we should spare no cost nor pains" and calls for perfect harmony among believers, noting that "various instruments being used in praising God, it should yet be done with an exact and perfect harmony."

Henry encourages Christians to "spend as much of our time as may be in this good work because in it we hope to spend a joyful eternity," urging believers to anticipate their eternal destiny by practicing praise now.

## Notable Verse-by-Verse Insights

**Verses 1-2**: Henry interprets "his sanctuary" both as the earthly temple and "the highest heavens, the residence of his glory." He notes comfort in knowing that while our earthly praise is imperfect, "it is so well done there" in heaven.

**Verses 3-5**: Rather than focusing on specific instruments, Henry emphasizes the spiritual significance, stating "non musica chordula, sed cor—not a melodious string, but a melodious heart."

**Verse 6**: Henry interprets "every thing that hath breath" as potentially including all creatures, noting that "the singing of birds is a sort of praising God" and that "brutes do in effect say to man, 'We would praise God if we could; do you do it for us.'"

## Cross-References to Other Scripture

Henry connects this psalm to several biblical passages: Ezekiel 37:26 regarding sanctuary privileges, Malachi 1:11 about Gentile inclusion in worship, Romans 15:6 concerning unified praise "with one mind and one mouth," Genesis 7:22 and 2:7 regarding breathing creatures, Revelation 5:13 about universal creation praise, and Revelation 19:1,3 emphasizing "Hallelujah" as heaven's perpetual song.

He concludes by noting the structural progression of the Psalter's endings, with the final book concluding simply with "Hallelujah" because "the last six psalms are wholly taken up in praising God."
